Breakfast Pancakes

The morning stack, from buttermilk cakes to dosa and plättar.

Breakfast is a use, not a country. A Southern diner stack, a Mangalore neer dosa, and a Swedish plättar can all be the first thing on the table.

Print this booklet when you want the morning recipes in one sheaf. The journal page for each cake has the longer history.

Canada · No chili heat · 10 ployes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Canada · North America

Acadian ployes

Mild buckwheat, a little sour from the grain, with a holey top and a soft underside.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 ployes

History

Acadian ployes are buckwheat pancakes of the Madawaska region, on the New Brunswick side of the Saint John River and in nearby Maine. The batter is usually cooked on one side. Buckwheat suited the cool seasons of that border country. The map pin is in Canada, and the same cakes are made across the river in the United States.

Cultural background

A ploye batter is thin, often buckwheat plus some wheat flour, water, and baking powder. Many recipes have no egg and no milk. This one follows that pattern. It is not gluten-free, because of the wheat flour.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat tastes earthy, and cooking on one side leaves the top soft and full of holes while the bottom browns. Water keeps the flavor plain. Butter or maple at the table is how many people eat them, and those additions are dairy or sweetness on top of a batter that has neither.

How it is made

Thin buckwheat batter cooked on one side on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook them on one side only, until the top is dry and bubbled. Flipping them makes a different pancake. Serve them as a bread, not only as a dessert.

How it is used

Hot, often with butter, maple syrup, or baked beans. Cretons, a pork spread, is a local accompaniment for people who eat pork.

Field notes

  • Ployes belong to the Madawaska region of New Brunswick and to nearby Maine.
  • They are traditionally cooked on one side, until the top dries and forms holes.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup buckwheat flour
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups cold water
  • 1 teaspoon neutral oil, for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours, the baking powder, and the salt.
  2. Whisk in the cold water until the batter is smooth and thin, like cream. Add a spoon of water if it is stiff.
  3. Heat a griddle over medium-high heat and wipe it with a thin film of oil.
  4. Pour about 1/4 cup batter. It should spread.
  5. Cook on one side until the top is dry, the surface is full of holes, and the underside is brown. Do not flip.
  6. Stack them and serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some families use a higher share of buckwheat. Unless that flour is certified gluten-free, do not call the cakes gluten-free.
  • An egg in the batter is a later enrichment and removes the vegan label.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Maple syrup
  • Baked beans
  • Butter, if you eat dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Medium · 8 adai

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Adai

A sturdy mixed-lentil pancake, peppery and hot from dried chile, with curry leaf in the batter.

30 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 adai

History

Adai is a mixed-lentil pancake of Tamil Nadu and the wider South. Toor, chana, urad, and moong are soaked with rice, then ground with dried red chile and curry leaf. Cooks often grind and cook it the same day. A long dosa-style ferment is optional, not the definition. The result is thicker than a hotel dosa and hot enough to stand with a knob of jaggery or a spoon of avial on the side.

Cultural background

Madurai and other Tamil kitchens keep adai as a meal when a fermented batter was not planned. The several dals make it more filling than a thin rice crepe. Dried chile is the usual heat, so this version is medium.

Why it tastes this way

Each dal brings a different note: toor is earthy, chana is sweet, urad is creamy, moong is grassy. Rice keeps the cake from turning dense. Dried red chile and black pepper supply heat that green chile alone would not. Curry leaf, ground in rather than fried on top, perfumes the crumb all the way through.

How it is made

Soak a mix of lentils and rice, grind them coarsely with dried chile, and cook thick savory rounds.

Authentic touch. Leave the grind a little coarse. A silky batter is dosa territory. Adai should show flecks of dal and chile.

How it is used

Serve hot with jaggery, coconut chutney, or avial. The sweet jaggery against the chile is a Tamil habit, not a dessert course.

Field notes

  • Adai uses several lentils at once. It is not a single-dal cheela.
  • Same-day grinding is traditional. An overnight ferment is a choice, not a requirement.

Ingredients

  • 1/4 cup toor dal
  • 1/4 cup chana dal
  • 2 tablespoons skinned urad dal
  • 2 tablespoons moong dal
  • 1/3 cup raw rice
  • 3 dried red chiles
  • 10 curry leaves
  • 1/2 teaspoon black pepper
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • Water and neutral oil

Steps

  1. Rinse the dals and rice together and soak them with the dried chiles for 3 to 4 hours.
  2. Drain and grind coarsely with curry leaves, pepper, salt, and a little water. The batter should be thick and speckled, not a smooth cream.
  3. Cook it promptly, or rest it for 30 minutes. No long ferment is required.
  4. Oil a hot griddle. Spread a ladle into a small, thick round. Make a tiny hole in the center if you like, a common adai habit that helps the middle cook.
  5. Drizzle oil, cook until the underside is deep gold, flip, and cook the other side. Serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A fistful of chopped onion folded into the batter
  • Less dried chile for a milder cake

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Jaggery
  • Coconut chutney
  • Avial
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Denmark · No chili heat · about 28 aebleskiver

Flying Pancakes of the World

Denmark · Northern Europe

Aebleskiver

Round Danish pancakes from a molded pan, cardamom, jam, and powdered sugar.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · about 28 aebleskiver

History

Aebleskiver are round pancakes cooked in a special pan with hemispherical cups. The name means apple slices. Older versions tuck in a bit of apple. Many Danish kitchens now cook them plain, with cardamom, and serve jam and powdered sugar. They are especially common in December, and they are also eaten at other times. Church breakfasts in the American Upper Midwest are a later home of the pan, not the origin of the cake. This recipe is the Danish one.

Cultural background

The pan is the recipe's tool, as with Dutch poffertjes, and the batters are not the same. Aebleskiver are turned with a knitting needle or a skewer so a ball forms. They are Advent and Christmas food in many families now.

Why it tastes this way

Whipped egg whites and baking powder make them light. Cardamom perfumes the crumb. The crust browns in butter while the inside stays like a small popover. Powdered sugar and a tart jam are the contrast. Apple, if you add it, steams inside and tastes like the name.

How it is made

A batter of flour, buttermilk, cardamom, and separated eggs is cooked in a buttered aebleskiver pan, turned once into rounds, and served with jam and powdered sugar.

Authentic touch. Turn each cake before the center is dry, so the unset batter runs into the cup and makes a ball. A flat pan cannot do this. A piece of apple in the cup is the older filling. Leaving it out is the common modern cake.

How it is used

Hot, with jam and powdered sugar, at breakfast, as a snack, or through December. The American church breakfast is a later table, not the source.

Field notes

  • The name means apple slices, and many current recipes leave the apple out.
  • Upper Midwest church breakfasts are a later home, not the origin.
  • The ball shape comes from turning the cake in a molded pan.

Ingredients

  • 180 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • 1/2 tsp ground cardamom
  • 2 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 2 large eggs, separated
  • 250 ml buttermilk
  • 40 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the pan
  • Jam and powdered sugar, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, cardamom, sugar, and salt. Stir in the yolks, buttermilk, and melted butter.
  2. Beat the whites to soft peaks and fold them in.
  3. Heat an aebleskiver pan, butter the cups, and fill them almost full. Cook until the bottoms are brown.
  4. Turn each cake with a skewer and cook until the ball is set and browned. Serve with jam and powdered sugar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A cube of apple in each cup
  • A yeasted batter, which is an older style than baking powder

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Feast or fast day
  • Snack
  • Jam
  • Powdered sugar
  • A small piece of apple hidden in the batter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Turkey · No chili heat · About 8 thin pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Turkey · Middle East

Aegean akıtma

Thin Aegean semolina pancakes with a tangy crumb, finished with walnuts and syrup.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · About 8 thin pancakes

History

Akıtma is a semolina pancake from the Aegean coast of Turkey. The name sits next to the verb for pouring, and the batter is meant to be poured thin. Around Ayvalık, Izmir, and the nearby towns it is breakfast or a sweet snack, often with crushed walnuts or a light syrup. Semolina is wheat. Walnuts make this version a tree-nut dish. It is not gözleme, which is a stuffed flatbread, and it is not a layered katmer.

Cultural background

Aegean kitchens keep semolina, yogurt, and olive oil close at hand. Akıtma uses that pantry. Some houses eat it with cheese instead of walnuts. This page chooses the walnut and syrup finish and says so, because the allergen is different.

Why it tastes this way

Semolina gives a fine grit and a mild sweetness. Yogurt makes the batter tangy, and yeast lifts it without a long sour ferment. The pancake is thin, so the griddle browns a larger share of the crumb. Walnuts add bitterness and fat, and the syrup makes those pieces glossy. The Aegean habit is this contrast, not a stack of thick cakes.

How it is made

Whisk fine semolina, a little flour, yeast, yogurt, water, and salt into a pourable batter. Rest it until foamy. Cook thin rounds on an oiled griddle. Top with crushed walnuts and a warm sugar syrup.

Authentic touch. Pour the batter thin, in the Aegean way, and finish with walnuts. If you leave the nuts off, say so, because this written recipe is not nut-free. Cheese instead of walnuts is a real alternative and drops the tree-nut allergen only when the nuts are actually absent.

How it is used

Akıtma is an Aegean breakfast or a sweet snack. Tea goes with it. It can be dessert when the syrup is generous. It is not a street stuffed bread.

Field notes

  • Akıtma is an Aegean Turkish semolina pancake, poured thin.
  • This version includes walnuts, so it is not nut-free.
  • The place on the map is Ayvalık, on the Aegean coast.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup fine semolina (about 160 g)
  • 1/2 cup wheat flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 cup yogurt
  • 1 cup warm water
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1/2 cup chopped walnuts
  • 1/2 cup sugar
  • 1/4 cup water, for the syrup
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir the semolina, flour, yeast, yogurt, warm water, and salt into a thin batter. Cover it for 30 to 40 minutes, until foamy.
  2. Simmer the sugar and the quarter-cup of water for 5 minutes to make a light syrup. Keep it warm.
  3. Oil a griddle over medium heat and pour a thin round. This is a pourable Aegean batter, not a stuffed flatbread.
  4. Cook until the top sets and the underside spots, then flip once and cook briefly.
  5. Top each pancake with walnuts and a spoon of syrup. The walnuts are a tree nut.

Semolina and flour are wheat. Yogurt is dairy. Walnuts are a tree nut. This plate is not nut-free.

Eat akıtma the day it is cooked. Refrigerate plain pancakes separately from the syrup and nuts, and finish them when you reheat them. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Cheese instead of walnuts removes the tree nut and keeps the dairy.
  • More yogurt makes a tangier batter.
  • A savory herb finish is a home choice and not this sweet plate.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Snack
  • Black tea
  • Extra crushed walnuts
  • A salty white cheese, if you want a savory contrast
  • Vegetarian

United States · No chili heat · 12 flapjacks

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Alaska sourdough flapjacks

Mildly sour wheat cakes, a little richer from evaporated milk, with a browned edge.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 flapjacks

History

Sourdough flapjacks are the breakfast cooks made from a living starter in Alaska, where gold-rush camps kept a starter going because fresh yeast was hard to replace. The word sourdough became a nickname for an old hand in that country. The cakes are wheat pancakes leavened by the starter and finished with a little soda. Skagway, a Klondike gateway, is the map pin.

Cultural background

In this journal, flapjack means the American pancake, not the British oat bar. Evaporated milk was a practical canned dairy in camps and is used here. The starter has to be active before you start, so feeding it is not counted in the prep time.

Why it tastes this way

A fed starter brings a light sourness and some of the rise. Baking soda reacts with that acid and gives the cakes a quicker lift on the griddle. Evaporated milk tastes slightly cooked and rich. If the starter is sleepy, the cakes taste flat and do not brown as well.

How it is made

Sourdough batter finished with baking soda and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Use a bubbly wheat starter you have already fed. Stir the soda in just before cooking so the batter does not collapse on the counter.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and syrup.

Field notes

  • Gold-rush cooks in Alaska and the Yukon kept sourdough starters because fresh yeast was scarce.
  • Sourdough also became a nickname for someone who had lasted in that country.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup active sourdough starter, fed ahead of time
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/2 cup evaporated milk
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for the griddle
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda

Steps

  1. Stir the starter, flour, sugar, salt, egg, and evaporated milk until smooth. The starter should already be bubbly from a feeding done before this recipe.
  2. Rest the bowl 15 minutes.
  3. Stir in the oil and baking soda.
  4. Oil a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the surface is full of holes and the underside is brown. Cook until the center is set.
  6. Serve hot with butter and syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A water-based batter without evaporated milk is leaner and less dairy-rich, but then the recipe changes.
  • Fresh milk can replace evaporated milk if that is what you have. Use 1/2 cup.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup or berry jam
  • Coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Pakistan · Mild · 6 stuffed breads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Pakistan · South Asia

Aloo paratha

A wheat flatbread stuffed with spiced potato, griddled in ghee until the surface is spotted and flaky.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 stuffed breads

History

Aloo paratha is a stuffed griddled bread of Punjabi kitchens in Pakistan and north India. This page places it in Lahore and lists India as well. Mashed potato, green chile, cilantro, and spices fill a wheat dough that is rolled and cooked on a tawa with ghee. It is a stuffed bread, not a batter pancake. Breakfast stalls and homes both cook it. The potato is the filling that made this paratha the everyday one.

Cultural background

The dough is atta, the wheat flour used for chapati. The filling must be dry and cool or it tears the dough. Lahore breakfasts often set the paratha next to yogurt, pickle, or a fried egg. The egg would be an extra allergen.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat toasted in ghee tastes nutty and rich. Potato takes on cumin, chile, and salt and stays soft inside the flake. A little ajwain or cumin keeps the filling from tasting bland. Because the bread is rolled, you get layers of dough around one pocket of spice, which a poured batter cannot do.

How it is made

Stuff a wheat dough with seasoned mashed potato, roll it, and griddle it in ghee until both sides spot.

Authentic touch. Seal the edges before you roll. A leak of potato on the tawa burns and sticks. Roll from the center outward with a light hand.

How it is used

Serve hot, torn with the hands, with pickle or yogurt. It is a breakfast and a travel food.

Field notes

  • Aloo paratha is a stuffed wheat bread cooked on a griddle.
  • Pakistan and north India both claim it as everyday breakfast food.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups whole wheat flour (atta)
  • 3/4 cup water, plus more if needed
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt for the dough
  • 3 medium potatoes, boiled and mashed
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt for the filling
  • Ghee for cooking

Steps

  1. Mix the flour and 1/2 teaspoon salt with water into a soft dough. Rest it 15 minutes.
  2. Mix the mashed potato with chile, cilantro, cumin, and salt. The filling should be cool and dry, not wet.
  3. Divide the dough into six balls. Flatten one, add a spoon of potato, pinch it closed, and roll gently into a thick round.
  4. Cook on a hot tawa, spreading a little ghee, until both sides are browned in spots and the dough is cooked through.
  5. Repeat with the rest. Serve immediately.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped fresh fenugreek in the potato
  • Oil instead of ghee, which removes the dairy if the dough has none

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Mango pickle
  • Plain yogurt
  • A salad of onion and lime
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 6 ambolis

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Amboli

A soft, spongy, lightly sour rice pancake from the Konkan coast.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 ambolis

History

Amboli is a fermented rice pancake of the Konkan, especially Malvani kitchens in Maharashtra and the coast down toward Goa. Rice is soaked and ground, then left to sour. Some cooks add a little urad. This recipe uses a spoon of flattened rice for softness and keeps the batter wheat-free. It is served with coconut curries and chutneys, a monsoon and breakfast food of the coast rather than a crisp hotel dosa.

Cultural background

Ratnagiri and Malvan cook amboli as a sponge that soaks up a spicy side. The pancake on this page is not itself a hot dish. The heat lives in the fish or vegetable curry beside it. A fish curry would change the allergens, so the pairing listed here is a coconut chutney.

Why it tastes this way

A long rice ferment in humid coastal air turns sweet soaked rice into something mildly sour and bready. The cake is thick, so the sourness stays soft instead of toasting away. Coconut chutney adds fat and sweetness the batter does not have. The coast's humidity is not a myth here: it is why an overnight bowl sours reliably without a starter.

How it is made

Grind soaked rice, ferment it overnight, and cook thick covered rounds on a moderate griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook it covered and do not chase a deep brown crust. Amboli is done when the top is set and the crumb looks spongy, like a pancake that never tried to be a wafer.

How it is used

Serve in a stack with coconut chutney. On the Konkan it also meets spicy curries. Those are separate dishes.

Field notes

  • Amboli is a Konkan fermented rice cake, soft on purpose.
  • This batter has no wheat, so the pancake is gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 2 tablespoons flattened rice (poha)
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse the rice and soak it with the flattened rice for 4 hours.
  2. Drain and grind to a smooth batter a little thicker than crepe batter. Stir in the salt.
  3. Ferment covered overnight, until bubbly and mildly sour. Do not count those hours as active prep. Discard the bowl if it spoils.
  4. Oil a pan over medium-low. Pour a thick round and cover it.
  5. Cook until the top is set and spongy and the underside is pale gold. Flip only if the center needs it. Repeat.

Discard batter that smells rotten or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A tablespoon of soaked urad ground with the rice
  • A pinch of sugar if the ferment went very sour

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • A dry potato bhaji
  • Sweetened coconut milk
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Appalachian buckwheat cakes

Earthly and slightly sour, with a gray-brown crumb and a sorghum finish if you use it at the table.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 cakes

History

Appalachian buckwheat cakes use flour milled from buckwheat, a crop that grows on cooler mountain ground, usually mixed with wheat flour so the cake holds together. Scots-Irish and English settlement lines up with this griddle, and German-speaking neighbors lived in the same hills. Enslaved people and later Black Appalachian cooks worked many of the same farms. The cakes are a tangy breakfast, often finished with sorghum rather than maple.

Cultural background

Buckwheat is not a kind of wheat. This recipe still uses all-purpose flour, so the cakes are not gluten-free. Elkins stands in for a griddle that was widespread in the uplands.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat tastes stronger and earthier than white flour, and buttermilk sharpens it. Soda reacts with the buttermilk and lifts a batter that would otherwise be heavy. Sorghum at the table adds a mineral sweetness that white sugar does not.

How it is made

Griddled buttermilk and buckwheat batter.

Authentic touch. Keep at least half the flour as buckwheat so the cakes taste like the grain. Serve them with sorghum if you can get it.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and sorghum syrup.

Field notes

  • Buckwheat fell out of mountain fields as wheat flour became cheap, and the cakes got rarer with it.
  • Sorghum, not maple, was the everyday sweetener with these cakes in much of the upland South.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup buckwheat flour
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours, the baking soda, and the salt.
  2. Whisk in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter until just combined. The batter will be dark and a bit thick.
  3. Heat a buttered griddle over medium heat.
  4. Pour 1/4 cup rounds. Cook until the top is set and the underside is brown, then flip.
  5. The second side browns faster. Move the cakes off when the center springs back.
  6. Serve with butter and sorghum syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A yeasted overnight batter is the older leavening and belongs to a longer method.
  • All-buckwheat cakes are more fragile and are not automatically gluten-free unless the flour is certified.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Sorghum syrup
  • Butter
  • Sausage, if you eat meat
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Appalachian dried-apple pancakes

Soft wheat cakes with chewy, tart pieces of dried apple and a hint of cinnamon.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Appalachian dried-apple pancakes use apples that were dried for winter, a common mountain way to keep fruit after the fresh crop was gone. The slices are soaked and then folded into a wheat batter. The cake is a cold-weather breakfast in the upland South, not a fresh-apple diner special. Boone, in the North Carolina mountains, is the map pin.

Cultural background

Drying rings of apple on strings or racks was ordinary preservation before reliable refrigeration. The pancake is one way those apples came back to the table.

Why it tastes this way

Dried apples keep a sharper, chewier taste than fresh slices, which turn soft and watery in a batter. A short soak brings back enough moisture that they are not leathery. Cinnamon is restrained so the fruit still tastes like apple.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter with rehydrated dried apples folded in.

Authentic touch. Start with dried apples and soak them. Fresh apples are a different cake, filed with the cider pancakes.

How it is used

Hot, with butter. Sorghum or a little sugar is enough sweetness.

Field notes

  • Dried apples were a winter pantry staple in the mountains, used when fresh fruit was gone.
  • Soaking them first keeps the dried edges from staying leathery inside the cake.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup dried apple slices
  • 1 cup boiling water
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Put the dried apples in a bowl, cover them with the boiling water, and soak 15 minutes. Drain and chop them. The soaking liquid can be discarded.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, cinnamon, and salt.
  3. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter until just combined. Fold in the apples.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Cook a little longer than plain pancakes, until the apple pieces are hot and the center is set. Flip once.
  6. Serve with butter and, if you want it, sorghum.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave out the cinnamon if the apples are strongly flavored.
  • A handful of the soaked apples, chopped finer, disappears into the crumb.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Sorghum syrup
  • Hot coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Appalachian sorghum pancakes

Malty and lightly bitter-sweet from sorghum, with a plain wheat crumb underneath.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Appalachian sorghum pancakes are wheat cakes sweetened with sorghum syrup, the juice of sorghum cane pressed and boiled at local mills. Sorghum, more than maple, was the everyday sweetener in much of the upland South. The syrup goes into the batter and onto the finished cakes. Whitesburg is a map pin in eastern Kentucky, where sorghum is still pressed in season.

Cultural background

Sorghum mills were neighborhood work in the fall. The syrup is not molasses made from sugarcane, and it is not maple. Those three sweeteners show up in different cakes in this journal.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum tastes grassy and deep, with a slight bitterness that keeps the cake from tasting like candy. Baking soda balances some of that acid edge and helps the batter rise. Butter rounds the bitterness at the table.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter sweetened with sorghum.

Authentic touch. Put sorghum in the batter and serve more on the side. Maple syrup turns this into a different breakfast.

How it is used

Stacked, with butter and a second pour of sorghum.

Field notes

  • Sorghum syrup comes from sorghum cane crushed at a mill, not from maple trees.
  • In much of Appalachia it was the sweetener people made nearby, while white sugar was bought.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 3 tablespoons sorghum syrup, plus more to serve
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  2. Whisk the buttermilk, sorghum syrup, egg, and melted butter in a second bowl.
  3. Stir the wet ingredients into the dry ingredients and stop as soon as the flour is moist.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the bubbles set. Cook until the center is done.
  6. Serve with butter and more sorghum syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Use the syrup only at the table if you want a plainer crumb.
  • Buckwheat flour in place of half the wheat flour moves the cake toward the buckwheat page.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Sorghum syrup
  • Butter
  • Black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 8 appams

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Appam

A bowl-shaped rice hopper with a lacy crisp rim, a spongy sweet center, and coconut in the batter.

35 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 appams

History

Appam is Kerala's fermented rice hopper, cooked in a curved appam pan so the center stays thick and the sides climb into a lace. Coconut milk is a usual part of the batter. Where toddy is still used, it is the ferment. This home recipe uses a pinch of yeast as the reliable stand-in and says so. Sri Lankan hoppers are a close cousin with their own page. Nei appam, the sweet ghee cake, is not this dish.

Cultural background

Kerala breakfasts pair appam with stew: vegetable, egg, or meat. The hopper itself is rice and coconut. A yeast ferment will not taste identical to a toddy ferment, and the page does not pretend it will. The bowl shape is non-negotiable.

Why it tastes this way

Rice and coconut milk make the center sweet and tender. Fermentation, whether from toddy or from yeast, adds a light sourness that keeps the coconut from tasting flat. The thin batter that climbs the hot wall of the pan dries into lace and picks up a toasted edge. Salt is the only savory note in the cake. The stew around it does the rest.

How it is made

Ferment a rice and coconut-milk batter and swirl it in a covered curved pan so it finishes bowl-shaped.

Authentic touch. Swirl the pan as soon as you pour, so a puddle remains in the center and only a film coats the sides. Then cover the pan. An open flat griddle cannot make an appam.

How it is used

Serve the bowls at once, center side up, with a mild vegetable stew. Meat stew is a common pairing and would add a meat allergen.

Field notes

  • The bowl shape comes from a curved appam pan and a swirl, not from a mold pressed into flat batter.
  • This savory hopper is a different cake from sweet nei appam.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 1/2 cup cooked rice
  • 1/2 cup coconut milk, plus more if needed
  • 1/4 teaspoon dry yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water as needed
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Soak the raw rice for 3 hours. Drain and grind it with the cooked rice and a little water to a smooth batter.
  2. Stir in coconut milk, yeast, sugar, and salt. The batter should be thinner than dosa batter and thicker than milk.
  3. Cover and ferment until bubbly and lightly sour, often 6 to 10 hours in a warm kitchen. Active prep excludes that wait.
  4. Heat a curved appam pan, grease it, and pour a ladle of batter. Swirl so the sides take a thin coat and the center stays deep.
  5. Cover and cook on medium-low until the rim is lacy and the center is set and spongy. Ease it out and repeat.

Discard batter that smells rotten or grows mold. Cook the center until it is set, not glossy and raw.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Toddy in place of yeast where you can get it and trust it
  • A spoon of sugar for a sweeter palappam

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Mild vegetable stew
  • Sweetened coconut milk
  • A ripe banana
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 10 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Apple cider pancakes

Reduced sweet cider and soft apple pieces in a lightly spiced wheat cake.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 pancakes

History

Apple cider pancakes are a fall breakfast in New York and New England cider country. Sweet cider is reduced so its flavor is strong enough to taste in a wheat batter, and a little diced apple goes in too. The acid in the cider also helps the soda lift the cakes. Hudson, New York, is the map pin.

Cultural background

Use sweet cider, sometimes sold as fresh apple cider, not bottled vinegar. Reducing it on the stove is what concentrates the flavor. The diced apple is the texture.

Why it tastes this way

Unreduced cider tastes thin once it is mixed with flour. Boiling it down leaves a sharper apple syrup. The diced apple softens on the griddle and stays in pieces. Cinnamon is a background, not the whole flavor.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter made with reduced sweet cider and diced apple.

Authentic touch. Reduce the cider before it goes into the bowl. Pouring cold cider straight into a pancake mix will not taste like this.

How it is used

Hot, with butter. More cider, warmed, can be poured on lightly.

Field notes

  • Reducing sweet cider is what makes the apple flavor strong enough to taste in batter.
  • The acid in cider reacts with baking soda and helps the cakes rise.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup sweet apple cider
  • 1/2 cup small-diced peeled apple
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 cup whole milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Boil the cider in a small saucepan until it reduces to 1/2 cup. Cool it.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, cinnamon, and salt.
  3. Stir in the reduced cider, milk, egg, and melted butter. Fold in the diced apple.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the underside is brown and the top is set. Cook until the apple pieces are tender.
  6. Serve with butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave the diced apple out if you only want the cider in the crumb.
  • A pinch more cinnamon is plenty. Nutmeg goes bitter quickly.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Warm reduced cider
  • Maple syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Colombia · No chili heat · 6 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa boyacense

A lightly sweet highland cake of corn and wheat, studded with cuajada and cooked on a griddle.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 arepas

History

The arepa boyacense is a highland cake from Boyacá, built around cuajada, the fresh curd of the region. It is often a mix of precooked corn and wheat flour, enriched with egg, butter, and sugar, then griddled or baked. That makes it a different dough from a Medellín white-corn arepa and from a coastal fried arepa de huevo. Wheat in the dough means it is not gluten-free. Corn is still the main grain, and corn cakes in the region are older than wheat hotcakes, even when a later recipe folds wheat flour into the ball.

Cultural background

In Tunja and the towns around it, these arepas are breakfast and a traveler's snack, slightly sweet, with curd cheese you can see. They are thicker and richer than the thin arepa that comes with a bandeja. This recipe cooks them on a griddle.

Why it tastes this way

Sugar and butter push the cake toward a mild sweetness, while cuajada stays salty and cools that sweetness down. Wheat flour softens the crumb compared with a cake of only precooked corn. Egg helps it brown. You taste corn first, then butter, then the milky curd. There is no hogao on this plate and no fried egg inside.

How it is made

Mix precooked cornmeal, wheat flour, sugar, egg, melted butter, milk, and salt. Pat rounds, press in cuajada, and griddle until set.

Authentic touch. Press cubes of cuajada into the rounds so they show on the surface, instead of shredding the cheese until it vanishes. Cook on moderate heat. The wheat and egg brown fast, and the center still needs time.

How it is used

Serve them warm at breakfast or with hot chocolate. They are a snack cake, not a stuffed sandwich and not a fried coastal arepa.

Field notes

  • Cuajada, fresh curd, is the cheese people expect in an arepa boyacense.
  • Wheat flour in this dough means the cake is not gluten-free.
  • It is a highland griddled cake, not the fried egg arepa of the Caribbean coast.

Ingredients

  • 120 g precooked cornmeal
  • 60 g wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 egg
  • 30 g melted butter, plus more for the griddle
  • 80 ml milk
  • 100 g cuajada, in small cubes

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal, wheat flour, sugar, and salt.
  2. Beat the egg, melted butter, and milk, and mix them in until a soft dough forms. Add a spoon of milk if it is too stiff to pat.
  3. Divide into 6 rounds. Press cubes of cuajada into the tops.
  4. Cook on a buttered griddle over medium-low heat until both sides are browned and the center is set.
  5. Serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some Boyacá cooks bake the rounds in a moderate oven until the cheese browns. The dough is the same.
  • Leave out the wheat flour and use only cornmeal if you need the cake free of gluten. The texture will be denser.
  • A savory version with less sugar exists. This one keeps the highland sweetness.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Hot chocolate
  • A second piece of cuajada
  • Small black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Colombia · No chili heat · 6 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa de choclo

A thick sweet-corn cake with pockets of fresh cheese, griddled until the edges brown.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 cakes

History

Choclo means fresh field corn. An arepa de choclo is a thick sweet-corn cake, often with cheese, and the batter is ground fresh kernels. It is not a dough of precooked dried cornmeal, and it is not nixtamalized masa. It belongs to Andean Colombian cooking, from Bogotá bakeries to home skillets when corn is in season. Corn cakes here are older than wheat hotcakes. This one simply keeps the corn in its fresh form. It is also not a Venezuelan cachapa, though both start from fresh kernels and both carry cheese.

Cultural background

Street and bakery versions in Bogotá are often larger and sweeter than a plain Antioquia arepa, with cheese pressed into the top or mixed through. People eat them at breakfast or in the middle of the afternoon. The fresh corn is the ingredient you cannot skip.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh corn is juicy and sweet, so the cake tastes like corn milk even before any sugar is added. A pinch of sugar only underlines what is already there. Cheese stays in soft salty pieces and keeps the cake from eating like dessert. Browning on the griddle adds a toasted edge. A spoon of fine untreated cornmeal, if the blend is watery, thickens it without turning it into masa.

How it is made

Pulse fresh corn with salt and a little sugar. Fold in crumbled white cheese and a spoon of fine cornmeal if the batter will not hold. Spoon thick cakes onto a buttered griddle and cook them through.

Authentic touch. Grind the kernels until thick and nubby, not into a juice. Fold in the cheese at the end. Cook over moderate heat so the center sets before the bottom scorches. If you only have precooked cornmeal, make a different arepa.

How it is used

Eat them warm, as breakfast or a snack, with more cheese or a pat of butter. They do not need a split or a stew.

Field notes

  • Choclo is fresh corn. This batter is not precooked arepa flour and not Mexican masa.
  • Cheese in the cake is what keeps an arepa de choclo from tasting only like a sweet pancake.
  • A smooth puree will run. Leave some of the kernel in pieces.

Ingredients

  • 3 cups fresh corn kernels
  • 100 g fresh white cheese, crumbled
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1 tablespoon fine cornmeal, not masa harina
  • Butter for the griddle

Steps

  1. Pulse the corn, salt, and sugar until thick and coarse. Stir in the cornmeal if the blend will not hold a shape.
  2. Fold in the crumbled cheese.
  3. Butter a griddle over medium heat.
  4. Spoon thick rounds and cook until the underside is browned and the top sets, then flip and cook through.
  5. Serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A Venezuelan cachapa is the other fresh-corn pancake in this journal, usually folded, and it is not called an arepa.
  • More sugar pushes the cake toward dessert. This one stays only lightly sweet.
  • Leave the cheese out and use oil instead of butter if you need a dairy-free cake. The corn still has to be fresh.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Street food
  • Hot chocolate, a Bogotá breakfast drink
  • Butter
  • A mild coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Colombia · No chili heat · 4 arepas and chicharrón

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa santandereana

A thick yellow-corn cake, griddled, served with crisp pork chicharrón.

20 min prep · 50 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 arepas and chicharrón

History

In Santander the arepa is often yellow corn, thicker than the white arepa of Antioquia, and it is eaten with chicharrón. The pork is part of this plate, so the recipe contains meat and is not vegetarian. Yellow corn dough here is cooked corn that has been ground, or yellow precooked cornmeal at home. It is not nixtamalized masa and not a fresh-corn choclo cake. The corn cake as a form is older than wheat hotcakes in the region. Serving it with fried pork belly is a Santander breakfast, not a stuffed coastal egg arepa.

Cultural background

Bucaramanga breakfasts pair the yellow arepa with chicharrón, sometimes with hogao or avocado. This recipe keeps the cake plain and puts the pork on the same plate, because that is how the dish is recognized. The arepa can be eaten alone, but then you are no longer cooking the plate this page describes.

Why it tastes this way

Yellow corn tastes deeper and a little sweeter than white precooked meal. A thick cake stays moist inside and toasts outside. Chicharrón is salt, rendered fat, and a crackling crust, which is why the mild corn can stand next to it without salsa. There is no chile in the written recipe. The richness is pork fat, not cheese.

How it is made

Form thick cakes from yellow precooked cornmeal and salt water and griddle them. Simmer pork belly, then fry it until crisp, and serve the two together.

Authentic touch. Simmer the pork belly until it is tender, dry it, then fry it in its own fat until the skin blisters and the meat is cooked through. Griddle the arepas separately so they do not sit in that fat. Season the dough only with salt.

How it is used

Serve one or two arepas with a piece of chicharrón for breakfast. Avocado on the side is common. The pork is still part of the plate.

Field notes

  • Santander breakfast arepas are yellow corn and are often eaten with chicharrón.
  • The pork in this recipe means the plate is not vegetarian.
  • Yellow precooked cornmeal stands in for cooked, ground yellow corn. It is not masa harina.

Ingredients

  • 250 g yellow precooked cornmeal
  • 340 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt, plus more for the pork
  • 400 g skin-on pork belly, in four pieces

Steps

  1. Cover the pork belly with water, salt it, and simmer until the meat is tender, about 30 minutes. Drain and pat dry.
  2. Stir the cornmeal, 1 teaspoon salt, and warm water into a soft dough. Rest 5 minutes. Form 4 thick cakes and smooth the cracks.
  3. Griddle the arepas over medium heat until both sides are crusty and the center is cooked, about 7 minutes a side.
  4. Fry the dried pork in a skillet over medium heat, turning, until the skin is crisp and the meat is cooked through. Pour off excess fat as it renders.
  5. Serve the arepas with the chicharrón.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the pork and the plate is a plain yellow arepa, which can be vegetarian. This written recipe includes the chicharrón.
  • A little cheese on the arepa adds dairy.
  • White precooked cornmeal changes the color Santander cooks expect from this cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Sliced avocado
  • Hogao, if you want a sauce
  • Small black coffee
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Argentina · No chili heat · 8 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Argentina · South America

Argentine panqueques

Thin milk crepes rolled around dulce de leche, with a browned-butter edge.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 crepes

History

Panqueques in Buenos Aires are thin wheat crepes, a dessert and a Sunday treat, filled most often with dulce de leche. The batter is flour, egg, and milk, the crepe family that spread through the Río de la Plata in the twentieth century along with other European kitchen habits. They are not corn cakes. Corn cakes elsewhere in South America are older than wheat hotcakes, and these are frankly a later wheat dessert. Dulce de leche, milk and sugar cooked until tan, is the filling people expect.

Cultural background

Home cooks in Buenos Aires make a stack of panqueques for dessert or for a late breakfast, then roll them while they are warm. Bakeries sell them already filled. The crepe is a vehicle. The jar of dulce de leche is the flavor most of the table is waiting for.

Why it tastes this way

A Buenos Aires panqueque is a thin egg batter, and the sweetness stays in the filling. Dulce de leche tastes of caramel and cooked cream, thick enough to stay in a ribbon when the crepe is rolled warm. The batter itself should taste lightly of milk and browned butter, not of sugar. A pinch of salt keeps the milk from tasting flat.

How it is made

Whisk flour, egg, milk, melted butter, and salt. Rest the batter, cook thin crepes in a buttered pan, and roll them around dulce de leche.

Authentic touch. Cook the crepes thin enough to see the pan through the batter, and fill them while they are still flexible. Cold crepes crack. Warm the dulce de leche if it is too stiff to spread, without boiling it.

How it is used

Serve them warm, two to a plate, as dessert or a sweet breakfast. A dust of sugar is optional and is not required once the filling is in.

Field notes

  • In Buenos Aires the usual filling is dulce de leche, milk cooked with sugar until it turns tan.
  • The batter is a thin wheat crepe. It is not a corn arepa and not a fritter.
  • Roll the crepes while they are warm so they bend instead of cracking.

Ingredients

  • 100 g wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 250 ml milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter, plus more for the pan
  • Pinch of salt
  • 200 g dulce de leche

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, egg, milk, melted butter, and salt into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Heat a skillet, film it with butter, and pour in just enough batter to coat the bottom. Swirl.
  3. Cook until the edge lifts and the underside spots, then flip and cook briefly.
  4. Spread dulce de leche on the warm crepe and roll it.
  5. Repeat with the rest of the batter and serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Dulce de membrillo, quince paste, is another Río de la Plata filling. The Uruguayan page uses it on purpose.
  • A savory ham filling adds meat and is not vegetarian.
  • Orange zest in the batter is a home perfume. It does not replace the dulce de leche.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Café con leche
  • A glass of milk
  • Extra dulce de leche at the table
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Australia · No chili heat · About 8 wide pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Australia · Oceania

Australian pancakes

Wide, thin lemon-and-sugar pancakes with a lacy brown rim and a soft center.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · About 8 wide pancakes

History

These are the larger Australian pancakes, thinner than pikelets and finished with lemon and sugar. British settlers brought that lemon-and-sugar habit, and weekend pans still use it. The batter on this page is looser than a pikelet batter, and the cakes are cooked wider in the pan. Pikelets remain the small thick tea cakes. They are a different recipe, with less milk and a smaller spoon, and they are not repeated here.

Cultural background

A home breakfast pan, often on a slow morning, is the setting. The pancakes are stacked or folded, with lemon squeezed over sugar so the juice makes a quick syrup. They are dessert when the same pan is cooked after supper. They are not the jam pikelets of afternoon tea.

Why it tastes this way

More milk makes a thinner batter, so the cake spreads and browns in a lacy rim instead of rising tall. Egg keeps it flexible. Sugar in the batter is slight. The real sweetness and the acid are lemon and sugar on the hot surface, which taste bright rather than like jam. Butter in the pan adds a nutty edge.

How it is made

Whisk flour with a little baking powder, sugar, salt, egg, and enough milk to make a batter that spreads in the pan. Cook wide rounds, flipping once. Sprinkle sugar on the hot pancake and squeeze lemon over it. Fold and serve.

Authentic touch. Use the looser batter and a wider round, then add lemon and sugar. If you thicken the batter and drop it by the tablespoon, you are making pikelets. Say the difference while you cook: these are the larger, thinner pancakes.

How it is used

These pancakes are breakfast or a simple dessert. Lemon and sugar are the finish. They can be a Shrove-style treat in houses that keep that calendar, and they are also an ordinary weekend breakfast. Pikelets are the tea-table cousin, not this plate.

Field notes

  • These pancakes are larger and thinner than Australian pikelets.
  • The batter has more milk than a pikelet batter, so it spreads.
  • Lemon and sugar, not jam, is the finish written here.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup wheat flour (about 125 g)
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1 tablespoon sugar, plus more to finish
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 egg
  • 1 1/4 cups milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted unsalted butter, plus more for the pan
  • 2 lemons, cut into wedges

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, sugar, and salt. Add the egg, milk, and melted butter and whisk to a smooth, loose batter. It should spread in the pan, unlike a thick pikelet batter.
  2. Rest the batter for 10 minutes.
  3. Butter a wide pan over medium heat. Pour enough batter to make a round larger and thinner than a pikelet.
  4. Cook until the surface looks set and the underside is browned in spots, then flip once.
  5. Sprinkle sugar on the hot pancake and squeeze lemon over it. Fold and serve. Repeat with the rest.

The batter contains wheat, egg, and dairy. Lemon juice on a hot cake can spatter if you squeeze hard from above the pan.

Eat them as they come off the pan. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat covered. Add lemon and sugar after reheating so the juice does not make the crumb soggy in the refrigerator. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A squeeze of orange instead of lemon is sweeter and less sharp.
  • A handful of berries in the pan makes a different cake and is not required.
  • Maple syrup is a later pantry habit. This page stays with lemon and sugar.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Lemon wedges
  • Sugar
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Bacon griddle cakes

Salty, smoky bacon in a lightly sweet wheat pancake, cooked in bacon fat.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 cakes

History

Bacon griddle cakes cook chopped bacon into a wheat pancake so the breakfast meat and the cake come off the same griddle. The idea is a savory American morning plate. Nothing about it is older than the habit of serving bacon beside pancakes and then putting the bacon inside them. Madison is the map pin for a widespread plate, not a local invention.

Cultural background

The bacon is cooked first, and some of its fat greases the griddle. The batter is a plain buttermilk pancake. This recipe contains meat, so it is not vegetarian.

Why it tastes this way

Rendered bacon fat browns the cake and carries smoke and salt into the crumb. Pieces of bacon stay chewy if they were cooked crisp before they went in. Maple syrup at the table is the sweet against that salt. A batter that is very sweet fights the bacon.

How it is made

Buttermilk pancakes with chopped cooked bacon, griddled in bacon fat.

Authentic touch. Cook the bacon first and use a spoon of the fat on the griddle. Raw bacon stirred into batter will not crisp.

How it is used

Hot, with maple syrup if you want the salty-sweet plate.

Field notes

  • The bacon has to be cooked before it is added, or it stays flabby inside the cake.
  • This is a meat pancake. The diet list does not call it vegetarian.

Ingredients

  • 6 slices bacon
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon unsalted butter, melted

Steps

  1. Cook the bacon in a skillet until crisp. Drain it on a rack, reserve 2 tablespoons of the fat, and chop the bacon.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  3. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter. Fold in the chopped bacon.
  4. Heat a griddle over medium heat and brush it with the reserved bacon fat.
  5. Pour 1/4 cup cakes. Flip when the tops set and the undersides are brown.
  6. Cook until the center is done. Serve hot, with maple syrup if you want it.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A fully savory cake leaves out the sugar.
  • Sausage in place of bacon is a different meat and still not vegetarian.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Maple syrup
  • Fried eggs
  • Black coffee
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Bahamas · No chili heat · 1 skillet cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Bahamas · Caribbean

Bahamian johnnycake

A mildly sweet wheat cake, buttery, cooked covered in a skillet so the center steams.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 8 · 1 skillet cake

History

Bahamian johnnycake is a wheat bread, mildly sweet, enriched with butter and milk. Baked versions, closer to a low loaf, are common in home ovens. This page is the pan version: one cake cooked in a covered skillet and turned once. It is not the thin white-flint-corn johnnycake of Rhode Island, and it is not Jamaican festival, which is a fried cornmeal log. The shared word hides three different doughs.

Cultural background

In Nassau and the Family Islands, johnnycake shows up at breakfast and beside stews. The baked loaf is what many people mean by the name. The skillet cake here is the same dough cooked on the stove when the oven is not the point.

Why it tastes this way

Sugar stays in the background, so the cake tastes of wheat and butter rather than of a doughnut. Milk softens the crumb. A lid traps steam and helps the center cook before the bottom scorches. The crust is browned only where it touches the pan. There is no cornmeal in this recipe, which is why it does not taste like a hoecake.

How it is made

Cut butter into flour, sugar, baking powder, and salt. Mix in milk, pat the dough into a buttered skillet, cover, and cook over low heat, turning once.

Authentic touch. Keep the heat low and the lid on. High heat burns the bottom while the center stays raw. If the cake is thick, give it time after the flip. Baked johnnycake uses the same dough in a tin, in an oven, until a skewer comes out clean.

How it is used

Slice it warm for breakfast or set it next to a stew. Say clearly, if you bake a loaf instead, that you have made the oven version of the same bread.

Field notes

  • This page is a skillet johnnycake. Baked loaves are also common in the Bahamas.
  • Rhode Island johnnycakes are a thin cornmeal cake. The Bahamian one is wheat.
  • The dough is slightly sweet and enriched, closer to a quick bread than to a fritter.

Ingredients

  • 250 g wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 40 g cold butter, plus more for the skillet
  • 120 ml milk

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, sugar, baking powder, and salt. Rub in the cold butter until the mixture is mealy.
  2. Stir in the milk until a soft dough forms. Do not knead it hard.
  3. Butter a skillet, pat the dough in, and cover the pan.
  4. Cook over low heat for about 12 minutes, until the bottom is browned and the top has set.
  5. Turn the cake, cover again, and cook until the center is done, about 10 minutes more. Slice and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bake the dough in a loaf tin if you want the oven johnnycake. The flavor is the same family.
  • Coconut milk can replace part of the dairy milk in some Family Island recipes.
  • A spoon of cornmeal is a different bread. This Nassau-style cake is wheat.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Stewed fish, a plate that adds fish
  • Butter and a little guava jam
  • Coffee or cocoa
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 8 pancakes with sauce

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Bananas Foster pancakes

A plain buttermilk pancake under bananas in brown sugar, butter, cinnamon, and rum.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes with sauce

History

Bananas Foster is a 1951 dessert created at Brennan's in New Orleans, bananas cooked with butter, brown sugar, cinnamon, and rum, and named Bananas Foster. Pancakes with that sauce are a later adaptation, not the original restaurant dessert. The cakes here are a wheat stack with the banana sauce spooned over them. The restaurant dessert is flambéed, and this home sauce cooks the rum in the pan.

Cultural background

New Orleans already had bananas through the fruit trade. The 1951 dessert is a restaurant showpiece. Putting the sauce on pancakes came afterward, on brunch menus and in home kitchens.

Why it tastes this way

Brown sugar and butter make a caramel that sticks to the banana. Rum perfumes the sauce once the raw alcohol cooks off. Cinnamon is the spice. The pancake underneath is plain so the sauce stays the loud flavor. A very thick stack will not warm through under a spoon of sauce.

How it is made

Griddled buttermilk pancakes with a stovetop banana, brown sugar, and rum sauce.

Authentic touch. Make the sauce and the pancakes. A bottled banana syrup on a stack is not Bananas Foster. Simmer the rum. You do not need to light the pan.

How it is used

Pancakes on plates, sauce and bananas spooned over while hot.

Field notes

  • Bananas Foster was created in 1951 at Brennan's in New Orleans.
  • The pancake version is a later adaptation of that dessert.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle
  • 4 tablespoons unsalted butter, for the sauce
  • 1/2 cup packed brown sugar
  • 2 ripe bananas, sliced
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 2 tablespoons dark rum

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, and salt. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted tablespoon of butter.
  2. Cook 8 small pancakes on a buttered griddle over medium heat, flipping once, until the centers are set. Keep them warm.
  3. For the sauce, melt 4 tablespoons of butter in a skillet. Stir in the brown sugar and cinnamon until the sugar dissolves.
  4. Add the bananas and cook about 1 minute, until they soften at the edges.
  5. Add the rum and simmer 1 minute so the sauce thickens slightly. You do not need to set it on fire.
  6. Spoon the bananas and sauce over the pancakes and serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the rum for an alcohol-free butter and brown-sugar banana sauce.
  • The original dessert was served over ice cream, which is the 1951 plate, not this pancake.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • The banana sauce
  • A little extra rum left out of the pan if you want it at the table
  • Coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free
