India · Mild · 6 cheelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Besan cheela

A savory gram-flour pancake with onion, ajwain, and cumin, soft in the middle and spotted brown.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 cheelas

History

Besan cheela is a North Indian gram-flour pancake, mixed and cooked at once. Besan is chickpea flour. Delhi breakfasts and Rajasthani kitchens both keep it as a fast meal, not as a fermented dosa. There is no rice and no urad soak. Onion, ajwain, and a green chile go into the bowl. A Gujarati pudla is a related besan cake with its own page and its own breakfast habits.

Cultural background

Cheela, also spelled chilla, is weeknight cooking: flour, water, spices, a pan. It does not ask for a grindstone or an overnight bowl. That speed is why it sits in a different column from dosa, even though both are round and griddled.

Why it tastes this way

Chickpea flour tastes nutty and slightly sweet once it cooks. Ajwain adds a thyme-like sharpness that North Indian batters use to keep besan from feeling heavy. Cumin and onion make it savory. Because nothing ferments, there is no sour smell. The mild chile is a lift, not a contest.

How it is made

Whisk besan with water, onion, and spices, rest it briefly, and cook small pancakes in a filmed pan.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter 10 minutes so the besan hydrates, then cook on medium. High heat scorches the outside and leaves raw flour inside.

How it is used

Eat hot with mint-cilantro chutney or ketchup. It is a breakfast or a tiffin, not a dessert.

Field notes

  • Besan is chickpea flour. This cheela is unfermented.
  • With only besan and no wheat, the written recipe is gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup besan (chickpea flour), with no wheat added
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/4 teaspoon ajwain seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk besan, salt, and water into a lump-free batter about as thick as heavy cream.
  2. Stir in onion, chile, cumin, ajwain, and cilantro. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Heat a pan and oil it lightly. Pour a small ladle and spread it into a round.
  4. Cook until the top looks set and the underside is brown in spots. Flip and cook the other side.
  5. Repeat, stirring the bowl so the onion stays mixed in.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Grated carrot in the batter
  • A pinch of turmeric if you want color without making the Gujarati pudla

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Mint and cilantro chutney
  • Sliced onion
  • Plain yogurt, a dairy side
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Bolivia · No chili heat · 16 fritters

Flying Pancakes of the World

Bolivia · South America

Bolivian buñuelos

Puffed fried dough scented with anise, served beside a cup of api, not cooked in it.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · 16 fritters

History

Bolivian buñuelos are fried dough, often eaten with api at Todos Santos and also at breakfast in the highlands. Api is a hot purple-corn drink, spiced with cinnamon and sweetened, served in a cup beside the fritters. It is not part of this dough. The buñuelos themselves are wheat flour, egg, milk, and anise, spooned into oil and fried until they puff. They are fried. Mexican Christmas buñuelos are a different fritter, rolled thin and soaked in piloncillo syrup. These are the highland spoon-drop kind.

Cultural background

Around the first days of November, Todos Santos, families in La Paz and other highland cities fry buñuelos and pour api. The same pair shows up at ordinary breakfasts when the weather is cold. The drink and the fritter share a table. They do not share a bowl.

Why it tastes this way

Anise is the perfume in the dough, and frying turns the outside brown and crisp while baking powder and steam open the center. Milk and egg make the crumb tender rather than cracker-dry. There is only a little sugar in the dough, so the fritter is mildly sweet and the cup of api, if you serve it, carries more of the sugar. Purple corn in the drink tastes fruity and earthy. None of that corn is in the fritter.

How it is made

Mix wheat flour, egg, milk, sugar, anise, baking powder, and salt into a soft dough. Fry spoons of it until puffed and golden. Offer api, the purple-corn drink, on the side if you are making the full Todos Santos plate.

Authentic touch. Fry spoons of soft dough in oil deep enough to float them, and do not crowd the pan. They should puff and turn gold. A flat, dark fritter was fried too hot or from a dough that was too stiff. Serve api in cups next to the plate. Do not pour it into the dough.

How it is used

Serve the buñuelos hot, at breakfast or for Todos Santos, with api in a separate cup. The drink is a pairing. The recipe below is only the fried dough.

Field notes

  • Api is a purple-corn drink served beside the buñuelos. It is not mixed into the dough.
  • These buñuelos are fried and puffed, a highland breakfast and a Todos Santos food.
  • The Mexican buñuelo in this journal is a thin fried sheet with piloncillo syrup, a different recipe.

Ingredients

  • 200 g wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 100 ml milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon anise seed
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for frying

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, sugar, anise, baking powder, and salt. Beat in the egg and milk until a soft, sticky dough forms.
  2. Heat 3 centimeters of oil until a pinch of dough sizzles and rises.
  3. Fry spoons of dough, turning once, until they puff and turn gold, about 2 to 3 minutes. Do not crowd them.
  4. Drain on a rack.
  5. Serve hot. If you make api, pour that purple-corn drink into cups beside the plate. Leave it out of the dough.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Buñuelos de queso add cheese to the dough and are still fried. They still contain dairy.
  • A cinnamon syrup poured over them is closer to the Mexican Christmas fritter and is not required here.
  • Api can be made from commercial purple-corn flour and spices. Keep it in the cup.

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Api, a purple-corn drink served beside them
  • A cup of coffee
  • A dust of sugar for people who want them sweeter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Brazil · No chili heat · 4 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Brazil · South America

Brazilian beiju

A plain tapioca flatbread, soft or crisp depending on how long it stays on the griddle, with almost no seasoning.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 flatbreads

History

Beiju is an Indigenous tapioca flatbread. Tupi-Guarani peoples use beiju. That name covers a language family spread over a wide region, not one village and not a secret recipe this page can honestly claim. The street tapioca crepe filled with cheese or coconut is a later urban relative that uses the same starch. Today's home beiju starts from commercial tapioca starch, which is already processed. It is a plain round cooked on a griddle. It is not a lesson in grating and pressing bitter cassava.

Cultural background

In Paraíba, Pernambuco, and other Northeastern kitchens, beiju is breakfast and a snack, sometimes soft, sometimes dried until it snaps. People eat it with butter, coconut, or nothing at all. This recipe leaves the cheese off so the flatbread itself is clear. Butter would add dairy. It is not in the ingredient list.

Why it tastes this way

Commercial tapioca starch is mild, almost neutral, with a faint sweetness when it toasts. Salt is optional and small. A short cook keeps the round flexible and chewy. A longer cook dries it toward a cracker. There is no cheese to salt it and no wheat to brown like bread. The flavor is the starch and the pan.

How it is made

Moisten dry commercial tapioca starch if needed, salt it lightly, sieve it into a dry skillet, and cook until the round holds together. Leave it longer if you want it crisp.

Authentic touch. Sieve the moist starch into an even layer and leave it alone until it coheres. Stirring breaks the sheet. For a crisp beiju, keep it on the heat longer after it sets, until the surface looks dry. Use commercial starch only.

How it is used

Eat it warm as a plain bread, next to coffee, or with coconut and salt if you want the Northeastern snack. Cheese belongs on the tapioca-crepe page, not in this dough.

Field notes

  • Tupi-Guarani is a language family. This page does not invent a single tribe's secret recipe.
  • Use commercial tapioca starch. Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous, and home processing is not taught here.
  • The filled street tapioca is a later relative. Beiju on this page is the plain flatbread.

Ingredients

  • 200 g commercial tapioca starch
  • Up to 80 ml water, only if the starch is dry
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon neutral oil, only if you need to wipe a sticky pan

Steps

  1. If the commercial starch is dry, moisten it with just enough water to feel like damp sand. Toss with the salt.
  2. Heat a dry nonstick skillet over medium heat.
  3. Sieve a thin, even layer of starch into the pan. Do not stir.
  4. Cook until the round holds together and the underside spots. For a crisp beiju, leave it longer, until the surface looks dry.
  5. Slide it out and repeat. Serve warm or let the crisp ones cool.

Use commercial tapioca starch. Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This recipe does not teach home processing of raw cassava.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Beiju de tapioca with coconut is a common Northeast snack. Coconut does not add wheat.
  • The filled cheese crepe is the other tapioca page in this journal.
  • Drying the round completely makes a cracker that keeps. A shorter cook makes a flexible bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Black coffee
  • Grated coconut and a pinch of salt
  • Butter, which adds dairy and means the bread is no longer vegan
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Brazil · No chili heat · 4 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Brazil · South America

Brazilian tapioca crepes

A lacy crepe of hydrated tapioca starch, folded over fresh cheese, chewy and mild.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 crepes

History

Street tapioca in Recife and across the Brazilian Northeast is hydrated tapioca starch, called goma, scattered in a skillet until it sets into a crepe and folded over cheese or coconut. The starch is commercial and already processed. Beiju, the plain Indigenous tapioca flatbread, is the older relative and has its own page. This one is the filled street crepe. It is gluten-free if nothing else is added. It is not a wheat panqueca and not a corn arepa.

Cultural background

Corner stands keep moist goma in a bowl, sieve it into a hot pan, and fill the round while it is still flexible. Cheese is the usual breakfast filling. Coconut and condensed milk are the sweet ones. This recipe uses cheese.

Why it tastes this way

Tapioca starch has almost no flavor of its own, so the crepe tastes clean, slightly sweet, and chewy, with a few toasted spots where the pan was hottest. Salt wakes it up. Fresh cheese is the savory part, milky and a little tangy, and it softens against the heat. There is no egg and no wheat, which is why the texture is elastic rather than bready.

How it is made

Sieve moist commercial tapioca starch into a nonstick skillet in a thin even layer, let it set, add cheese, fold, and turn once.

Authentic touch. The goma should feel like damp sand. If you only have dry commercial tapioca starch, sprinkle on just enough water to moisten it, then push it through a sieve into the pan. Do not stir it like a batter. Let the grains lace together.

How it is used

Eat it immediately, folded, at breakfast or from a street stand. It toughens as it cools. Coconut filling is the sweet alternative and is not in this batch.

Field notes

  • Use commercial goma de tapioca. Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous, and this recipe does not teach home processing.
  • The crepe is starch grains that fuse in the pan. There is no wheat batter to stir.
  • Cheese tapioca is a Northeast street breakfast, especially in Recife.

Ingredients

  • 200 g commercial hydrated tapioca starch (goma de tapioca)
  • Up to 80 ml water, only if the starch is dry
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 120 g sliced fresh white cheese

Steps

  1. If the commercial starch is dry, work in just enough water that it feels like damp sand. If it is already hydrated goma, use it as it is. Toss with the salt.
  2. Heat a nonstick skillet over medium heat. Do not add oil.
  3. Sieve a thin layer of starch into the pan, covering the bottom. Let it sit until the grains fuse into a crepe and the underside spots.
  4. Lay on the cheese, fold, and turn once until the cheese softens.
  5. Serve at once and repeat.

Use commercial goma de tapioca or packaged tapioca starch. Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This recipe does not teach home processing of raw cassava.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Grated coconut and a little salt, without cheese, can be dairy-free if the starch was moistened with water only.
  • Condensed milk is a sweet street filling and keeps the dairy allergen.
  • Beiju is the unfilled flatbread. Do not confuse the two plates.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Black coffee
  • Shredded coconut and condensed milk, a sweet version that is still dairy if the milk stays
  • A fresh fruit juice
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Bulgaria · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Bulgaria · Eastern Europe

Bulgarian palachinki

Thin Bulgarian pancakes with yogurt in the batter, sweet or with white cheese.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Palachinki are Bulgaria's thin pancakes. A spoon of yogurt in the batter is a home habit and is how this page is written. They are eaten with jam or powdered sugar, and also in a savory way with sirene, the brined white cheese. Both servings are real. This recipe writes the sweet jam plate and names sirene as a variation so the ingredients stay a dessert batter. They are not Serbian palačinke and not the same filling as the Croatian page.

Cultural background

They are breakfast, snack, and dessert. Yogurt is a staple in Bulgarian cooking, so it is a natural liquid in a batter. The cakes are still thin, not a risen stack.

Why it tastes this way

Yogurt adds a gentle tang that milk alone does not. The crepe browns in butter and tastes slightly sour under the jam. Sirene, if you choose the savory plate, is salty and briny against that tang. The sweet version lets the jam stay bright.

How it is made

Flour, egg, milk, and yogurt make a thin batter. The crepes are cooked in butter and served with jam.

Authentic touch. Use plain yogurt, not a sweetened one. Keep the cakes thin. If you fill them with sirene, call the plate savory and expect a salty cheese, not a dessert.

How it is used

Rolled with jam, as breakfast or dessert. A sirene filling turns them savory.

Field notes

  • Yogurt in the batter is the Bulgarian home touch on this page.
  • Jam is the sweet serving. Sirene, a brined white cheese, is the savory one.
  • The cakes are thin palachinki, not small yeast pancakes.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • 200 ml whole milk
  • 100 ml plain yogurt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, salt, eggs, milk, and yogurt into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in butter until both sides are spotted.
  3. Spread with jam, or leave them plain for a savory cheese filling.
  4. Roll or fold and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Crumbled sirene and dill, a savory plate
  • A stack dusted only with sugar

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Jam
  • Powdered sugar
  • Sirene, for the savory variation
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Venezuela · No chili heat · 4 cachapas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Venezuela · South America

Cachapas

Coarse sweet-corn pancakes folded over fresh white cheese, juicy rather than bready.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 cachapas

History

A cachapa is a Venezuelan sweet-corn pancake, sold at roadside stands and folded over fresh cheese. It is not an arepa. An arepa is made from precooked or cooked dried corn. A cachapa is made from fresh kernels blended until coarse. It is not masa. Corn cakes of this region are older than wheat hotcakes, and the cachapa sits in the fresh-corn branch of that family, beside Colombia's arepa de choclo but with its own shape: a wide, folded pancake rather than a thick griddled round of dried corn.

Cultural background

Stands along the roads out of Maracay and across the center of the country grind corn to order. The cheese is often queso de mano or another fresh white cheese that softens in the heat. People eat cachapas as breakfast, a drive-out lunch, or a rainy-afternoon snack.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh corn carries sugar and juice, so the pancake tastes sweet without a dessert amount of sugar. Leaving the grind coarse keeps bursts of kernel instead of a uniform custard. Butter browns at the edge. Fresh cheese is salty and milky and melts only enough to soften. The fold traps steam, which is why a cachapa should be eaten before it slumps.

How it is made

Pulse fresh corn with salt, a little sugar, and a splash of milk. Cook wide pancakes in butter and fold them over slices of fresh white cheese.

Authentic touch. Pulse the corn. Do not liquefy it. The batter should be spoonable and full of pieces. Cook it thicker than a wheat crepe, on moderate heat, and fold it over the cheese while the pancake is hot. Do not add precooked arepa flour and call it the same thing.

How it is used

Serve them at once, folded, from the pan or wrapped in paper the way a stand does. They are a snack or a breakfast, not a stuffed dried-corn arepa.

Field notes

  • A cachapa is not an arepa. One is fresh corn. The other is cooked dried corn.
  • The batter should stay coarse. A smooth puree loses the kernel texture.
  • Fold the cheese in while the pancake is hot so it softens without a long melt.

Ingredients

  • 4 cups fresh sweet-corn kernels
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 2 tablespoons milk
  • 150 g fresh white cheese, sliced
  • Butter for the skillet

Steps

  1. Pulse the corn, salt, sugar, and milk until coarse and thick. Stop before it is a smooth liquid.
  2. Heat a skillet over medium heat and add butter.
  3. Spread a wide, thick pancake of batter. Cook until the underside browns and the top sets.
  4. Flip, lay on the cheese, and cook until the center is done.
  5. Fold and serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Queso de mano is the classic melting fresh cheese. Another mild white cheese still works.
  • A sweeter batter, with more sugar, is a home choice. Roadside cachapas are only gently sweet.
  • An arepa uses dried precooked corn. Do not substitute that flour here.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • More fresh cheese
  • A pat of butter
  • Black coffee or a cold fruit juice
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 hotcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Cape Cod cranberry hotcakes

Tart cranberry pieces in a lightly sweet, orange-scented wheat cake.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 hotcakes

History

Cape Cod cranberry hotcakes put chopped cranberries in a wheat batter. Cranberries are native to the bogs of southeastern Massachusetts, and Wampanoag people used them long before commercial bogs. The hotcake is a later breakfast. Commercial cranberry agriculture expanded on the Cape in the nineteenth century.

Cultural background

Fresh cranberries are sharp. Chopping them keeps that tartness in pieces instead of staining every bite the same color. A little orange zest is a common local pairing with the berry.

Why it tastes this way

Cranberries are high in acid and pectin, so they stay bright against a sweet crumb instead of tasting like jam. Extra sugar in this batter is there to meet that acid. Orange zest smells floral and keeps the cake from tasting only sour.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter studded with chopped cranberries.

Authentic touch. Chop fresh or frozen cranberries. Whole berries burst and leave you with raw, hard centers unless the cakes overcook.

How it is used

Hot, with butter. A little maple syrup is enough.

Field notes

  • Cranberries are native to southeastern Massachusetts bogs.
  • Wampanoag use of the berry is far older than the wheat hotcake.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup fresh or frozen cranberries, chopped
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 3 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon finely grated orange zest
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Chop the cranberries into small pieces. If frozen, chop them while frozen.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, salt, and orange zest.
  3. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter. Fold in the cranberries.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat and pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the underside is golden. Cook until no wet batter remains.
  6. Serve with butter and a light pour of maple syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Dried cranberries, chopped and soaked 10 minutes, work when fresh berries are out of season.
  • Leave out the orange zest if you want the berry alone.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup
  • Hot tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 8 blintzes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Cheese blintzes

A thin, lightly sweet pancake wrapped around tangy soft cheese and fried in butter.

35 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 blintzes

History

Cheese blintzes are thin pancakes wrapped around a sweet cheese filling, an Ashkenazi dish from Eastern Europe that became Lower East Side and wider New York home cooking. Dairy blintzes are a Shavuot food and also an ordinary breakfast. The wrapper is a thin egg batter, and the cake is fried again after it is filled.

Cultural background

Farmer cheese or dry cottage cheese is the usual filling. Wet ricotta needs to be drained or the blintz leaks. The Eastern European origin and the New York life of the dish are both part of the story.

Why it tastes this way

The wrapper tastes of egg and butter, and the second frying makes it crisp at the seam. Farmer cheese is tangy and slightly dry, so the filling stays in a soft curd instead of running. A little sugar and lemon zest keep it from tasting like a plain cheese omelet.

How it is made

Thin pancakes filled with sweet cheese, folded, and fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Cook the wrappers on one side, fill the pale side, and fry the filled packets in butter until both faces are golden. Boiling them is not the dish.

How it is used

Hot, with sour cream or a little jam. Applesauce is a common side.

Field notes

  • Blintzes traveled from Eastern European Ashkenazi cooking to New York, including the Lower East Side.
  • Cheese blintzes are a dairy dish associated with Shavuot and also eaten as breakfast.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 cup whole milk
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 tablespoon unsalted butter, melted, plus 2 tablespoons for frying
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup farmer cheese
  • 1 large egg yolk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon finely grated lemon zest

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, milk, whole eggs, melted tablespoon of butter, and salt into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Stir the farmer cheese, egg yolk, sugar, and lemon zest.
  3. Butter an 8-inch skillet lightly and heat it over medium. Pour about 1/4 cup batter and swirl it into a thin round.
  4. Cook until the top is set and the underside is spotted. Slide it out cooked side up. Repeat, stacking the wrappers.
  5. Spoon cheese onto the pale side of each wrapper. Fold the sides in and roll the wrapper closed.
  6. Fry the rolls in the remaining butter over medium heat until golden on both sides. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A potato filling makes a savory blintz and is a different recipe.
  • Dry cottage cheese can stand in for farmer cheese if it is drained well.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Feast or fast day
  • Sour cream
  • Fruit jam
  • Applesauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Bangladesh · No chili heat · 12 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Bangladesh · South Asia

Chitoi pitha

Small, soft rice cakes with a dimpled top, mild and a little sweet if date molasses is poured on.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 small cakes

History

Chitoi pitha are Bangladeshi rice cakes cooked in a dimpled chitoi pan during winter pitha season. Rice flour and water, sometimes a little salt, set over the pits of a special pan into small cushioned cakes. They belong to the same winter weeks as other pithas, when date-palm molasses is in season. They are a mold cake, not a rolled patishapta and not a fried malpua.

Cultural background

Dhaka homes and village kitchens both bring the pan out in the cool months. The cakes are often eaten with khejur gur, date molasses, or with a savory bhorta. This recipe keeps the cake plain so either path stays open. The written ingredients have no wheat.

Why it tastes this way

Rice flour and water taste clean and slightly sweet once they steam in the molds. Browning is light, so the flavor stays soft. Date molasses, if you use it, adds mineral caramel. A savory side such as mashed dried fish would add fish and a salty contrast. The dimples of the pan brown a pattern into a cake that would otherwise be plain.

How it is made

Mix a smooth rice-flour batter and cook it in a covered dimpled chitoi pan until the cakes set.

Authentic touch. Wipe a tiny film of oil on hot molds, then pour. Too much oil fries the cakes hard. Too little, and they weld to the pan. Covering the pan helps the tops set.

How it is used

Serve warm with date molasses, or with grated coconut and a pinch of salt. Winter is the season.

Field notes

  • Chitoi pitha are winter cakes, cooked when Bangladesh is in pitha season.
  • The dimpled pan gives the cakes their shape and their spotted crust.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 1/4 cups water
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for the molds

Steps

  1. Whisk rice flour, salt, and water into a smooth batter about as thick as crepe batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Heat a chitoi pan over medium heat. Brush the molds lightly with oil.
  3. Fill each mold about two-thirds full. Cover the pan.
  4. Cook until the tops look set and the undersides are pale gold, then turn the cakes if the pan allows, or cover longer until the centers are cooked.
  5. Unmold and repeat. Serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of soaked rice ground into the batter for a softer crumb
  • A savory top of onion and green chile

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Feast or fast day
  • Breakfast
  • Date-palm molasses
  • Grated coconut with salt
  • A vegetable bhorta
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Chocolate chip pancakes

A plain sweet pancake with pockets of melted semisweet chocolate.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Chocolate chip pancakes are a late twentieth-century American diner cake, wheat batter with pieces of chocolate melted into the crumb. They have no older regional origin. Columbus, Ohio, is the map pin only as a stand-in for that diner breakfast. Most semisweet chocolate chips contain soy lecithin, so soy is listed here.

Cultural background

The cake is the milk-and-egg diner pancake with chocolate added. It is not a cocoa batter. The chips should stay in pieces so the crumb is not uniformly brown.

Why it tastes this way

Chocolate melts at the griddle face and stays in pockets inside. If the chips are stirred too long they streak and sink. Sugar in the batter and sugar in the chocolate make this one of the sweeter cakes in the set. Butter at the edge is the plain contrast.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter with semisweet chocolate chips.

Authentic touch. Scatter the chips on the cakes after pouring, or fold them in gently at the end. Do not blend them into a cocoa paste.

How it is used

Hot, when the chips are still soft. Syrup is optional because the chocolate is already sweet.

Field notes

  • This is a late twentieth-century diner pancake, not an older regional recipe.
  • Standard semisweet chips usually contain soy lecithin, which is why soy is an allergen here.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups whole milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle
  • 3/4 cup semisweet chocolate chips, which usually contain soy lecithin

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, and salt.
  2. Stir in the milk, egg, and melted butter until just combined.
  3. Butter a griddle over medium-low heat. Chocolate scorches easily.
  4. Pour 1/4 cup cakes and scatter chocolate chips over each one.
  5. Flip when the underside is golden and the chips look glossy. Cook briefly on the second side.
  6. Serve while the chocolate is soft.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chop a semisweet chocolate bar if you want larger melts.
  • A cocoa-powder batter is a different, fully chocolate cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Butter
  • A little maple syrup
  • Cold milk
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Colombia · No chili heat · 6 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Colombian arepas

A thinner white-corn arepa with cheese in the dough, buttered, beside tomato and scallion hogao.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 arepas

History

In Antioquia the everyday arepa is a thin white-corn round, cooked on a griddle and set beside eggs, beans, or hogao. Cheese may be mixed into the dough. Butter on a hot arepa is a normal breakfast. This is a different plate from the thick split arepa of a Venezuelan counter. Both are corn cakes, and corn cakes in this part of the world are older than wheat hotcakes. The thin Antioquia cake is not a fresh-corn arepa de choclo and not a Mexican tortilla. Precooked cornmeal is the home flour, not nixtamalized masa.

Cultural background

Medellín breakfasts often include an arepa, a small cup of coffee, and eggs. Hogao, scallions and tomato cooked in oil with cumin, is the sauce that shows up next to beans and rice as well. This recipe puts a little cheese in the dough and serves the cake whole, with butter and hogao, rather than splitting it into a sandwich.

Why it tastes this way

A thinner cake dries and toasts more than a thick Caracas arepa, so you taste browned corn at the surface. Cheese in the dough adds salt and a little fat, which helps the round brown. Butter on the hot cake smells milky. Hogao is sweet from cooked tomato, green from scallion, and warm from cumin, without chile heat. The plate is savory and mild.

How it is made

Mix precooked white cornmeal, warm water, salt, and grated cheese. Pat thin rounds, griddle them, and serve with butter and a cooked hogao of scallion, tomato, and cumin.

Authentic touch. Use a bit more water than you would for a thick stuffed arepa, and pat the rounds thinner, under a centimeter. Cook them longer than you think, until the outside is dry and spotted. Hogao should be cooked until the tomato collapses, not served as a raw salsa.

How it is used

Serve the arepas whole at breakfast, with butter melting on top and hogao on the side. Eggs and red beans can share the plate. They are not folded over cheese the way a cachapa is.

Field notes

  • Antioquia's daily arepa is usually thinner than a stuffed Caracas arepa and is often left whole.
  • Hogao is cooked scallion and tomato. It is not a raw Mexican salsa.
  • The flour is precooked cornmeal, not masa harina and not fresh choclo.

Ingredients

  • 200 g precooked white cornmeal
  • 300 ml warm water
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt, divided
  • 80 g grated fresh white cheese
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 1 ripe tomato, chopped
  • 1 tablespoon oil, plus butter for the table
  • 1/4 teaspoon ground cumin

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal, 1/2 teaspoon salt, cheese, and warm water into a soft dough that can be patted thin. Rest 5 minutes.
  2. Fry the scallions in the oil, add the tomato, cumin, and remaining salt, and cook until the tomato collapses. That is the hogao.
  3. Pat 6 thin rounds. Cook them on a griddle over medium heat until both sides are dry and spotted, several minutes a side.
  4. The cake should be cooked through, not doughy in the center.
  5. Serve whole, with butter and hogao.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Arepa de choclo, on its own page, is fresh corn and a different batter.
  • A coastal arepa de huevo is fried with an egg inside and is not vegetarian.
  • Leave the cheese and butter out, and cook the cake in oil, if you need it dairy-free and vegan.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Hogao and a pat of butter
  • Red beans and a fried egg, which adds egg
  • Small coffee, a tinto
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · No chili heat · 12 tortillas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Corn tortillas

Soft, toasted corn with a faint mineral note from nixtamal, cooked dry on a hot comal.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 tortillas

History

The corn tortilla is the foundational griddle cake of Mexico, older than wheat hotcakes by a very long stretch. The dough is masa: dried corn simmered with an alkali, rested, rinsed, and ground. That process is nixtamalization. Fresh nixtamal, ground wet, is the older method. Masa harina is dried nixtamalized corn, milled and sold as flour, and it is the practical home shortcut. Neither one is a bowl of plain untreated cornmeal.

Cultural background

A comal, a round griddle, sits on the stove in home kitchens and in tortillerías. Tortillas wrap food, accompany stews, and become the next day's chips or enchiladas. This recipe is the plain cake, just masa and water.

Why it tastes this way

Nixtamal gives the tortilla its corn aroma and a slight mineral taste that plain cornmeal never has. The alkali loosens the skins, changes the flavor, and makes the amino acid niacin more available. Cooked dry, the surface smells toasted. There is no wheat gluten to make it bready, so the cake stays flexible only while it is warm and moist.

How it is made

Stir masa harina with warm water and salt, rest it covered, and press thin rounds. Cook on a hot dry comal until the tortilla releases, flips, and puffs in spots.

Authentic touch. The dough should feel like soft clay that does not crack at the edge and does not glue itself to your palm. If it cracks, add water a teaspoon at a time. If it sticks, knead in a sprinkle of masa harina. Keep the comal dry.

How it is used

Eat them warm, wrapped in a cloth so they steam themselves soft. They are the plate under eggs, beans, salsas, and stews, and they are a meal by themselves with salt.

Field notes

  • A tortilla that puffs has enough moisture and a hot enough comal. The puff is steam, not baking powder.
  • Masa harina is already nixtamalized. Do not add cal again, and do not swap in untreated cornmeal.
  • Corn tortillas in this recipe are masa and water. Lard belongs to some flour tortillas, not to this dough.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 360 ml warm water, plus more if the dough cracks
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Extra masa harina for dusting if the dough sticks

Steps

  1. Stir the masa harina, salt, and warm water until a dough forms. Knead for a minute. It should feel like soft clay.
  2. Cover and rest for 20 minutes so the flour hydrates. Adjust with a spoon of water or a dust of masa harina.
  3. Divide into 12 pieces. Press each between plastic sheets in a tortilla press, or under a heavy skillet, into a thin round.
  4. Cook on a dry comal over medium-high heat for about 45 seconds, flip, cook the second side, then flip once more until the tortilla smells toasted and puffs in places.
  5. Wrap the tortillas in a clean cloth as they come off the heat so they stay soft.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Blue or red masa harina changes the color and keeps the same method.
  • Fresh nixtamal from a mill can replace masa harina. Use less water, because the dough is already wet.
  • A flour tortilla is a different bread, made with wheat and fat in the north.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Frijoles de la olla and a salsa
  • Scrambled eggs and avocado
  • A stew that needs a soft scoop
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Costa Rica · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Costa Rica · Mexico & Central America

Costa Rican chorreadas

Sweet fresh-corn pancakes, lightly eggy, served with sour cream.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Chorreadas are Costa Rican pancakes made from fresh sweet corn, eggs, and milk, cooked for breakfast in the Central Valley and in rural kitchens. The kernels are ground while they are fresh. That batter is not masa, and it is not the dried-corn dough of a tortilla. Corn cakes in Mexico and Central America are older than the wheat hotcakes that arrived later. A chorreada is the fresh-corn branch of that older family, finished with natilla, the local sour cream.

Cultural background

When sweet corn is in season, home cooks grind it for breakfast instead of opening a bag of flour. Alajuela and the farms around the Central Valley both claim the habit. The cake is a weekday breakfast, not a stuffed street antojito.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh corn brings its own sugar and a juicy, green-corn aroma that dried cornmeal cannot copy. Egg and milk set the pancake and add a custard note. A spoon of fine cornmeal, if you need it, only thickens the batter. It is untreated cornmeal, not nixtamal. Sour cream on top is cool, tart, and salty against the sweet corn. There is no chile.

How it is made

Blend fresh corn with egg, milk, sugar, and salt until coarse. Cook thick pancakes on a buttered skillet and serve them with sour cream.

Authentic touch. Pulse the kernels until the batter is coarse, with visible pieces of corn. A smooth puree cooks into a flat custard. If the batter spreads too fast, stir in the spoon of fine cornmeal. Do not reach for masa harina.

How it is used

Serve them warm at breakfast, with sour cream and, if you want, a little extra salt or a drizzle of honey. They are a plate by themselves.

Field notes

  • Chorreadas use fresh sweet corn. They are not tortillas and not nixtamalized masa.
  • Natilla, sour cream, is the usual finish in a Costa Rican kitchen.
  • Corn cakes were here before wheat hotcakes. This one simply keeps the corn fresh.

Ingredients

  • 4 cups fresh sweet-corn kernels, cut from the cob
  • 1 egg
  • 60 ml milk
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon fine cornmeal, not masa harina
  • Butter for the skillet
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Pulse the corn, egg, milk, sugar, and salt until the batter is thick and still coarse. Stir in the cornmeal if it looks watery.
  2. Heat a skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  3. Spoon the batter into thick rounds. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set, then flip.
  4. Cook the second side until the center is no longer wet.
  5. Serve warm with sour cream.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A Nicaraguan güirila is a different fresh-corn cake, usually with cuajada in the batter and less sugar.
  • Leave out the sour cream and you still have dairy from the milk, unless you rebuild the recipe.
  • Masa harina does not belong in this bowl. The corn has to be fresh.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Natilla or other sour cream
  • A mild white cheese on the side
  • Black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Czechia · No chili heat · 16 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Czechia · Eastern Europe

Czech lívance

Small yeasted Czech pancakes served with quark and jam, not rolled.

1 hr 20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 16 small pancakes

History

Lívance are small yeast pancakes from Czechia. They are thicker than palačinky and are served in a pile with tvaroh and jam on top, not rolled around a filling. The yeast batter needs time to rise. They are a home dessert and a substantial sweet breakfast. This page keeps them separate from the thin crepe.

Cultural background

A special small pan is sometimes used. A ordinary frying pan works if you keep the cakes small and thick. Jam and fresh cheese are spooned on after cooking.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast gives them a mild fermented flavor and an open crumb. They brown in a little fat and taste toasted at the rim. Cool quark and tart jam on a hot cake is the contrast. They are less creamy inside than a filled crepe because the cheese stays on top.

How it is made

A yeast batter of flour, milk, and egg rises, then is cooked as small thick cakes and topped with quark and jam.

Authentic touch. Do not roll them. Spoon quark and jam onto the hot cakes. Let the batter rise until it is bubbly before you fry.

How it is used

In a pile, with quark and jam, as dessert or a sweet breakfast.

Field notes

  • Lívance are small and yeasted. Palačinky are thin crepes.
  • The cheese and jam go on top. The cakes are not rolled.
  • They need a rise before cooking.

Ingredients

  • 250 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 300 ml warm milk
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • 200 g quark
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, yeast, sugar, salt, egg, and warm milk into a thick batter. Cover until bubbly, about 1 hour.
  2. Stir it down. Cook small thick rounds in butter until both sides are brown and the center is done.
  3. Stack them on plates.
  4. Top with quark and jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Plum jam, a very typical fruit
  • Thin palačinky, the rolled crepe page

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Quark or farmer cheese
  • Jam
  • A little melted butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Denmark · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Denmark · Northern Europe

Danish pandekager

Thin Danish pancakes, rolled with sugar or jam.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Danish pandekager are thin wheat pancakes, rolled with sugar, jam, or ice cream. They are everyday food and also appear around Fastelavn, the Danish carnival before Lent. Fastelavn is not only a pancake day. The cream-filled fastelavnsboller are the buns of that festival. This page does not treat the pancake as the whole of Fastelavn. Aebleskiver, the molded round cakes, are a different page.

Cultural background

A Danish home pancake is closer to a crepe than to a thick American stack. Sugar on the hot cake, then a roll, is the simplest serving. Jam is just as common.

Why it tastes this way

Egg and milk make a soft, lightly sweet sheet. Browning in butter adds the toasted note. Sugar crystals melt on contact. A tart jam keeps the roll from being only sweet. They are thin, so the surface area is the flavor.

How it is made

A thin batter of flour, egg, milk, sugar, and salt is cooked in butter and rolled with sugar or jam.

Authentic touch. Roll them while hot so the sugar sticks. A little sugar in the batter is enough. Do not confuse these with aebleskiver, which need a molded pan.

How it is used

Rolled, as dessert, breakfast, or a Fastelavn table among other foods.

Field notes

  • Pandekager are thin and rolled. Aebleskiver are the round molded cakes.
  • Fastelavn includes these pancakes and, famously, the filled buns. The buns are not this recipe.
  • Sugar or jam is the usual filling.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar, plus more for serving
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • 350 ml whole milk
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, salt, eggs, and milk into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in a buttered pan until spotted on both sides.
  3. Sprinkle with sugar or spread with jam.
  4. Roll and serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A squeeze of lemon
  • Aebleskiver, the molded cake on its own page

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Feast or fast day
  • Sugar
  • Jam
  • Vanilla ice cream, for a dessert plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 10 oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Derbyshire oatcakes

A yeasted oatmeal pancake from Derbyshire, a little thicker than the Staffordshire shop cake.

1 hr 20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 oatcakes

History

Derbyshire oatcakes are the Peak District's yeasted oatmeal pancakes. They share a method with Staffordshire oatcakes and they are not the same batter. This page uses equal parts oatmeal and wheat flour and an egg, and it cooks a smaller, thicker cake. Bakers in the two counties do not treat the recipes as interchangeable. Neither cake is a Scottish oatcake, which is unleavened and dry.

Cultural background

They are breakfast food in Derbyshire, eaten with butter, cheese, or bacon. The batter is mixed ahead so the yeast can work. A cold kitchen will take longer than an hour.

Why it tastes this way

The extra wheat and the egg make a cake that holds together when it is thicker. Oats keep it soft. Yeast keeps the sweetness in check. You taste grain first, then the salty filling.

How it is made

Oatmeal, wheat flour, yeast, egg, and warm milk ferment into a batter. Spoonfuls cook on a greased griddle until set.

Authentic touch. Do not roll these as thin as a Potteries oatcake. If the batter will not drop from a spoon in a thick ribbon, add a splash of milk.

How it is used

Buttered, or filled with cheese. They are a morning savory cake, not a dessert stack.

Field notes

  • Derbyshire and Staffordshire both keep yeasted oat pancakes, with different local batters.
  • This batter includes an egg. The Staffordshire page does not.
  • They are soft griddle cakes, not the crisp Scottish biscuit.

Ingredients

  • 150 g fine oatmeal
  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 1 large egg
  • 400 ml warm milk
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • Butter, for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix the oatmeal, flour, yeast, sugar, egg, milk, and salt. Cover until the batter is bubbly, about 1 hour.
  2. Stir it down. It should drop from a spoon in a ribbon. Add a spoon of milk if it is stiff.
  3. Grease a medium-hot griddle. Cook small rounds until the top sets, then turn and brown the second side.
  4. Serve warm with butter or cheese. They reheat in a dry pan.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The oat-heavier Staffordshire oatcake, made to fold
  • A spoon of honey on a plain one

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Butter
  • Cheese
  • Bacon, for a meat plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · 24 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Diner silver-dollar pancakes

Mild, buttery, and lightly sweet, with more browned edge than center because the cakes are small.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 24 small pancakes

History

Silver-dollar pancakes are ordinary diner pancakes cooked in rounds about the size of an old silver dollar. The small diameter browns the edge while the center stays tender, and a plate holds a pile of them. The batter is the wheat, milk, and egg mix of a twentieth-century diner, not a regional heirloom. Jersey City is the map pin for a habit that was never limited to one town.

Cultural background

Short-order cooks liked the size because the cakes finished quickly and stacked neatly on an oval plate. The name is a comparison to the coin, not a recipe tied to a mint.

Why it tastes this way

Small cakes spend more of their surface against the griddle, so they taste toastier than a wide pancake from the same batter. Milk keeps the flavor milder than buttermilk. Butter in the pan is the main richness.

How it is made

Griddled as small rounds in butter.

Authentic touch. Keep each round near 2 inches across. A full-size pancake from this batter is a different order.

How it is used

A tall pile on a warm plate, with butter and maple syrup.

Field notes

  • The name describes the diameter, about the width of an old silver dollar.
  • Diner cakes of this size cook through in a couple of minutes.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups whole milk
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, and salt.
  2. Whisk in the milk, eggs, and melted butter until the batter is smooth and pourable.
  3. Heat a griddle over medium heat and coat it lightly with butter.
  4. Pour tablespoons of batter so each cake stays about 2 inches wide.
  5. Flip when the tops look set and the undersides are golden. Cook the second side briefly.
  6. Stack and serve with butter and maple syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Use buttermilk and a pinch of baking soda if you want them tangy.
  • Cook a blueberry into the center of each round.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup
  • Fried eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free
