Djibouti · No chili heat · About 8 breakfast cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Djibouti · East Africa

Djiboutian lahoh

Small, holey breakfast cakes, mildly sour, with a soft wheat crumb.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · About 8 breakfast cakes

History

Lahoh in Djibouti is a breakfast bread in a port city where Somali, Afar, and Yemeni cooking already meet. The batter is fermented and cooked in a small pan until the top is spongy. It is related to Somali canjeero and to Yemeni lahoh, and it is not either of those pages. This version is the Djibouti morning cake: a wheat batter, a little sorghum for grain flavor, eaten early with honey or eggs, not as a liner under a platter of stews.

Cultural background

Breakfast in the city can be lahoh, tea, and something sweet or a boiled egg. The bread is made at home and also bought from women who cook it to order on a round pan. The ferment usually starts the evening before.

Why it tastes this way

The overnight batter is gently sour. Sorghum beside the wheat adds a drier, grainier note than a sweet white loaf. The holes catch honey or a little butter. Because the cakes are small and thick, they taste softer and less like a sheet of injera. Egg on the side tastes of the egg, not of the batter.

How it is made

Mix wheat flour, a little sorghum flour, yeast, sugar, and water. Ferment it overnight, then salt it. Cook small rounds on one side in a covered pan. Serve them in the morning with honey or with eggs and tea. The sorghum does not cancel the wheat.

Authentic touch. Cook it as a breakfast cake in a small pan, one side, until the surface is full of holes. Do not copy the Somali soup-and-ghee service onto this page, and do not turn it into the Yemeni foul breakfast.

How it is used

Djiboutian lahoh is breakfast. Honey, a smear of butter, or a boiled egg are the usual company, with tea. It is not the bread under a large shared stew. Butter and egg add dairy and egg at the table.

Field notes

  • In Djibouti, lahoh is a breakfast cake, cooked small and eaten early.
  • It is related to Somali canjeero and Yemeni lahoh and written here as its own bread.
  • Sorghum in the batter adds grain flavor. The wheat still means this is not gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups wheat flour (about 190 g)
  • 1/2 cup sorghum flour
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 2 cups warm water
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours with the sugar, yeast, and warm water into a batter like cream. Do not add the salt yet.
  2. Cover the bowl overnight. In the morning it should smell faintly sour and look bubbly. This is the Djibouti breakfast batter, not a stew bread.
  3. Stir in the salt. Thin with water if it no longer pours.
  4. Heat a small pan, wipe it with a drop of oil, and pour a round that covers the bottom.
  5. Cover and cook on one side until the holes set and the top is matte. Keep the cakes warm for breakfast.

This batter contains wheat, even with sorghum in the bowl. Honey or egg at the table adds those allergens only if you use them.

Refrigerate leftover lahoh and reheat it covered. The batter should be cooked once it has soured, not left for a week. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • More sorghum makes a grainier cake and still contains wheat in this recipe.
  • A saved spoon of batter can start the next morning.
  • Black sesame on the oiled pan is a finish some cooks like. Sesame would add that allergen.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Honey
  • A boiled egg
  • Cardamom tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Netherlands · No chili heat · 12 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Netherlands · Western Europe

Drie-in-de-pan

Three small Dutch pancakes in the pan, with apple in the batter.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 small pancakes

History

Drie-in-de-pan means three in the pan. The pancakes are small and thick enough that three fit in an ordinary frying pan. Apple or currants are the usual fruit. They are a home breakfast and a children's pancake, not the restaurant-sized pannenkoek and not poffertjes. This recipe uses diced apple and a little cinnamon.

Cultural background

The name is the method: do not crowd more than the pan can turn easily, and do not pour one giant cake. They are eaten with sugar or syrup.

Why it tastes this way

Apple softens and sweetens the crumb from the inside. The outside browns in butter. A thick batter gives a soft center, so they taste more like a small cake than a crepe. Cinnamon should stay light. The apple is the flavor.

How it is made

A thick flour, egg, and milk batter is mixed with diced apple and cooked three at a time in butter.

Authentic touch. Fit three in the pan, not a crowd of tiny drops and not one large pancake. Press a few apple pieces into each spoonful so every cake has fruit.

How it is used

Warm, with sugar or syrup, at breakfast or as a snack.

Field notes

  • The name means three in the pan, and that is how they are cooked.
  • Apple or currants are the usual fruit.
  • They are smaller and thicker than a plate-sized pannenkoek.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1 large egg
  • 200 ml milk
  • 1 apple, finely diced
  • 1/4 tsp ground cinnamon
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, salt, and sugar. Beat in the egg and milk. Fold in the apple and cinnamon.
  2. Melt butter in a frying pan over medium heat.
  3. Cook three spoonfuls at a time until bubbles set on top, then turn and cook until the apple inside is soft.
  4. Serve warm with sugar or syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Currants instead of apple
  • A strip of bacon beside them, which makes the meal a meat plate

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Dessert
  • Sugar
  • Dark syrup
  • A spoon of applesauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 1 large oven pancake

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Dutch baby

Eggy, custardy in the center, and crisp at the lifted rim, with browned butter.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 1 large oven pancake

History

The Dutch baby is an American oven pancake whose name is usually explained as Deutsch, meaning German. It is not a cake from the Netherlands, and it is not a Pennsylvania Dutch recipe. Seattle restaurants are often credited with popularizing it in the early twentieth century, and that popularizer story is the usual account.

Cultural background

The batter is closer to a popover than to a stack of griddle cakes: eggs, milk, and flour baked in a hot buttered skillet. It puffs in the oven and settles as it cools. Seattle is the place on this page.

Why it tastes this way

Steam from the wet batter, trapped by eggs, throws the edges up the sides of a hot pan. Butter browns on the skillet and flavors the crust. The center stays softer because it is thicker. Lemon and sugar at the table cut the richness.

How it is made

Baked in a preheated buttered skillet in a hot oven.

Authentic touch. Bake it in a preheated skillet. A cold pan will not lift the rim. Do not flip it on a griddle and expect the same shape.

How it is used

Straight from the skillet, often with lemon and powdered sugar.

Field notes

  • The usual explanation of the name is Deutsch, meaning German, not a cake from the Netherlands.
  • Seattle restaurants are often credited with popularizing it in the early twentieth century, and that story is the usual account.

Ingredients

  • 3 large eggs
  • 2/3 cup whole milk
  • 2/3 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons unsalted butter

Steps

  1. Heat the oven to 425°F with a 10-inch ovenproof skillet inside.
  2. Whisk the eggs, milk, flour, sugar, and salt until smooth. A few small lumps will bake out.
  3. Add the butter to the hot skillet and return it to the oven until the butter melts and browns slightly.
  4. Pour the batter into the hot butter. Bake about 18 minutes, until the edges are high and browned and the center is just set.
  5. Do not open the oven early or the puff drops.
  6. Serve at once with lemon and powdered sugar. It settles as it cools.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory version leaves out the sugar and adds grated cheese and herbs, which is a different plate.
  • Individual pans make smaller babies and finish a few minutes sooner.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Lemon wedges
  • Powdered sugar
  • Fruit preserves
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Netherlands · No chili heat · 4 large pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Netherlands · Western Europe

Dutch pannenkoeken

Large, thin Dutch pancakes, plate-sized, with syrup, apple, or bacon.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 large pancakes

History

A Dutch pannenkoek is a large thin pancake, often the size of the plate. Pannenkoeken restaurants cook them to order, sweet or savory. Stroop, a dark Dutch syrup, and powdered sugar are the usual sweet finish. Bacon and cheese are the usual savory ones. This page writes a plain batter and a syrup finish, and names bacon as a variation so the base recipe stays vegetarian. Poffertjes, the small yeast cakes, are a different pan and a different page.

Cultural background

They are family restaurant food and home food. Children and adults share the same batter. The pancake is dinner as often as it is dessert, which is why both uses are tagged.

Why it tastes this way

The batter is a little thicker than a French crepe, so the crumb is soft with a lacier edge. Butter browns it. Syrup adds bitterness and molasses-like depth that maple syrup does not quite copy. A pinch of salt in a sweet pancake keeps it from tasting flat.

How it is made

A batter of flour, egg, and milk is cooked as one wide pancake, then finished with syrup or sugar.

Authentic touch. Cook one large pancake per person rather than a stack of small ones. Spread inclusions such as apple on the raw batter in the pan, instead of stirring a heavy handful into the bowl.

How it is used

Flat on a plate, sweet with stroop or sugar, or savory if you add bacon and cheese. Cut into wedges if you share.

Field notes

  • A pannenkoek is often cooked one to a plate.
  • Poffertjes are the small dimpled cakes, not this pancake.
  • Stroop, a dark syrup, is the usual sweet topping.

Ingredients

  • 200 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 500 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Dark syrup, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, eggs, milk, and salt into a smooth batter, a little thicker than a crepe batter.
  2. Heat a wide pan and melt a little butter.
  3. Pour enough batter to cover the pan in a thick crepe layer. Cook until the top is set and the underside is brown, then turn and finish.
  4. Slide onto a plate and serve with syrup. Repeat for each person.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bacon and cheese, which adds meat and is no longer vegetarian
  • Apple pieces pressed into the uncooked top

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Dutch stroop or another dark syrup
  • Powdered sugar
  • Apple slices cooked on the batter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sri Lanka · No chili heat · 4 egg hoppers

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sri Lanka · South Asia

Egg hoppers

A coconut rice hopper with a soft egg set in the center, crisp at the rim.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 egg hoppers

History

An egg hopper is a Sri Lankan hopper with an egg broken into the thick center so the white sets against the rice and the yolk stays soft. The batter is the same fermented rice and coconut milk used for plain hoppers. The egg is the addition that gives this page its own name. It is a breakfast from hopper stands in Colombo and elsewhere on the island. It is not a string hopper, and it is not a wheat omelet.

Cultural background

The cook pours and swirls the batter, then cracks the egg at once so it cooks under the lid with the rice. A firm yolk is a missed hopper. Chile sambol is served beside it, not baked inside, in the usual plate.

Why it tastes this way

The rice bowl is lightly sour and sweet from coconut. The egg adds richness the plain hopper does not have, and a soft yolk sauces the spongy center. The crisp rim stays free of egg if the crack is careful, so you still get the cracker edge. Salt and the heat of a sambol finish the plate.

How it is made

Swirl fermented rice batter in a hopper pan, crack an egg into the center, and cover until the white sets.

Authentic touch. Crack the egg into the center puddle, not onto the thin wall. An egg on the lace burns and welds the hopper to the pan.

How it is used

Eat at once with a spicy onion sambol. The yolk should still move. Dried-fish sambols add a fish allergen on the side.

Field notes

  • The egg goes into the center of a rice hopper. It does not replace the rice batter.
  • String hoppers are pressed rice noodles, a different dish from this pan.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented rice and coconut-milk hopper batter
  • 4 eggs
  • Salt and pepper
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Use hopper batter that has fermented until bubbly, as on the plain hopper page.
  2. Heat and grease a hopper pan. Pour a ladle and swirl so the center remains deep.
  3. Immediately crack one egg into that center. Sprinkle a little salt and pepper.
  4. Cover and cook on medium-low until the rim is crisp, the egg white is set, and the yolk is still soft.
  5. Loosen the rim and slide the hopper out. Repeat with the remaining eggs.

Cook the egg white until it is set. People who need fully cooked yolks should leave the lid on longer.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of salt and pepper on the yolk
  • A firmer yolk if you cover the pan longer

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Onion sambol
  • Seeni sambol
  • Banana and a second plain hopper
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Arab Emirates · No chili heat · About 16 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Arab Emirates · Middle East

Emirati chebab

Small yeasted pancakes perfumed with saffron and cardamom, served with date syrup.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · About 16 small pancakes

History

Chebab are Emirati fermented pancakes, small and yeasted, often scented with saffron and cardamom. Date syrup, dibs, is the usual condiment, with butter or a soft cheese nearby. They are a home breakfast across the Emirates, not a restaurant invention from one hotel. The batter rests until it is bubbly, then it is cooked in little rounds. This batch is written from an Al Ain kitchen and eaten the same way in Abu Dhabi or Dubai.

Cultural background

Dates are everyday food, and dibs is the concentrated syrup made from them. Saffron is used by the pinch, steeped in warm milk, not by the handful. Chebab sit at breakfast beside eggs or cheese more often than at the end of a formal meal, though they are sweet enough for dessert.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast gives a mild tang and a soft rise. Saffron tastes floral and hay-like, and cardamom is camphorous and sweet. Milk and egg make the crumb tender. Date syrup is dark, caramelized, and less floral than honey, so the finish is toffee rather than blossom. The cakes are small, so the crust-to-center ratio stays high.

How it is made

Bloom yeast and saffron in warm milk. Whisk in flour, egg, sugar, cardamom, and salt. Rest until foamy. Cook small rounds on a buttered pan until both sides are golden. Serve with date syrup.

Authentic touch. Steep a pinch of saffron in warm milk before it joins the flour, and serve the cakes with date syrup. A large plain pancake with maple syrup is not chebab.

How it is used

Chebab are breakfast, and they work as a sweet snack or a simple dessert. Date syrup is poured or spooned. A salty white cheese on the side is a savory contrast and is still dairy, which this batter already contains.

Field notes

  • Saffron and cardamom are the usual scents in Emirati chebab.
  • Date syrup, not honey, is the condiment written here.
  • The cakes are small and yeasted. They are cooked after the batter has rested.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups wheat flour (about 190 g)
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 cup warm milk
  • 1 pinch saffron threads
  • 1 egg
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cardamom
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • Butter for the pan
  • 1/2 cup date syrup, to serve

Steps

  1. Stir the saffron into the warm milk and add the yeast and sugar. Let it stand for 5 minutes.
  2. Whisk in the egg, flour, cardamom, and salt until you have a thick batter. Cover it for 45 minutes, until bubbly.
  3. Heat a pan and grease it with butter. Drop small rounds, a generous spoon each.
  4. Cook until bubbles set on top, then flip and cook until the second side is golden.
  5. Serve warm with date syrup. The syrup is the condiment, not a glaze cooked into the batter.

The batter contains wheat, egg, and dairy. Saffron should be a pinch. Date syrup is a concentrated sweetener.

Refrigerate leftover chebab and reheat them in a covered pan. Keep date syrup covered at room temperature or in the refrigerator, apart from the cakes. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Rose water instead of saffron is a different perfume and not this batch.
  • A thicker batter makes a puffier cake. The traditional ones stay small.
  • Leaving out the egg will change the crumb. This recipe includes it.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Date syrup
  • A salty white cheese
  • Cardamom coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 crumpets

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

English crumpets

Ring-cooked yeast cakes, toasted so butter sinks into the holes.

1 hr 40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 crumpets

History

Crumpets are English yeast cakes cooked in rings. The ring holds a loose batter so the top stays wet and forms holes while the base sets. Pikelets, on the previous page, are the freeform cousin. A crumpet is meant to be toasted, not only eaten straight from the pan. Shop crumpets are the same idea baked to a schedule. This is a home batter.

Cultural background

They sit on a breakfast or tea table with butter. Cheese, jam, or a poached egg are all reasonable. The cake is a vehicle for something that melts.

Why it tastes this way

The inside stays soft and slightly tangy from yeast. Toasting makes a dry, browned rim so butter has somewhere to go. The holes are not decoration. They are the texture that separates a crumpet from a dropped scone.

How it is made

Yeast batter rises, baking soda is added, and the batter is poured into greased rings on a warm griddle. The cakes are cooled, then toasted.

Authentic touch. Grease the rings as well as the griddle. Cook the top until the bubbles have burst and the surface is set before you turn the cake. A wet turn makes a flat crumpet.

How it is used

Toasted on a fork or under a grill, with butter. They are a snack or breakfast, not a dessert course.

Field notes

  • The ring is what lets the batter stay deep enough to form holes.
  • Crumpets are toasted before they are eaten, even when just cooked.
  • Without rings, the same family of batter becomes pikelets.

Ingredients

  • 225 g strong white wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 200 ml warm milk
  • 150 ml warm water
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/2 tsp baking soda
  • Butter, for the rings, the griddle, and serving

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, yeast, sugar, milk, water, and salt into a smooth batter. Cover until foamy, about 1 hour.
  2. Stir the baking soda into a spoon of warm water, beat it in, and rest the batter 15 minutes. It should be pourable and bubbly.
  3. Grease crumpet rings and a griddle. Set the rings on medium-low heat and fill them halfway. Cook until the top is open with holes and dry to the touch.
  4. Remove the rings, turn the crumpets, and color the tops for a minute. Cool, then toast and butter.

Discard the batter if it smells spoiled rather than yeasty.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Wholemeal flour for part of the white flour
  • A cheese and chutney topping

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Butter
  • A slice of cheese, melted in the holes
  • Jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 12 pikelets

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

English pikelets

Small freeform yeast cakes with a holey top, toasted and buttered.

1 hr 30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pikelets

History

In England a pikelet is a small yeast griddle cake, close to a crumpet but cooked without a ring. The batter sits in a free puddle, so the cake stays flatter and wider than a ring-bound crumpet. The name is used differently in Australia, and that cake has its own page. This one is the Midlands and northern English pikelet.

Cultural background

Pikelets belong to tea tables and breakfasts. They are toasted, then buttered, so the butter runs into the holes. Some home recipes now use baking powder. This one keeps the yeast.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast and a loose batter make an open, damp top. The bottom browns against the griddle. Toasting dries the surface just enough for butter to melt in. The taste is mildly sour from the yeast, not sweet unless you add jam.

How it is made

A yeast batter of flour, milk, and water rises, then a pinch of baking soda is stirred in. Spoonfuls cook on a greased griddle until the top is set and honeycombed.

Authentic touch. Do not use crumpet rings for this page. Spoon the batter freely. If the top is not full of holes, the batter is too thick or the pan is too hot.

How it is used

Toasted and buttered, with jam or honey if you want them sweet. Honey adds a honey allergen and means the plate is not vegan, which this batter was not anyway.

Field notes

  • English pikelets are cooked without rings. Crumpets use rings.
  • Australian pikelets are a different cake and a different page.
  • The holes are for melted butter, which is why they are toasted.

Ingredients

  • 225 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 250 ml warm milk
  • 100 ml warm water
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/2 tsp baking soda
  • Butter, for the griddle and for serving

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, yeast, sugar, warm milk, water, egg, and salt into a batter like thick cream. Cover and leave until bubbly, about 1 hour.
  2. Dissolve the baking soda in a spoon of water and stir it in. The batter should look busy with bubbles. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Grease a griddle over medium heat. Spoon on small rounds and cook until the top is full of holes and no longer liquid.
  4. Turn and brown the other side briefly. Cool on a cloth. Toast later and butter them while hot.

The batter should smell yeasty. If it smells rotten, discard it.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A baking-powder pikelet, faster, with a different flavor
  • Cheese melted on a toasted pikelet

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Cold butter
  • Jam
  • Strong tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 thin pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

English Shrove pancakes

Thin egg pancakes, lemon and sugar, made to clear the kitchen before Lent.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 thin pancakes

History

These are the thin English pancakes of Shrove Tuesday, the day before Lent. The batter uses the eggs, milk, and fat that a strict Lenten fast would set aside. Shrove Tuesday is one day, not a week, and it is not the French crepe holiday. In England the usual finish is lemon juice and sugar. Butter, jam, or a savory filling exist, but lemon and sugar is the plate this page records.

Cultural background

Pancake races and church bells are local Shrove customs in some English towns. The cake itself is older than those events and does not depend on them. A home cook only needs a pan and a toss they can afford to miss.

Why it tastes this way

The batter is loose with egg and milk, so it sets in a thin sheet instead of a fluffy stack. The surface browns in butter. Lemon cuts the fat, and sugar stays gritty for a moment on the hot cake. There is no baking powder, so the flavor is egg, milk, and browned butter rather than a soda tang.

How it is made

Whisk a thin batter of flour, egg, and milk. Cook one ladle at a time in a hot buttered pan, turning once, until both sides are spotted brown.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter for half an hour if you can. The first pancake is the test piece. Toss it or turn it with a spatula, then eat it while the next one cooks.

How it is used

On Shrove Tuesday, folded or rolled with lemon and sugar. They are a feast-day pudding more than a tall breakfast stack.

Field notes

  • Shrove Tuesday is the single day before Lent, not a butter week.
  • The first pancake is traditionally the cook's test, and it is still eaten.
  • Lemon and sugar, not syrup, is the usual English finish.

Ingredients

  • 100 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 300 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan and for serving
  • 2 lemons, cut into wedges
  • 3 tbsp sugar

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour and salt. Beat in the eggs, then the milk, until the batter is as thin as light cream. Rest it for 20 minutes if you have the time.
  2. Heat a frying pan over medium-high heat and grease it with a little butter.
  3. Pour in a thin layer of batter and swirl. Cook until the top looks dry and the underside is golden, about 1 minute. Turn and cook the second side for about 30 seconds.
  4. Fold or roll onto a warm plate. Add a squeeze of lemon and a spoon of sugar. Repeat, buttering the pan lightly between pancakes.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Orange juice instead of lemon
  • A savory ham and cheese fold, which is a later meal and not the Shrove plate

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Lemon wedges and sugar
  • A little jam, if lemon is not on the table
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Estonia · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Estonia · Eastern Europe

Estonian kohupiim pancakes

Small fried cakes of Estonian kohupiim, a curd cheese, with jam.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Kohupiim is Estonia's curd cheese, an acid-set fresh cheese. These pancakes fry that cheese with egg, a little flour, and sugar. They sit near Russian syrniki on the map and they are not the same page. This is the Estonian breakfast or dessert cake, served with jam or sour cream. Flour means they are not gluten-free. They are vegetarian and not vegan.

Cultural background

Kohupiim is used in pastries and eaten plain. Frying it into small cakes is a home method, close to other curd cakes of the region without being a copy of one city's recipe. Jam is the usual sweet note.

Why it tastes this way

The cheese is tangy and slightly grainy if you do not mash it to glue. Sugar rounds it. A thin crust from flour and butter contrasts with the soft center. Jam adds fruit acid. They taste fresher than a cheesecake and richer than a flour pancake.

How it is made

Kohupiim is mashed with egg, sugar, and flour, shaped into small cakes, and fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Use a dry curd. If you only have cottage cheese, drain it thoroughly and mash it. Call it a stand-in, not kohupiim. Serve jam.

How it is used

Warm, at breakfast or as a sweet snack, with jam or sour cream.

Field notes

  • Kohupiim is Estonian curd cheese.
  • These are fried cheese cakes, related to syrniki and written as their own recipe.
  • Jam or sour cream is the serving.

Ingredients

  • 400 g kohupiim or well-drained farmer cheese
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 3 tbsp plain wheat flour
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for frying
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Mash the cheese with the egg, sugar, salt, and flour until it holds together.
  2. Shape eight small cakes.
  3. Fry in butter over medium heat until both sides are golden and the center is hot.
  4. Serve with jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Raisins in the cheese
  • Russian syrniki, the neighboring curd cake

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Jam
  • Sour cream
  • A spoon of honey, which adds a honey allergen
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Ethiopia · No chili heat · About 8 small rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ethiopia · East Africa

Ethiopian kita

Mild, soft teff flatbread with a light toasted face and no sour ferment.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · About 8 small rounds

History

Kita is a quick unfermented flatbread, cooked the day it is mixed. It is not injera. Injera needs days of souring and is cooked on one side. Kita is mixed, rested only long enough for the flour to drink the water, and cooked on a skillet. Torn kita becomes chechebsa, tossed with spiced butter and berbere, or kita firfir, soaked in a spiced sauce. This recipe uses teff and stays unfermented.

Cultural background

Kita is a breakfast and a way to use a hot pan when there is no sour batter waiting. In home kitchens it is torn by hand. The bread itself is plain. The chile and the spiced butter arrive when it is turned into chechebsa or firfir.

Why it tastes this way

Because the batter is unfermented, kita tastes of toasted teff rather than of lactic sourness. There are no ayen. The crumb is closer to a soft griddled flatbread than to a sponge. If it sits for days it will start to become something else, which is why this method cooks it the same morning.

How it is made

Stir teff flour, water, and salt into a thick batter and rest it for about ten minutes so the flour hydrates. Do not ferment it. Cook small rounds on a moderate skillet, flipping once, until both faces are set and lightly spotted. Tear the bread while it is warm if you are making chechebsa or kita firfir.

Authentic touch. Say it plainly while you cook: this dough is unfermented. Tear the finished bread for chechebsa or kita firfir instead of leaving it as a neat stack. A fermented sour loaf is injera, and it does not belong on this page.

How it is used

Plain kita is breakfast with tea. Torn and mixed with niter kibbeh, the spiced clarified butter, and berbere, it becomes chechebsa. Torn into a berbere sauce, it becomes kita firfir. Those finishes add dairy and chile. They are not in the bread.

Field notes

  • Kita is unfermented. Leaving the batter for days would turn the method into a different bread.
  • Chechebsa is torn kita tossed with spiced butter and berbere.
  • Kita firfir is the torn bread soaked in a spiced sauce rather than folded.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups teff flour (about 210 g)
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 teaspoon neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir the teff flour, water, and salt into a thick batter with no dry pockets. This dough is unfermented. Do not add yeast or sourdough.
  2. Rest it for 10 minutes so the flour hydrates. Do not leave it overnight.
  3. Heat a skillet over medium heat and wipe it with the oil. Spread a small ladle of batter into a round about the width of a saucer.
  4. Cook until the underside is set and spotted, then flip once and cook the second side. Kita is cooked on both sides, unlike injera.
  5. Stack the rounds under a cloth. Tear them for chechebsa or kita firfir, or eat them plain while warm.

The bread is gluten-free only if the teff flour is free of wheat. Berbere used later for chechebsa is a chile blend and can be very hot.

Eat kita the day it is cooked. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Do not ferment leftover batter and call it this recipe. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Wheat kita is common and must be tagged as wheat. It is not gluten-free. This written recipe does not use it.
  • Chechebsa uses spiced butter, so that version is not vegan or dairy-free.
  • A drier dough makes a firmer bread for tearing. A looser batter makes a softer round.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Tea
  • Niter kibbeh and berbere, for chechebsa
  • A berbere sauce, for kita firfir
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Lithuania · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Lithuania · Eastern Europe

Farmer cheese latkes

The older Ashkenazi Hanukkah pancake, made of farmer cheese rather than potato.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

These are cheese latkes, the older Ashkenazi Hanukkah dairy pancake. The customary teaching connects Hanukkah dairy foods with Judith, who served cheese to an enemy general. Potato latkes became common later, after potatoes spread in Eastern Europe in the 19th century. This page is written from a Lithuanian Jewish table and does not claim that only Lithuania fried cheese. The potato page is pinned to Poland. Cheese, egg, and flour make a cake that is vegetarian and not vegan, not dairy-free, and not gluten-free.

Cultural background

They are Hanukkah food and a breakfast cake when cheese is in the house. Sweet and lightly salty versions both exist. This one is slightly sweet, with sour cream or a little jam.

Why it tastes this way

Farmer cheese is tangy and rich. Sugar is restrained so it still tastes like cheese. Egg sets the center. Flour makes a thin crust in the butter. They brown slowly. A high flame dries them out and loses the creamy middle that distinguishes them from a potato latke.

How it is made

Farmer cheese is mixed with egg, sugar, salt, and flour, shaped into small cakes, and fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Use a dry farmer cheese or well-drained tvorog. This is a dairy Hanukkah dish, so do not serve it as the potato latke and do not call it gluten-free.

How it is used

Warm at a dairy Hanukkah meal, with sour cream or jam. They are vegetarian.

Field notes

  • Cheese pancakes are the older Ashkenazi Hanukkah custom.
  • The Judith story is the customary explanation for dairy at that holiday.
  • Potato latkes came after potatoes were common, and they have their own page.

Ingredients

  • 400 g farmer cheese or drained tvorog
  • 2 large eggs
  • 3 tbsp plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for frying
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Mash the cheese with the eggs, flour, sugar, and salt until it holds a shape.
  2. Form eight small cakes.
  3. Fry in butter over medium heat until both sides are golden and the center is hot, not dry.
  4. Serve with sour cream or jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory cheese latke with salt and dill and no sugar
  • Potato latkes, the later Hanukkah cake

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Breakfast
  • Sour cream
  • Jam
  • A dusting of sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Fiji · No chili heat · About 10 fried pieces

Flying Pancakes of the World

Fiji · Oceania

Fijian babakau

Soft yeasted dough, fried, with a mild sweetness and a pale, airy center.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 5 · About 10 fried pieces

History

Babakau is Fijian fried dough for tea. A yeasted wheat dough is rested, pulled into pieces, and fried. It is shop food and home food, eaten with tea in Suva and far beyond it. Wheat flour, yeast, and tea mark it as a food from the period when those goods were already in Fijian kitchens. It is not an ancient dish, and this page does not call it one. The method is frying, so the base here is a fritter even though the dough is yeasted.

Cultural background

Tea in Fiji can mean a meal, not only a drink, and babakau belongs on that table. Homes fry a batch for guests. Shops sell them through the day. The dough is plain. Flavor comes from the fry and from whatever is served beside it, often butter or a sweet spread.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast gives a light, slightly fermented smell and a soft hole in the center. Frying browns only the outside, so the inside tastes of wheat and a little sugar rather than of crust. Salt keeps the sweetness in check. There is no banana and no coconut in this batch, which sets it apart from other island fried cakes that use fruit.

How it is made

Mix flour, yeast, sugar, salt, and water into a soft dough and let it rise until puffy. Divide it, flatten the pieces lightly, and fry them in oil until golden and cooked through. Drain and serve with tea.

Authentic touch. Fry a yeasted dough and serve it for tea. Do not describe babakau as ancient. A baking-powder dough will fry, and it will not have the same slow rise this recipe uses.

How it is used

Babakau is a snack and a breakfast or tea bread. It is eaten plain, with butter, or with a sweet spread. Butter adds dairy. The dough written here does not contain it. It is not a ceremonial loaf.

Field notes

  • Babakau is fried dough for tea in Fiji.
  • The dough is yeasted, and the cooking method is frying.
  • It belongs to the era of wheat flour and tea. It is not an ancient dish.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • Oil for frying

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, yeast, sugar, and salt. Add the warm water and mix to a soft dough. Knead until smooth.
  2. Cover and let it rise until puffy, about 45 to 60 minutes. This is a yeasted dough, and it is not an ancient recipe.
  3. Divide the dough into 10 pieces and flatten each slightly.
  4. Heat the oil. Fry the pieces, turning once, until they are golden and cooked through. Babakau is fried dough for tea.
  5. Drain and serve warm.

The dough contains wheat. Hot oil should not be left alone. There is no dairy in the dough unless you add butter at the table.

Babakau stales quickly. Eat it the day it is fried. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat them in a hot oven so the outside crisps. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A round, bun-like shape and a flatter disk are both sold. This recipe flattens the pieces slightly.
  • Milk in the dough is a richer fry and adds dairy.
  • A pinch of cinnamon is a home choice and not required.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • Tea
  • Butter, which adds dairy
  • A little sugar or jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Philippines · No chili heat · 8 hotcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Philippines · Southeast Asia

Filipino hotcakes

Soft wheat breakfast pancakes, browned in margarine and eaten with sugar or condensed milk.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 hotcakes

History

Filipino hotcakes are a 20th century breakfast. American colonial influence brought wheat pancakes and canned milk into Philippine kitchens, and the cakes stayed after that period as a local morning food. Homes and small cafes cook them in margarine and serve them with sugar or sweetened condensed milk more often than with maple syrup. They sit beside rice meals. They did not replace rice. This page tells that history directly. The batter is wheat, egg, and milk, a modern breakfast cake, not a precolonial rice cake.

Cultural background

A Manila breakfast can include hotcakes, eggs, and garlic rice on the same table. The hotcake is the wheat part of that spread. Margarine is the common frying fat. Condensed milk is both an ingredient in some batters and a topping. This recipe uses milk in the batter and condensed milk at the table.

Why it tastes this way

The flavor is a soft, sweet wheat crumb with browning from the pan and a salty dairy note from margarine. Egg and milk make it tender. Sugar on top or condensed milk makes it sweeter than a butter-and-maple stack, with a cooked-milk taste. There is no ferment and no coconut. The taste is a 20th century breakfast that became ordinary in Filipino homes.

How it is made

Mix a simple baking-powder batter and cook thick breakfast pancakes in margarine, finished with sugar or condensed milk.

Authentic touch. Fry them in margarine, not a dry nonstick pan, if you want the breakfast-shop taste. Serve sugar or condensed milk while the cakes are hot enough to melt it.

How it is used

Stack two or three on a plate for breakfast. Offer sugar and condensed milk. Coffee or hot chocolate can sit beside them.

Field notes

  • These hotcakes became a Philippine breakfast in the 20th century, through American colonial influence, and then became local.
  • Margarine, sugar, and condensed milk are the usual fats and sweets, more than maple syrup.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup milk
  • 2 tablespoons margarine, melted, plus more for the pan
  • A pinch of salt
  • Sweetened condensed milk or extra sugar to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, baking powder, sugar, and salt.
  2. Stir in egg, milk, and melted margarine until just combined.
  3. Heat a pan and add a little margarine.
  4. Pour small rounds and cook until bubbles set on top and the undersides are brown. Flip and finish.
  5. Serve hot with sugar or condensed milk.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A ripe banana sliced into the batter
  • A little more margarine for a crisper edge

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • White sugar
  • Sweetened condensed milk
  • Fried eggs and garlic rice on the same breakfast table
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Finland · No chili heat · 10 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Finland · Northern Europe

Finnish lettu

Thin Finnish pancakes, served with jam, sugar, or whipped cream.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 pancakes

History

Lettu, plural letut, is the thin Finnish pancake. Ohukainen is a related name, often for a smaller cake. This page is the thin lettu cooked in a pan. It is everyday home food, served with jam, sugar, or whipped cream. The Åland oven pancake, made from porridge, is a different page. This batter is flour, egg, and milk, not a baked porridge.

Cultural background

Letut are a dessert and a weekend treat more than a ceremonial calendar food. A thin flexible cake is the goal. Families argue about jam versus sugar. Both are honest.

Why it tastes this way

They taste of egg, milk, and browned butter, with almost no spice. A thin sheet means more browned surface. Jam adds acid. Whipped cream adds richness the batter only hints at. If the batter is thick, you lose the lettu and gain a different pancake.

How it is made

A thin batter is rested and cooked like crepes, then served with jam or sugar.

Authentic touch. Cook them thin enough to roll. Ohukaiset, smaller and sometimes a little thicker, are the cousin. Do not bake this batter as an Åland pancake.

How it is used

Rolled or folded, with jam, sugar, or whipped cream, as dessert or a weekend breakfast.

Field notes

  • Lettu is the thin Finnish pancake. Ohukainen is a close name for a smaller cake.
  • The Åland pancake is a baked porridge cake and a different page.
  • Jam, sugar, or whipped cream are the usual finishes.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • 400 ml whole milk
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, salt, eggs, and milk into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in a buttered pan until both sides are spotted.
  3. Spread with jam or sprinkle with sugar.
  4. Roll or fold and serve, with whipped cream if you want it.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ohukaiset, cooked smaller
  • A savory filling of cheese, less typical than jam

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Jam
  • Whipped cream
  • Sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · 8 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Florida plantain pancakes

Sweet ripe plantain, a little cinnamon, and a fried edge.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 small cakes

History

These plantain pancakes are Cuban-American home cooking in Miami, where ripe plantains are mashed, bound with egg and a little flour, and fried in small cakes. The dish belongs to twentieth-century Cuban kitchens in the city. It is not a colonial-era recipe.

Cultural background

A plantain for this cake has a heavily spotted or black peel and tastes sweet. Green plantains are starchy and belong in savory fried chips, not in this batter. The small amount of wheat flour helps the cakes hold.

Why it tastes this way

Ripe plantain caramelizes in the pan and tastes like banana with a firmer, drier sweetness. Egg sets the mash. Cinnamon is a light perfume. Oil, not butter, is the frying fat in this recipe, so the edge tastes clean rather than milky.

How it is made

Mashed ripe plantain fried as small cakes in oil.

Authentic touch. Wait until the plantains are very ripe. A firm yellow plantain will taste starchy. Keep the cakes small so they fry through.

How it is used

Warm, as breakfast or a snack, with coffee.

Field notes

  • This is Cuban-American home cooking in Miami, not a colonial-era recipe.
  • The plantains need to be ripe, with black-spotted peels, or the cakes taste starchy.

Ingredients

  • 2 very ripe plantains
  • 2 large eggs
  • 3 tablespoons all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil

Steps

  1. Peel the plantains and mash them with a fork until mostly smooth. A few lumps are fine.
  2. Stir in the eggs, flour, sugar, cinnamon, and salt.
  3. Heat the oil in a skillet over medium heat.
  4. Drop about 1/4 cup of the mixture for each cake and flatten it slightly.
  5. Fry until the underside is brown, about 2 minutes, then flip and cook until the center is set.
  6. Drain briefly and serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the cinnamon for a plainer cake.
  • More flour makes them cakier and less like plantain.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Coffee
  • A squeeze of lime
  • Salt, for a less sweet bite
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · No chili heat · 8 tortillas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Flour tortillas

Soft, wheaty, and a little rich from fat, with browned spots and a flexible crumb.

40 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 tortillas

History

Flour tortillas belong to northern Mexico, especially Sonora, where wheat became a staple crop. The dough is wheat flour, fat, salt, and water, rolled thin and cooked on a comal. Corn tortillas are older. Wheat hotcakes are later still. This northern flatbread is its own thing: a pliable wheat wrap for machaca, beans, and burros, not a corn tortilla made with different flour.

Cultural background

In Sonora and neighboring northern states, the flour tortilla is daily bread. Home cooks roll them larger than the corn tortillas of the center and south. This version uses vegetable shortening so the recipe stays vegetarian. Many family recipes use lard instead.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat gluten, handled lightly, stretches into a soft sheet instead of a tough cracker. Fat shortens that gluten, which is why the tortilla bends without snapping while it is warm. The comal adds toasted spots. There is no egg and no baking powder in this dough, so the flavor stays plain and wheaty, a carrier for the filling.

How it is made

Cut shortening into flour and salt, add warm water, knead briefly, and rest. Roll thin rounds and cook them on a hot comal until they blister.

Authentic touch. Rest the dough at least 30 minutes before rolling. The flour hydrates, and the rounds relax instead of springing back. Cook them dry or with only a trace of fat. Stack them in a cloth at once.

How it is used

Use them warm as the wrap for beans, eggs, or stews, or fold one around cheese while it is still hot. They are a bread, not a dessert.

Field notes

  • Sonoran flour tortillas are often much larger than a central Mexican corn tortilla.
  • This dough uses vegetable shortening. Swap in lard and the tortilla is no longer vegetarian.
  • Corn cakes in Mexico are older than this wheat flatbread and older than wheat hotcakes.

Ingredients

  • 250 g wheat flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 40 g vegetable shortening
  • 150 ml warm water

Steps

  1. Stir the flour and salt. Rub in the shortening until the mixture looks like coarse crumbs.
  2. Add the warm water and knead just until a smooth, soft dough forms. Cover and rest for 30 minutes.
  3. Divide into 8 pieces. Roll each into a thin round on a floured board.
  4. Cook on a hot dry comal for about 30 to 45 seconds a side, until brown spots appear and the surface blisters.
  5. Stack the tortillas in a cloth so they steam and stay flexible.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lard in place of vegetable shortening is common in northern kitchens. That version contains meat and is not vegetarian.
  • A spoon of wheat flour more or less sets the size. Keep the dough soft, not wet.
  • Some cooks add a pinch of baking powder for a softer puff. This recipe leaves it out.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Mashed beans and a mild salsa
  • Scrambled eggs and roasted chile
  • Carne asada, on a plate that is no longer vegetarian
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Turkey · No chili heat · 2 large folded pastries

Flying Pancakes of the World

Turkey · Middle East

Gaziantep katmer

Crisp layered flatbread filled with pistachio and kaymak, which is clotted cream.

40 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 large folded pastries

History

Gaziantep katmer is a layered pastry of thin dough, pistachio, and kaymak, cooked on a griddle or a saj. Kaymak is clotted cream, the thick cream skimmed from heated milk. The city is known for its pistachios, and this breakfast pastry uses them crushed, not as a flavoring extract. It is a stuffed, folded flatbread, not a batter pancake. Bakers stretch the dough, fill it, and cook it to order in the morning.

Cultural background

Katmer is often a shared morning plate, cut into squares beside tea. The dough has to be thin or the cream turns heavy. Home cooks who cannot find kaymak sometimes use a thick cream, and the pastry is then a cousin rather than the Gaziantep version. This recipe asks for kaymak and pistachio.

Why it tastes this way

Pistachio is buttery and slightly bitter, and it stays green if you crush it rather than toast it to a powder. Kaymak tastes of cooked milk, richer and less sour than yogurt. Thin dough on a hot saj shatters at the edge and stays flexible where the cream is. Sugar is light. The pastry should not eat like a soaked baklava.

How it is made

Make a soft dough and rest it. Stretch it very thin on an oiled table, spread kaymak and crushed pistachio and a little sugar in the center, and fold it into a square. Cook on a hot saj or griddle until blistered and golden. Cut and serve warm.

Authentic touch. Use kaymak, and say what it is: clotted cream. Crush pistachios so they stay in pieces. Cook the folded bread on a griddle or saj. A poured pancake cannot hold this filling.

How it is used

Katmer is breakfast in Gaziantep and sweet enough to be dessert. Tea is the partner. It is not a stew bread. The filling is dairy and tree nut, so the plate is neither dairy-free nor nut-free.

Field notes

  • Kaymak is clotted cream, not a soft cheese and not butter.
  • Gaziantep katmer is cooked on a griddle or a saj, a convex metal dome.
  • Pistachio in the filling is a tree nut.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups wheat flour (about 190 g)
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 2 tablespoons oil, plus more for stretching
  • 150 g kaymak, clotted cream
  • 3/4 cup crushed pistachios
  • 1 tablespoon sugar

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, salt, water, and oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth and rest it for 20 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough in half. Oil the table and stretch one piece until it is thin enough to see the table through the center.
  3. Spread kaymak over the center. Kaymak is clotted cream. Scatter pistachios and sugar over the cream. The pistachios are a tree nut.
  4. Fold the dough into a square that seals the filling. This is a layered griddle pastry, not a pourable batter.
  5. Cook on a hot saj or griddle, turning once, until both sides blister and turn golden. Cut into pieces and serve warm. Repeat with the second half.

Pistachios are a tree nut. Kaymak is a fresh dairy food and should be kept cold until you use it. The dough contains wheat.

Eat katmer the morning it is cooked. Refrigerate leftovers promptly because of the cream, and reheat them on a dry pan. The layers will be less crisp. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A thinner layer of kaymak keeps the pastry crisp.
  • Some shops add a little semolina sugar between the folds.
  • Without pistachio the pastry is a different, plainer katmer and would not carry the tree-nut tag.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Black tea
  • More crushed pistachio
  • A glass of cold water, because the cream is rich
  • Vegetarian

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Georgia peach pancakes

Juicy ripe peach against a tangy buttermilk crumb, sweet but not candied.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Georgia peach pancakes are a seasonal wheat cake made when ripe peaches are in the market. The state's commercial peach industry grew in the late nineteenth century. The pancake is a summer breakfast. Fort Valley sits in the peach-growing belt and is the map pin, not the birthplace of a plantation recipe.

Cultural background

Fresh peaches are wet. Dicing them small and folding them in at the end keeps the batter from turning pink and soggy. Frozen peaches, drained, are the off-season substitute.

Why it tastes this way

A ripe peach tastes floral and tart, and heat concentrates that juice at the edge of the piece. Buttermilk keeps the crumb from tasting only of sugar. Too much syrup hides the fruit, which is why the batter sugar stays low.

How it is made

Griddled buttermilk batter with fresh peach pieces folded in.

Authentic touch. Use ripe peaches in season and cut them yourself. A peach-flavored syrup on a plain pancake is a different dish.

How it is used

Straight off the griddle, with butter and the smallest pour of syrup.

Field notes

  • Georgia's commercial peach crop expanded in the late nineteenth century.
  • Fort Valley is a real center of that growing belt, which is why the map pin sits there.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup diced peeled ripe peaches
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Pat the diced peaches dry with a towel so they do not water down the batter.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  3. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter until just mixed. Fold in the peaches.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the bubbles set. Peek underneath so the peach juice does not scorch.
  6. Serve as soon as the center is set, with butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Drain thawed frozen peaches and pat them dry when fresh fruit is gone.
  • A pinch of nutmeg is enough spice. They do not need a heavy spice mix.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • A few extra peach slices
  • Hot tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Germany · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Germany · Western Europe

German Eierkuchen

Thin German egg pancakes. In Berlin, do not call them Pfannkuchen.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Eierkuchen means egg cake, and in much of Germany it is the clear word for a pancake. The naming trap is real: in Berlin and parts of eastern Germany, Pfannkuchen means a jam-filled doughnut. In much of the rest of the country, Pfannkuchen means this pancake, and the doughnut is a Berliner. Order carefully. The cake itself is a thin egg batter, served with sugar, applesauce, or a savory filling. This page uses sugar and lemon.

Cultural background

They are home food, cooked one after another and eaten as they come out of the pan. The Berlin doughnut that shares a confusing name is fried yeast dough, not this batter.

Why it tastes this way

Eggs make the sheet richer than a flour-and-water flatbread. Milk softens it. Butter browns the spots. Lemon and sugar brighten the fat, in the same family as an English Shrove pancake, with a German name and a German naming problem. The crumb is thin and flexible.

How it is made

Flour, eggs, and milk are whisked into a thin batter and cooked in butter, then finished with sugar and lemon.

Authentic touch. If you are in Berlin, ask for Eierkuchen when you want this pancake. Pfannkuchen may bring you a doughnut. Cook the batter thin.

How it is used

Folded, with sugar and lemon, as dessert or a weekend breakfast. Applesauce is another honest side.

Field notes

  • In Berlin, Pfannkuchen often means a jam doughnut. Eierkuchen means the pancake.
  • In much of western and southern Germany, Pfannkuchen means the pancake.
  • The doughnut and the pancake are different doughs that share a confusing name.

Ingredients

  • 125 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 300 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 1 tbsp sugar, plus more for serving
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • 1 lemon, cut into wedges

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, eggs, milk, salt, and sugar into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in a buttered pan until spotted on both sides.
  3. Sprinkle with sugar and add lemon.
  4. Fold and serve. If you are shopping in Berlin, remember the name trap.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory filling of mushrooms or cheese
  • Apple slices cooked on the batter

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Sugar and lemon
  • Applesauce
  • Cinnamon sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Gingerbread pancakes

Dark molasses, ginger, cinnamon, and clove in a soft wheat cake.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Gingerbread pancakes are a seasonal American breakfast that borrows molasses, ginger, and cinnamon from gingerbread. They are a twentieth-century table idea, a spiced wheat cake for cold mornings. Philadelphia is the map pin because molasses moved through that port, not because a single colonial manuscript holds this pancake. The crumb is darker and sharper than a plain buttermilk pancake.

Cultural background

Molasses and ground ginger are a trade sweetener and a pantry spice. The warmth is from ginger, cinnamon, and clove, not from chili.

Why it tastes this way

Molasses carries bitterness and a dark sweetness, and ginger makes the crumb feel warm. Clove and cinnamon are strong, so the amounts stay small. Buttermilk and soda keep the cake from turning into a dense gingerbread loaf. The edge browns fast because of the sugar in the molasses.

How it is made

Griddled molasses and ginger wheat batter.

Authentic touch. Cook them a step cooler than plain pancakes so the molasses does not scorch before the center sets. Taste the batter: the spice should be clear and not harsh.

How it is used

Hot, with butter. A little whipped cream turns them into dessert.

Field notes

  • These are a seasonal spiced breakfast, not a pancake copied from one colonial manuscript.
  • Molasses browns quickly, so the griddle should be a little cooler than for a plain cake.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 teaspoons ground ginger
  • 1 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 1/4 teaspoon ground cloves
  • 1 1/4 cups buttermilk
  • 1/4 cup molasses
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, baking soda, salt, ginger, cinnamon, and cloves.
  2. Whisk the buttermilk, molasses, egg, and melted butter. Stir into the flour until just combined.
  3. Heat a griddle over medium-low heat and butter it.
  4. Pour 1/4 cup cakes. They will darken quickly.
  5. Flip when the underside is deep brown and the top looks set. Cook until the center springs back.
  6. Serve with butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A finer grating of fresh ginger can replace half the ground ginger.
  • Leave out the clove if you want a milder cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Butter
  • Whipped cream
  • Applesauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 10 hotcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Hawaiian banana-macadamia hotcakes

Ripe banana and toasted macadamia in a rich diner hotcake.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 hotcakes

History

Banana-macadamia hotcakes are twentieth-century Hawaiian diner food, a wheat breakfast piled with banana and macadamia nuts. The macadamia was introduced to Hawaii from Australia and later became a local crop and a diner ingredient. This is not an ancient Hawaiian dish. Honolulu is the map pin.

Cultural background

The plate is a tourist-era and local-diner breakfast that uses ingredients that were available in twentieth-century Hawaii. Macadamia nuts are tree nuts. The batter is a wheat hotcake, not a taro or coconut bread.

Why it tastes this way

Macadamias are very oily, so they taste buttery when they are toasted and bitter if they burn. Banana adds perfume and moisture. A richer batter, with milk and butter, matches the nut. The combination is sweet and rich, which is why the cakes are small.

How it is made

Griddled wheat hotcakes with banana and toasted macadamia.

Authentic touch. Toast the macadamias and chop them. Use a ripe banana. Do not describe the plate as ancient.

How it is used

A short stack with butter, banana slices, and more nuts.

Field notes

  • Macadamia trees were introduced to Hawaii. They are not an ancient Hawaiian crop.
  • The banana-macadamia hotcake is twentieth-century diner food.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup chopped roasted macadamia nuts
  • 1 ripe banana, mashed, plus 1 banana, sliced
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups whole milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. If the macadamias are raw, toast them in a dry skillet until pale gold, then chop them. If they are already roasted, chop them.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, and salt.
  3. Stir in the mashed banana, milk, egg, and melted butter. Fold in half of the nuts.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium-low heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes and lay a banana slice and a few nuts on each.
  5. Flip carefully when the underside is golden. The nuts burn if the pan is too hot.
  6. Serve with butter, the remaining banana, and the remaining nuts.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave the nuts out only if you are cooking for someone who cannot eat tree nuts, and use a clean pan.
  • Coconut flakes are a different topping and are not required here.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Banana slices
  • More chopped macadamias
  • Vegetarian

Open the journal page

Honduras · No chili heat · 6 baleadas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Honduras · Mexico & Central America

Honduran baleadas

A soft griddled wheat tortilla folded over refried beans and crumbled cheese.

35 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 baleadas

History

Baleadas are a north-coast Honduran meal, especially around La Ceiba and San Pedro Sula. A large wheat tortilla is griddled, spread with refried beans and cheese, and folded. The tortilla is the bread, cooked on a comal, not fried. Corn tortillas are older in the region than this wheat wrap, and older than wheat hotcakes. The baleada became the everyday breakfast and late-night plate of the coast. This recipe keeps it to beans and cheese.

Cultural background

Stands sell baleadas simples, just beans and cheese, and baleadas especiales with egg, avocado, or crema added. The simple one is the recipe here. It is breakfast, lunch, and a snack after a dance, depending on the hour.

Why it tastes this way

The wheat tortilla tastes soft and slightly toasted, with enough fat in the dough to bend without cracking. Refried red beans are salty and earthy. Fresh cheese crumbles rather than stretching, so the bite stays milky and dry against the beans. There is no chile in the written filling, which is why a plain baleada is gentle. Hot sauce is a choice at the table.

How it is made

Make a soft wheat dough with oil, rest it, and griddle large thin rounds. Mash beans with onion, season them, and fold each tortilla over beans and crumbled cheese.

Authentic touch. Griddle the tortilla until it puffs in spots and is still flexible. A crisp tortilla will not fold. Spread the beans while the bread is hot so the cheese softens. Fold once, like a taco, not a tight roll.

How it is used

Eat them hot, folded, for breakfast or a street meal. Two make a plate. Avocado on top is common and does not change the allergens.

Field notes

  • The tortilla for a baleada is griddled wheat bread, then folded. It is not a corn pupusa.
  • The simple street version is beans and cheese. Eggs and meat are additions.
  • Corn cakes in this region are older than the wheat tortilla used here.

Ingredients

  • 250 g wheat flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt, plus more for the beans
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil
  • 120 ml warm water
  • 300 g cooked red beans
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 100 g crumbled fresh white cheese

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, 1/2 teaspoon salt, oil, and warm water into a soft dough. Cover and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Fry the onion in a spoon of oil, add the beans, and mash them with a little bean broth until spreadable. Salt to taste.
  3. Divide the dough into 6 pieces and roll thin rounds.
  4. Cook each round on a dry or lightly oiled comal until it blisters and browns in spots, under a minute a side. Keep them soft.
  5. Spread hot beans and cheese on one half and fold. Serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A baleada especial adds egg, avocado, and crema. The egg adds an allergen. The crema is more dairy.
  • A spoon of hot sauce raises the heat above none.
  • Lard in the tortilla dough adds meat and is not vegetarian. This dough uses oil.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Honduran sour cream, which is still dairy
  • Sliced avocado
  • A fried egg, which adds the egg allergen
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Hudson Valley buckwheat cakes

Sour, earthy buckwheat with a molasses edge and a crisp griddle face.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 cakes

History

Dutch settlers in the Hudson Valley cooked buckwheat cakes, and the grain fits a cool climate and a short season. The same grain shows up in other cold regions for that reason. Nineteenth-century New York breakfasts often included a yeasted buckwheat batter mixed with some wheat flour. These cakes are that Dutch-American breakfast, pinned at Kingston.

Cultural background

The overnight rise is the leavening. A pinch of baking soda in the morning steadies the sour batter and helps it brown. This recipe uses water rather than milk.

Why it tastes this way

Time, not a large dose of baking powder, makes the sour taste. Buckwheat carries an earthy flavor that wheat softens just enough to hold the cake. Molasses feeds the yeast and leaves a dark note. The soda in the morning takes the sharpest edge off.

How it is made

Yeasted overnight buckwheat batter cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Let the batter ferment overnight. A same-morning baking-powder cake can use these flours, but it will not taste like the old Hudson Valley breakfast.

How it is used

Hot, often with butter and maple syrup at a modern table. The batter itself has no dairy.

Field notes

  • Buckwheat suited the short seasons of the Hudson Valley, which is why Dutch-American cooks relied on it.
  • The overnight ferment is the rise. Do not count those hours as hands-on prep.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup buckwheat flour
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 tablespoon molasses
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups warm water
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil, for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir both flours, the yeast, molasses, salt, and warm water until smooth.
  2. Cover the bowl and leave it at room temperature overnight, about 8 to 12 hours, until the batter is bubbly and smells sour. That wait is not part of the active prep time.
  3. Stir in the baking soda just before cooking.
  4. Film a griddle with oil over medium heat.
  5. Pour small cakes. Cook until the top is full of holes and the underside is brown, then flip and finish.
  6. Serve hot. Butter and maple syrup are table additions.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of wheat flour more makes them less fragile.
  • Skip the morning soda if you want the sourness louder.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Maple syrup
  • Butter, if you eat dairy
  • Smoked fish, for a savory plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Iceland · No chili heat · 12 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Iceland · Northern Europe

Icelandic lummur

Small thick Icelandic pancakes with rye, buttered and sugared.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 small pancakes

History

Lummur are small thick Icelandic pancakes, often made with some rye flour and served with butter and sugar. They are a different cake from the paper-thin pönnukökur. Rye and wheat together mean this recipe is not gluten-free. They are a home snack and breakfast, cooked in spoonfuls on a griddle.

Cultural background

Rye has a long place in Icelandic baking because wheat was not always the easy grain. A lumma is hearty for its size. Brown sugar or white sugar on butter is the serving.

Why it tastes this way

Rye tastes deeper and slightly sour next to white flour. Baking powder lifts the crumb so it is not a cracker. Butter on the hot cake melts into the holes. Sugar is the contrast. They should be thick enough that the center stays soft.

How it is made

Wheat flour, rye flour, baking powder, egg, and milk make a thick batter that is cooked in small rounds and buttered.

Authentic touch. Keep rye as part of the flour, not the whole of it, for this page. Cook them as small thick rounds. Butter and sugar are the topping, not a rolled jam filling.

How it is used

Warm, with butter and sugar, as a snack or breakfast.

Field notes

  • Lummur are small and thick. Pönnukökur are thin.
  • Rye in the batter is part of this version.
  • They are buttered and sugared, not usually rolled around a filling.

Ingredients

  • 100 g plain wheat flour
  • 50 g rye flour
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 250 ml whole milk
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan and serving

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours, the baking powder, sugar, and salt. Beat in the egg and milk until you have a thick batter.
  2. Cook small rounds in butter over medium heat until bubbles set, then turn.
  3. The center should spring back and not taste of raw rye.
  4. Butter them and sprinkle with sugar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • All wheat flour, which loses the rye note and is still not gluten-free
  • Pönnukökur, the thin rolled pancakes

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Butter
  • Sugar
  • A spoon of jam if sugar is not enough
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · 10 slapjacks

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Indian slapjack

Soft corn, milk, and egg, with only a little wheat to hold the cake.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 slapjacks

History

Amelia Simmons's American Cookery, printed in Hartford in 1796, gives a formula for Indian slapjack made with Indian meal, milk, eggs, a little wheat flour, and salt. In that book, Indian meal means cornmeal. The cake is an early printed American griddle bread. This page does not assign the recipe to a particular Native nation.

Cultural background

American Cookery is often cited as the first cookbook written by an American and printed in the United States. The proportions here are scaled down from the quart of milk and pint of Indian meal in that formula.

Why it tastes this way

Cornmeal is the main flavor, and milk and egg make it custardy instead of dry. The small amount of wheat flour helps the thin batter hold. Because there is little sugar, the toasted corn is easy to taste.

How it is made

A thin cornmeal batter cooked on a griddle, following the 1796 formula in a smaller batch.

Authentic touch. Keep the wheat flour to a few tablespoons, as Simmons's 'spoons of flour' suggests. A modern pancake mix with a handful of cornmeal is not this cake.

How it is used

Hot off the griddle, with butter or molasses if you want them richer than the printed batter.

Field notes

  • In Amelia Simmons's 1796 American Cookery, Indian meal means cornmeal.
  • The printed slapjack is milk, cornmeal, eggs, a little wheat flour, and salt.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup cornmeal
  • 2 cups whole milk
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 tablespoons all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon unsalted butter, for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the cornmeal, milk, eggs, flour, and salt into a thin batter.
  2. Rest 5 minutes so the meal swells slightly.
  3. Heat a griddle over medium heat and rub it with butter.
  4. Pour about 1/4 cup batter and spread it thin.
  5. Cook until the surface looks dry and the underside is spotted brown, then flip and cook briefly.
  6. Serve hot. The cakes are tender and best eaten at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Simmons allows frying in a dry pan or a pan rubbed with suet, lard, or butter. This batch uses butter.
  • More flour makes a sturdier modern pancake and leaves the 1796 balance behind.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Molasses
  • Maple syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free
