Ireland · No chili heat · 8 pan cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish boxty

Pan boxty: raw grated potato and mashed potato, fried as cakes.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pan cakes

History

Boxty is an Irish potato cake. There are three forms: pan boxty, loaf boxty, and dumpling boxty. This page is only the pan cake. The batter mixes raw grated potato with mashed potato and flour, then the cakes are fried. Potatoes reached Irish kitchens and became a staple. The cake is one of the ways a raw potato was cooked without waiting for a roast. Flour means this recipe is not gluten-free.

Cultural background

Pan boxty is breakfast and supper food. It is associated with the north and the midlands as much as with any single county. This page does not assign it to one village.

Why it tastes this way

Raw potato brings starch and a clean flavor. Mash brings softness. Flour binds the two. Frying browns the starch. If you rinse all the starch off the grated potato, the cake falls apart and tastes watery. Salt is the seasoning. A crisp edge and a soft center is the point.

How it is made

Equal parts raw grated potato and mashed potato are mixed with flour, salt, and a little milk, then fried as flat cakes in butter or oil.

Authentic touch. Grate the raw potato and keep the liquid that settles into starch. Pour off the water and put the starch back. That paste helps the cake hold.

How it is used

Hot, with butter, eggs, or bacon. The cake on this page is vegetarian. Bacon is a serving choice, not part of the batter.

Field notes

  • Pan, loaf, and dumpling boxty are three methods. This is the pan cake.
  • The mix is raw grated potato plus mashed potato, not mash alone.
  • The starch from the grated potato helps bind the cake.

Ingredients

  • 250 g raw potatoes, peeled
  • 250 g cold mashed potato
  • 100 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 60 ml milk
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for frying

Steps

  1. Grate the raw potatoes onto a clean cloth. Squeeze the liquid into a bowl. Let it sit so the starch settles, pour off the water, and keep the starch.
  2. Mix the squeezed potato, the starch, the mash, flour, salt, and enough milk to make a stiff batter.
  3. Heat the butter in a frying pan. Shape flat cakes and fry over medium heat until both sides are brown and the raw potato is cooked through, about 4 minutes a side.
  4. Rest them a minute and serve with butter or eggs.

The raw potato must cook through. Cut one cake open. The center should be soft and hot, not glassy.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Boxty loaf, baked as a bread, which is a different method
  • Boxty dumpling, boiled, which is also a different method

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Butter
  • Fried eggs
  • Bacon, if you want a meat breakfast
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Ireland · No chili heat · 10 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish oatmeal pancakes

Soft oatmeal pancakes with buttermilk, a breakfast cake from Irish kitchens.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 pancakes

History

Oatmeal pancakes in Ireland use the porridge grain in a softer, egg-and-buttermilk batter. They are not Scottish oatcakes, which are thin and dry, and they are not Staffordshire's yeasted shop cakes. Soaking the oatmeal first keeps the texture from turning gritty. Wheat flour in this batter means the cakes are not gluten-free.

Cultural background

They are a home breakfast. Oats were everyday food, and a pancake was one way to serve them with butter and honey or jam. Honey, if you add it, is its own allergen.

Why it tastes this way

Soaked oats stay tender and taste toasted once the pan browns them. Buttermilk gives a quiet tang. The cakes are thicker than a crepe and less fluffy than a baking-powder drop scone. Butter on top is the richness. Sugar is not required in the batter.

How it is made

Oatmeal soaks in buttermilk, then flour, an egg, salt, and a pinch of baking soda are stirred in. The batter is cooked as small thick pancakes.

Authentic touch. Soak the oatmeal in the buttermilk for at least 15 minutes. Skipping the soak leaves hard flecks in the crumb.

How it is used

At breakfast, with butter and jam. They are a morning cake.

Field notes

  • The oatmeal is soaked so the pancake is not gritty.
  • This is a soft breakfast pancake, not a crisp Scottish oatcake.
  • Wheat flour in the batter means it is not gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 100 g medium oatmeal
  • 250 ml buttermilk
  • 80 g plain wheat flour
  • 1/2 tsp baking soda
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • Butter, for the pan and for serving

Steps

  1. Stir the oatmeal into the buttermilk and leave it for 15 minutes.
  2. Whisk in the flour, baking soda, salt, and egg.
  3. Cook spoonfuls in a buttered pan over medium heat until the bubbles set, then turn and finish.
  4. Serve warm with butter and jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A handful of raisins
  • All oatmeal and no wheat flour, which is a different cake and would need checking before you call it gluten-free

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Jam
  • Honey, which adds a honey allergen
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Ireland · No chili heat · 8 farls

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish potato farls

Mashed-potato farls, cut in quarters and cooked on a griddle.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 farls

History

Potato farls are a mashed-potato bread cooked on a griddle and cut into quarters. Farl means a fourth. They are an Ulster breakfast bread eaten in Ireland, which is why this page is pinned to Ireland. The wheat soda farl, a different dough, is on the Ulster soda farl page under the United Kingdom. Potato farls use mash, flour, and fat. They do not use the raw potato that defines pan boxty.

Cultural background

They are weekend breakfast food beside eggs, bacon, or soda bread. The dough should be soft and the cakes thin enough to cook through on a dry griddle.

Why it tastes this way

Mashed potato makes a mild, stretchy crumb once it meets a hot griddle. Flour keeps the shape. Butter in the dough tastes nutty when the surface toasts. They are saltier and drier than a fried potato cake, because the pan is only lightly greased.

How it is made

Cold mash is mixed with butter, salt, and flour, pressed into rounds, quartered, and cooked on a hot griddle until spotted brown.

Authentic touch. Cut the round into four before you cook it. Do not grate in raw potato. If you do, you have started a boxty, and that recipe is the other page.

How it is used

At breakfast, with butter, eggs, or a fry. The farls themselves are vegetarian.

Field notes

  • A farl is a quarter of the round.
  • This cake is mashed potato. Boxty also uses raw potato.
  • The pin is Ireland. Ulster soda farls are the wheat cake on the United Kingdom page.

Ingredients

  • 500 g cold mashed potato
  • 40 g unsalted butter
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 100 g plain wheat flour, plus more for pressing

Steps

  1. Beat the mash with the butter and salt. Work in the flour until you have a soft dough that does not stick.
  2. Divide in two. Press each half into a round about 1 cm thick on a floured board and cut into quarters.
  3. Cook on a hot dry griddle until brown spots show, then turn and cook the other side.
  4. Serve warm with butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of chopped spring onion
  • Tattie scones, the Scottish thin triangle of the same family

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Butter
  • Eggs
  • Bacon or sausage on a meat plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Jamaica · No chili heat · 4 bammies

Flying Pancakes of the World

Jamaica · Caribbean

Jamaican bammy

A dense, mild cassava round, dry and toasted on the outside, then fried so the edge crisps.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 bammies

History

Bammy is a Jamaican cassava flatbread, long associated with St. Elizabeth parish and with roadside plates of fried fish. Cassava bread in the Caribbean is older than the wheat johnny cakes that came later. The root itself has to be processed before it is safe to eat. This recipe starts from commercial cassava flour, which is already processed, or you may pan-fry a packaged bammy. It does not teach you to peel, grate, and press raw cassava at home.

Cultural background

Cooks griddle the dough into thick rounds, and many then dip the cake in salted water or coconut milk and fry it to serve. The plain griddled round is the bread. The fry is the usual plate next to fish. This version leaves the fish off so the recipe stays vegan.

Why it tastes this way

Commercial cassava flour tastes clean, starchy, and slightly sweet, without the toasted corn of a tortilla or the gluten of wheat. Salt is almost the only seasoning in the dough. Frying after a brief soak gives a soft center and a crisp rim. If you stop after the dry griddle, the bammy is firmer and better for soaking up sauce.

How it is made

Mix commercial cassava flour with salt and water into a stiff dough. Flatten thick rounds, griddle them dry, dip briefly in salted water, and pan-fry.

Authentic touch. Press the rounds firmly so they do not crack at the edge, and cook them on a dry skillet until the surface looks dry before you even think about the fry. A wet center will not finish in the oil.

How it is used

Serve hot beside fried fish, callaloo, or a stew. The bammy is the bread on the plate. Packaged bammy can be dipped and fried the same way if you are not making the dough.

Field notes

  • Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This page uses commercial cassava flour and does not teach home processing.
  • Bammy is often fried after the first cook, which is why it shows up next to fried fish.
  • There is no wheat in this dough, so it is gluten-free if the flour was not milled with wheat.

Ingredients

  • 280 g commercial cassava flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt, plus more for the dipping water
  • 200 ml water
  • 250 ml water for a brief dip
  • Neutral oil for pan-frying

Steps

  1. Stir the commercial cassava flour, 1 teaspoon salt, and 200 ml water into a stiff dough. If it cracks, add a spoon of water.
  2. Divide into 4 pieces and press each into a round about 1 centimeter thick.
  3. Cook on a dry skillet over medium heat for 6 to 8 minutes a side, until the surface is dry and spotted.
  4. Dip each round for a few seconds in salted water, then drain.
  5. Pan-fry in a thin layer of oil until the outside is crisp. Serve hot.

Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This recipe uses commercial cassava flour, or a packaged bammy, and does not teach home processing of raw cassava.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Soak the griddled round in coconut milk instead of salted water before frying.
  • Eat it after the dry griddle, without the fry, if you want a firmer bread.
  • A packaged bammy, already cooked and sold in rounds, can be dipped and fried. Read the package if you need to confirm it is only cassava.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Escovitch or fried fish, a plate that is not vegan
  • Callaloo and salt fish, also not vegan
  • A pepper sauce on the side for people who want heat
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Japan · No chili heat · 6 thick hotcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Japanese hotcakes

Thick, soft cafe hotcakes with a browned top, butter, and syrup, taller than a thin crepe and gentler than a souffle cake.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 thick hotcakes

History

Japanese hotcakes are a 20th century cafe style. Kissaten, the coffee shops that spread through Japanese cities in that century, served thick wheat cakes with butter and syrup alongside coffee. They are not an ancient dish. Baking powder lifts them. They are related to American breakfast pancakes and became their own cafe portion: tall, neat, and eaten with a fork. Souffle hotcakes, a later and much thicker trend, are a separate page.

Cultural background

A kissaten hotcake is cooked covered, in a ring or as a deep dollop, so the side stays pale and the top dries. Butter and maple syrup, or a local sugar syrup, are the usual finish. The cake is a breakfast and a dessert depending on the hour, not a savory okonomiyaki.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat, egg, and milk taste familiar and sweet, with browning only on the surfaces that touch the pan. Baking powder opens a soft crumb that syrup sinks into. Butter adds a dairy aroma the batter already started. Because the cake is thick, the center stays custardy if you pull it early, which is the cafe texture. There is no ferment and no cabbage. The flavor is a 20th century coffee-shop sweet.

How it is made

Mix a baking-powder batter with egg and milk, cook thick rounds covered on low heat, and serve them with butter and syrup.

Authentic touch. Cook on low heat under a lid. A hot pan browns the bottom before the center sets. Give a thick cake several quiet minutes.

How it is used

Serve two or three to a plate while hot, with butter melting on top. Coffee is the kissaten partner.

Field notes

  • These are 20th century kissaten hotcakes, not an ancient dish.
  • They are thick cafe pancakes. The very tall souffle kind is a later page.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup milk
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter, plus more to serve
  • A pinch of salt
  • Syrup to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, baking powder, sugar, and salt. In another bowl mix egg, milk, and melted butter.
  2. Stir the wet ingredients into the dry until just combined. A few lumps are fine.
  3. Heat a pan on low. Butter it. Pour tall rounds, or pour into buttered rings.
  4. Cover and cook until the tops are full of bubbles and the bottoms are brown. Flip and cover again until the centers set.
  5. Serve with butter and syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A ring mold for straight sides
  • A little lemon zest in the batter

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Butter
  • Maple or sugar syrup
  • Coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 8 jowar dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Jowar dosa

A pale sorghum dosa, mildly sweet and peppery, with a dry crisp edge.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 jowar dosas

History

Jowar dosa is a sorghum griddle cake from the dry-grain belts of Karnataka and Maharashtra, where jowar is a daily flour. This recipe ferments jowar flour with a spoon of rice flour and no wheat, so it can be tagged gluten-free. Sorghum rotis are the unleavened cousin. The dosa is the watered, soured, griddled one. The page does not pin the habit to a century.

Cultural background

Hubballi sits in a sorghum-growing region, and the flour is ordinary pantry stock there. A jowar dosa is a breakfast use of that flour, not a novelty millet. Cooks who skip the ferment and add yogurt are making the instant kind. This bowl is the fermented one.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum tastes mildly sweet and a little grassy, less bitter than finger millet. Fermentation brings a gentle sour note that keeps the sweetness from tasting raw. Rice flour helps the fragile sorghum starch hold together on the pan. Cumin and green chile are surface seasoning, so the grain still leads.

How it is made

Ferment a sorghum and rice-flour batter, then cook it in small thin rounds with cumin and chile.

Authentic touch. Spread gently. Jowar batter tears if you press the ladle the way you would on a glossy urad batter. A nonstick or very well-seasoned pan is the practical tool.

How it is used

Eat it hot with coconut chutney or a dry peanut chutney. Peanut chutney would add a peanut allergen, so the written cake stays nut-free.

Field notes

  • Jowar is sorghum, not millet and not wheat.
  • This recipe stays gluten-free by using sorghum and rice flour only.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup jowar flour (sorghum), with no wheat added
  • 2 tablespoons rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the flours, salt, and water into a lump-free batter.
  2. Ferment covered 8 to 12 hours until slightly sour. Count only the mixing as active prep. Discard batter that spoils.
  3. Stir in cumin and green chile. Loosen with water if needed.
  4. Oil a medium-hot pan. Spread a small round with a light hand.
  5. Cook until the underside is spotted and the edge is dry. Flip for a short minute. Repeat, oiling the pan each time.

Discard fermented batter that smells rotten or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped spinach stirred into the batter
  • Black pepper instead of green chile

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • A sprinkle of chopped onion
  • Sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Kenya · No chili heat · 8 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Kenya · East Africa

Kenyan chapati

Soft, flaky wheat flatbread with a lightly oiled, toasted surface.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 flatbreads

History

Kenyan chapati is a griddled flatbread, not a batter pancake. It came through the Indian Ocean with South Asian sailors, traders, and families, and it became a daily bread in Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania. The East African loaf is usually brushed with oil and rolled so the layers flake, then cooked on a dry pan. It is closer to a soft paratha than to a puffed dry phulka, and it has its own place at this table.

Cultural background

Chapati is weekend food and ordinary supper food. A stack disappears with beans, sukuma wiki, or a stew. The dough is mixed, rested, rolled, oiled, and rolled again. None of that is a bowl of batter. Homes in Kampala and Dar es Salaam cook the same bread.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat and salt are the flavor, with oil toasted between thin layers. The griddle adds brown spots that taste slightly smoky. Because fat is rolled in, the bread stays soft and flaky instead of crisp like a cracker. It does not taste sour. There is no ferment and no egg.

How it is made

Mix flour, salt, water, and a little oil into a soft dough and rest it. Roll each piece out, brush it with oil, coil it, and roll it again. Cook on a hot dry griddle, turning, until both sides blister and spot. Keep the stack covered so it steams soft.

Authentic touch. Treat it as a griddled flatbread. Roll, oil, and coil the dough so it flakes. Pouring a batter will not make Kenyan chapati. Cook it dry, with only the oil already in the dough.

How it is used

Chapati is an accompaniment to beans, sukuma wiki, and stews, and it is also breakfast with tea. It is a savory bread. It is not a dessert and not a pancake rolled around sugar.

Field notes

  • Kenyan chapati is a griddled flatbread, not a batter pancake.
  • The same bread is everyday food in Uganda and Tanzania.
  • Oil rolled into the dough is what makes the layers flake.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for brushing

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, salt, warm water, and a spoon of oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth. Cover and rest for 20 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough into 8 pieces. Roll one piece into a thin round.
  3. Brush the round with oil, roll it into a rope, and coil the rope into a disk. Roll the disk out again. This is a flatbread, not a batter.
  4. Cook on a hot dry griddle, turning several times, until both sides are blistered and spotted. Press the edges with a spatula so they cook through.
  5. Stack the chapatis under a cloth while you cook the rest.

The dough contains wheat. The griddle is dry but very hot. Oil in the layers can smoke if the pan is past medium-high.

Wrap leftover chapati so the surface does not dry. Refrigerate and reheat on a dry skillet. They stiffen in the refrigerator. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • More oil and an extra coil make a flakier bread.
  • Whole-wheat flour makes a denser chapati and still contains wheat.
  • A stuffed chapati is a different, thicker bread and not this plain one.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • Stewed beans
  • Sukuma wiki, braised greens
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Kenya · No chili heat · About 12 molded cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Kenya · East Africa

Kenyan vitumbua

Yeasted rice cakes scented with coconut and cardamom, crisp on the curve and soft inside.

25 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · About 12 molded cakes

History

Vitumbua are coconut rice pancakes of the Swahili coast, cooked in Kenya and along the Tanzanian coast as well. Rice flour, coconut milk, sugar, and yeast make a batter that is poured into a rounded pan, sometimes a pan with deep cups, and cooked until the shell browns. They are breakfast and street food in Mombasa. They are not the fried Zambian vitumbuwa, which have their own page.

Cultural background

Coastal kitchens already cook with coconut and rice. Vitumbua use both, often with cardamom. The rounded pan gives each cake a dome instead of a flat griddle cake. Vendors sell them in the morning beside tea.

Why it tastes this way

Coconut milk is sweet and rich without dairy. Rice keeps the crumb pale and mild. Yeast adds a faint ferment flavor and lift, and cardamom perfumes the fat. The curved pan browns a shell while the covered side stays tender, so the cake tastes fried at the edge and steamed in the middle.

How it is made

Whisk rice flour with coconut milk, water, sugar, yeast, salt, and cardamom. Rest until bubbly. Oil the cups of a rounded pan and cook the batter, turning once, until each cake is brown on the curve and set inside.

Authentic touch. Cook them in a rounded or deeply cupped pan if you have one. A flat crepe loses the shape people expect on the coast. Leave out wheat if you want this gluten-free version.

How it is used

Vitumbua are a Swahili-coast breakfast or a street snack with tea. They are sweet enough to eat plain. A extra splash of coconut milk is a home habit, not a syrup.

Field notes

  • Vitumbua are often cooked in a rounded pan, which gives them a dome.
  • They belong to the Swahili coast, including kitchens in Tanzania.
  • Zambian vitumbuwa are fried rice fritters and are not this pan.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups rice flour (about 200 g)
  • 1 cup coconut milk
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cardamom
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • Oil for the cups

Steps

  1. Whisk the rice flour, coconut milk, water, sugar, yeast, cardamom, and salt into a thick batter. There is no wheat in this recipe.
  2. Cover and leave it for 45 to 60 minutes, until the surface is bubbly.
  3. Oil the cups of a rounded vitumbua pan, or a heavy dimpled pan, and heat them over medium heat.
  4. Fill the cups about two-thirds full. Cover and cook until the bottoms are browned, then turn each cake and cook until the center is set.
  5. Serve warm. These are pan-molded cakes, not the fried fritters called vitumbuwa in Zambia.

The batter contains coconut milk. Coconut is not listed here as a peanut or a tree nut. Skip the recipe if you avoid coconut. Gluten-free only if the rice flour is free of wheat.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat in the oiled pan. The coconut crumb dries out in the refrigerator after a day or two. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • An overnight rest makes the batter tangier.
  • Baking powder instead of yeast is a quicker cake and less fermented.
  • The Tanzanian coast cooks the same family of coconut rice cakes.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Street food
  • Cardamom tea
  • A little extra coconut milk
  • Fresh mango
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Iran · No chili heat · 1 round cake, about 8 squares

Flying Pancakes of the World

Iran · Middle East

Kuku sabzi

A dense herb frittata, bright green inside, with walnut and a sour note from barberries.

25 min prep · 35 min cook · Serves 6 · 1 round cake, about 8 squares

History

Kuku sabzi is an Iranian herb and egg cake served at Nowruz, the new year, and at other tables through the year. It is a frittata, not a pancake. Eggs bind a large quantity of parsley, cilantro, and dill, with a spoon of flour for structure, and the cake is cooked slowly until the center sets. Walnuts and dried barberries, zereshk, are the usual scatter inside. It is not one of the symbolic haft-sin items. It is a dish on the Nowruz table.

Cultural background

The herbs are the body of the dish. A kuku that is mostly egg and a pinch of green is a different, thinner omelette. In Tehran and in other Iranian cities it is cut into squares for guests. The same cake appears at breakfast the next day, eaten cold or warm.

Why it tastes this way

Parsley, cilantro, and dill in this amount taste grassy, citrusy, and a little bitter, and egg makes that mass rich instead of like a salad. Turmeric colors the egg gold at the edges. Walnuts add fat and a tannic bite. Barberries are sour and small, so they pop against the herbs. Because it is a frittata, the texture is set and sliceable, not folded like a crepe.

How it is made

Chop a large bunch of herbs finely, squeeze them dry, and stir them into beaten eggs with turmeric, salt, a spoon of flour, chopped walnuts, and barberries. Cook the cake slowly in an oiled skillet, covered, until the center is set. Flip it or finish it under heat if the top is still wet.

Authentic touch. Call it a frittata, not a pancake. Use enough herbs that the batter looks green before it cooks. Walnuts mean this cake is not nut-free. If you cannot find barberries, leave them out rather than substituting a sweet dried fruit.

How it is used

Kuku sabzi is a Nowruz dish, a breakfast, and a savory plate with flatbread, yogurt, and herbs. The yogurt is extra dairy. The cake itself already has egg, wheat, and tree nut. It is feast food when the new year is the occasion, and ordinary food the rest of the year.

Field notes

  • Kuku sabzi is a frittata, an egg cake, not a pancake.
  • It is served at Nowruz, the Iranian new year, and it is not one of the haft-sin symbols.
  • Walnuts in this recipe are a tree nut, so the cake is not nut-free.

Ingredients

  • 4 eggs
  • 2 cups finely chopped parsley
  • 1 1/2 cups finely chopped cilantro
  • 1 cup finely chopped dill
  • 4 scallions, finely chopped
  • 1 tablespoon wheat flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground turmeric
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1/3 cup chopped walnuts
  • 2 tablespoons dried barberries, rinsed
  • 2 tablespoons oil

Steps

  1. Chop the herbs finely and squeeze out excess moisture. Beat the eggs in a bowl.
  2. Stir the herbs, scallions, flour, turmeric, salt, walnuts, and barberries into the eggs. The mixture should look green, not pale. The walnuts are a tree nut.
  3. Heat the oil in a skillet over medium-low heat. Pour in the herb mixture and smooth the top. This is a frittata, not a pancake batter.
  4. Cover and cook slowly until the sides are set and the center no longer jiggles like liquid, about 20 minutes.
  5. Flip the cake onto a plate and slide it back to cook the second side for a few minutes, or set the top under a hot broiler. Cool slightly, then cut.

Cook the egg until the center is set. Walnuts are a tree nut. The spoon of flour is wheat. Barberries are sour berries, not a nut.

Refrigerate kuku the same day and eat it within a couple of days, cold or reheated. Do not leave the egg cake out. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Without walnuts the cake drops the tree-nut allergen. This written recipe includes them.
  • A pinch of fenugreek leaf is common and makes the aroma stronger.
  • Baking the pan in a moderate oven, after a start on the stove, sets the center evenly.

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Flatbread
  • Plain yogurt, which adds dairy
  • More fresh herbs
  • Vegetarian

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 18 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Kuzhi paniyaram

Small fermented rice-and-urad balls, crisp outside, soft inside, with mustard seed and curry leaf.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 18 small cakes

History

Kuzhi paniyaram is fermented rice and urad batter cooked in a dimpled pan, a Tamil Nadu breakfast and tiffin. Kuzhi means the pit or hollow of the pan. The batter is the idli-dosa ferment. The pan, not a new grain, makes the shape. Savory versions take a tempering of mustard seed and curry leaf. Sweet versions use jaggery. This page is the savory one.

Cultural background

Leftover idli batter is the honest weeknight source. The dimples fry the outside while the center steams. A flat griddle would simply make dosa. Telugu and Kannada kitchens have cousin cakes in similar pans. This recipe stays with the Tamil savory paniyaram.

Why it tastes this way

The ferment is mildly sour and bready because the ball is thick. Mustard seed and curry leaf, fried and then folded in, perfume the batter before it ever hits the pan. Oil in the dimples browns a crust that a steamed idli never gets. The contrast, crisp shell and soft sour crumb, is the reason to use the special pan.

How it is made

Fold a mustard and curry-leaf tempering into fermented rice and urad batter, then fry spoonfuls in a heated dimpled pan.

Authentic touch. Heat the empty pan before you oil the pits. Cold oil in a cold mold sticks and pulls the cakes apart.

How it is used

Unmold them when the bottoms are deep gold. Eat with coconut chutney while the crust still crackles.

Field notes

  • The batter is the fermented rice and urad batter. The dimpled pan is the change.
  • Kuzhi refers to the hollows the batter is poured into.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented rice and urad idli or dosa batter
  • 1 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 10 curry leaves
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil, plus more for the pan
  • A pinch of salt if needed

Steps

  1. Use batter that has already fermented and smells pleasantly sour. Stir it down.
  2. Heat a tablespoon of oil, pop the mustard seeds, and add curry leaves and green chile for a few seconds.
  3. Fold that tempering into the batter.
  4. Heat a kuzhi paniyaram pan until hot. Add a few drops of oil to each dip. Fill the dips about three-quarters full.
  5. Cover and cook, turning the cakes once with a skewer, until both sides are brown and the centers are set. Drain briefly and serve.

Discard fermented batter that is moldy or smells putrid. Turn the cakes so the centers cook through.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped onion in the batter
  • A sweet batch with jaggery instead of the tempering

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Milagai podi
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 4 skillet cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Lacy cornbread

Crisp, salty corn with a brittle lace of holes and a tender spot in the middle.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 skillet cakes

History

Lacy cornbread is a very thin cornmeal batter cooked in a hot skillet until the surface opens into crisp holes. It belongs to Appalachian corn cooking, especially in West Virginia and southwestern Virginia. It is a griddle bread, closer to a crisp cake than to a deep pan of cornbread. The batter is meal, water, and salt.

Cultural background

Mountain cooks poured this batter into a hot greased skillet when they wanted cornbread in a hurry. The lace is what the heat does to a thin batter, not a separate ingredient.

Why it tastes this way

Extra water lets the batter spider across the pan instead of sitting in a mound. Hot fat fries the strands as they spread, so the cake tastes toasted and crisp. Coarse meal keeps a corn flavor that wheat cornbread would soften.

How it is made

Poured thin into a very hot oiled skillet so the batter laces.

Authentic touch. The batter should look thin, almost like cream. If it sits in a thick puddle, add water. The lace is the test that you got it right.

How it is used

Whole from the skillet, broken at the table, often with beans or greens.

Field notes

  • The holes are the batter frying as it spreads, which is why cooks call it lacy.
  • It is closer to a crisp griddle cake than to a square of baked cornbread.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 3/4 cups water
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil, divided

Steps

  1. Whisk the cornmeal, salt, water, and 1 tablespoon of the oil into a thin batter. It should run off the spoon.
  2. Rest the batter 5 minutes and whisk again.
  3. Heat a small skillet over medium-high heat. Add 1 1/2 teaspoons of oil and let it get hot.
  4. Pour in about 1/3 cup batter and tilt the pan. The batter should spread and break into holes.
  5. Cook until the top looks dry and the underside is brown and crisp, then flip carefully and brown the other side.
  6. Repeat with the remaining oil and batter. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bacon fat in place of oil is common in kitchens that cook pork.
  • A spoon of wheat flour stops the lace and makes a different bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Pot beans
  • Cooked greens
  • Butter, if you eat dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Lancashire potato cakes

Thick mashed-potato cakes, fried, from Lancashire breakfasts.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Lancashire potato cakes are mashed potato bound with flour and fried. They are a breakfast cake, often made when there is mash left from the day before. They are not Irish boxty, which uses raw grated potato as well as mash, and they are not Scottish tattie scones, which are rolled thin and cooked on a dry girdle. This version is a thicker round, fried in butter.

Cultural background

Potato cakes of this sort belong to the industrial breakfast as much as to a farmhouse. They use a staple that stores. The flavor depends on the potato, so a watery mash makes a weak cake.

Why it tastes this way

Flour and a little butter keep mashed potato from falling apart, and frying browns the outside. The inside stays soft and salty. Pepper is the usual seasoning. There is no egg in this recipe, so the cake tastes of potato rather than of omelet.

How it is made

Cold mashed potato is worked with flour, butter, and salt, shaped into thick rounds, and fried in butter until crusted.

Authentic touch. Dry the mash. A cake that weeps in the pan was too wet. Flour is the binder here, which means the cake is not gluten-free.

How it is used

At breakfast, with eggs or bacon, or with butter and salt alone. Bacon makes the plate a meat meal.

Field notes

  • These use mashed potato only. Boxty also uses raw grated potato.
  • Leftover mash is the usual reason to make them.
  • They are fried rounds, not the thin triangles of a tattie scone.

Ingredients

  • 500 g cold mashed potato, made without much liquid
  • 80 g plain wheat flour, plus more for shaping
  • 30 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for frying
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp black pepper

Steps

  1. Mix the mash, flour, melted butter, salt, and pepper into a soft dough. Add a spoon more flour if it sticks.
  2. Shape eight thick rounds on a floured board.
  3. Fry in butter over medium heat until both sides are brown and the center is hot.
  4. Serve at once with pepper, eggs, or bacon.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of chopped chives in the mash
  • Scottish tattie scones, which are thinner and cooked more dry

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Fried eggs
  • Bacon
  • Butter and black pepper
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Lebanon · No chili heat · 6 small flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Lebanon · Middle East

Lebanese manakish

Soft round flatbread topped with thyme, sumac, sesame, and olive oil.

30 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 small flatbreads

History

Manakish are Lebanese flatbreads baked with a topping, and the za'atar one is the breakfast loaf. Za'atar here means a mix of dried thyme, sumac, sesame, and salt, stirred with olive oil and spread on dough. Bakeries and home ovens both make them. This recipe uses a hot oven, not a saj. A saj griddle is the other common method and is not the one written out below. The singular is often man'oushe.

Cultural background

In Beirut, za'atar manakish are morning food, eaten on the way to work or at the kitchen table. Cheese manakish are a different topping and contain dairy. The za'atar bread is the one that can stay vegan. Sesame is in the topping, so the allergen list includes it.

Why it tastes this way

Thyme is herbal and dry, sumac is sour, and sesame is nutty and toasty once the oven hits it. Olive oil keeps the topping from tasting like dust and fries it lightly on the dough. The bread underneath is mild and a little sweet from wheat. There is no chile, so the brightness is acid from the sumac, not heat.

How it is made

Mix a soft dough and let it rise. Shape small rounds, dimple them, and spread a paste of thyme, sumac, sesame, salt, and olive oil. Bake on a hot stone or sheet until the rim puffs and the topping looks glossy and toasted.

Authentic touch. Bake these in a very hot oven, which is the method this page gives. Spread the za'atar and oil to the edge. A saj version is real and is not the set of steps below. Sesame is part of the topping.

How it is used

Za'atar manakish are breakfast, street food, and a snack. They are eaten with tea, labneh, or vegetables. Labneh is dairy and is not in the dough. Cheese manakish are a separate pie.

Field notes

  • This recipe bakes the bread in a hot oven. A saj griddle is the other usual method.
  • Za'atar on this page is thyme, sumac, sesame, salt, and olive oil.
  • Man'oushe is the singular. Manakish is the plural.

Ingredients

  • 2 1/2 cups wheat flour (about 310 g)
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 1 cup warm water
  • 1 tablespoon olive oil, for the dough
  • 3 tablespoons dried thyme
  • 1 tablespoon ground sumac
  • 2 tablespoons sesame seeds
  • 3 tablespoons olive oil, for the topping

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, yeast, half the salt, water, and the tablespoon of olive oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth. Cover and let it rise for 45 minutes.
  2. Stir the thyme, sumac, sesame seeds, remaining salt, and the topping oil into a spreadable paste. The sesame is an allergen in this bread.
  3. Heat the oven to 230 C, or as hot as it will go. Divide the dough into 6 rounds and press each into a disk.
  4. Spread the za'atar paste over each disk, leaving a small rim. This method is the oven, not a saj.
  5. Bake until the rims puff and the topping is fragrant and the bottoms are spotted, about 8 to 12 minutes.

The dough contains wheat. The topping contains sesame. Sumac is sour, not a chile, so the heat tag stays at none.

Eat manakish the day they are baked. Wrap leftovers and reheat them in a hot oven. The topping tastes dull from a microwave. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A cheese topping, often akkawi, adds dairy and is not this recipe.
  • More sumac makes a tarter topping. More thyme makes it grassier.
  • Cooking on a saj instead of in the oven is the other method and needs a different pan.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Tea
  • Sliced tomato and cucumber
  • Labneh, which adds dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 10 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Lemon ricotta pancakes

Soft, custardy ricotta cakes with bright lemon zest and a light crumb.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 small pancakes

History

Lemon ricotta pancakes are an Italian-American restaurant breakfast, a soft wheat cake enriched with ricotta and brightened with lemon. They became a brunch-menu item in American cities in the late twentieth century. This page does not trace them to a single village in Italy. The North End of Boston is the map pin for an Italian-American neighborhood.

Cultural background

Whole-milk ricotta makes a moister cake than a lean wheat batter. The lemon is zest plus a little juice, so the flavor is in the crumb and not only in a syrup.

Why it tastes this way

Ricotta adds fat and small curds, which taste milky and keep the center soft. Lemon zest holds the fragrant oil. Juice adds acid and helps the baking powder along. Overcooking dries the curds, so the cakes come off the heat as soon as they set.

How it is made

Small, thick ricotta cakes griddled in butter.

Authentic touch. Use whole-milk ricotta and fresh lemon zest. Bottled juice without zest will not smell like lemon. Keep the cakes small so they cook before the outside burns.

How it is used

Warm, with a little powdered sugar or berry sauce.

Field notes

  • These became an American brunch item in Italian-American restaurants in the late twentieth century.
  • The lemon flavor depends on zest. Juice alone tastes flatter.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup whole-milk ricotta
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1/2 cup whole milk
  • 1 tablespoon finely grated lemon zest
  • 2 tablespoons lemon juice
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the ricotta, eggs, milk, lemon zest, lemon juice, and sugar until smooth.
  2. Stir in the flour, baking powder, and salt until just combined.
  3. Butter a griddle over medium-low heat.
  4. Drop 1/4 cup mounds. These cakes stay thicker than a poured diner pancake.
  5. Cook until the underside is golden and the side looks set, then flip and finish briefly.
  6. Serve warm with powdered sugar or berries.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Orange zest in place of lemon is a milder cake.
  • Drained ricotta makes a thicker batter if the cheese you bought is watery.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Powdered sugar
  • Fresh berries
  • Lemon wedges
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Ecuador · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ecuador · South America

Llapingachos

Pan-fried potato cakes with cheese inside and a mild peanut sauce over the top.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Llapingachos are pan-fried potato cakes from the Ecuadorian Andes, especially the central highlands around Ambato. Mashed potato is stuffed with cheese, browned in a skillet, and often served with salsa de maní, a peanut sauce. Peanuts are an Andean crop. This recipe includes the sauce, so the plate contains peanut and is not nut-free. The cheese makes it a dairy dish. A fried egg or a sausage on the side is common and is not in the written steps.

Cultural background

They are breakfast and lunch in highland towns, sold in markets and cooked at home when potatoes are the meal. Annatto colors some batches a soft orange. The sauce is onion, roasted peanut, and milk, not a fiery chile paste. Ají can be served beside it. It is not mixed in here.

Why it tastes this way

Potato and salt are the body, fried so the outside browns and the cheese in the center softens. The peanut sauce tastes toasted and slightly sweet, loosened with milk and onion. Annatto adds color and a faint earthy note, not heat. Because there is no chile in the sauce, the dish stays at no chili heat. The peanut is obvious. Anyone who avoids peanuts should not make this version.

How it is made

Mash boiled potatoes with salt and a little annatto. Seal cubes of cheese inside cakes and pan-fry them. Cook onion and ground roasted peanuts with milk into a sauce and spoon it over.

Authentic touch. Chill the mashed potato before you stuff it, or the cheese leaks before a crust can form. Seal the cakes well. Simmer the peanut sauce until it is thick enough to spoon, not a broth, and salt it.

How it is used

Serve two cakes with sauce for breakfast or lunch. A fried egg on top adds the egg allergen. Chorizo adds meat and is not vegetarian. Neither is in this pan.

Field notes

  • The peanut sauce means this recipe is not nut-free.
  • Cheese inside the potato cake softens. It does not have to stretch like a pizza.
  • Ambato and the central highlands are the landscape people name with llapingachos.

Ingredients

  • 700 g potatoes, peeled
  • 1 teaspoon salt, divided
  • Pinch of ground annatto
  • 120 g fresh white cheese, in 8 cubes
  • 80 g roasted peanuts
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 120 ml milk
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil

Steps

  1. Boil the potatoes until tender. Drain, mash, and season with 1/2 teaspoon salt and the annatto. Cool until you can handle the mash.
  2. Divide into 8 portions. Seal a cube of cheese inside each and flatten into a thick cake.
  3. Pan-fry in the oil over medium heat until both sides are browned and the center is hot.
  4. Meanwhile, cook the onion in a spoon of oil, add the peanuts, milk, and remaining salt, and simmer. Mash or blend the sauce until thick.
  5. Spoon the peanut sauce over the cakes and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the peanut sauce and the allergen list loses peanut, and the plate can be nut-free. It is a plainer dish.
  • A fried egg is a usual topping and adds egg.
  • Chorizo on the side adds meat and is not vegetarian.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Ají on the side, which adds heat
  • A small salad of onion and tomato
  • Black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 10 griddle cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Lowcountry rice griddle cakes

Soft, lightly sweet rice cakes with a crisp buttered edge and separate grains in the crumb.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 griddle cakes

History

Lowcountry rice griddle cakes use the long-grain rice of the South Carolina and Georgia coast. Enslaved West Africans brought rice-growing knowledge, and planters built a brutal rice economy on their labor and on the labor of their descendants. The cake is a later griddle use of that grain. It is not a rename of one village dish.

Cultural background

Charleston and the surrounding Lowcountry were the center of that rice system. Carolina-grown long-grain rice is the grain to use when you can get it. Any cooked long-grain white rice will hold the cake.

Why it tastes this way

Cooked rice stays in distinct grains, so the cake tastes steamy and mild instead of like a smooth wheat crumb. Rice flour binds the cakes without gluten. Butter browns the outside and keeps the rice from tasting flat.

How it is made

Cooked rice and rice flour griddled in butter.

Authentic touch. Use leftover cooked rice, not raw rice thrown into a wheat pancake. Name the labor when you tell the story of the grain.

How it is used

Warm, with butter. A little sorghum or cane syrup fits the coast.

Field notes

  • Rice in the Lowcountry is tied to West African knowledge and to enslaved labor, not to a single exported village recipe.
  • Leftover cooked rice is the usual base, which is why these cakes are a breakfast after a rice supper.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups cooked long-grain white rice, cooled
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1/2 cup milk
  • 1/3 cup rice flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mash the rice with a fork so some grains break and some stay whole.
  2. Stir in the eggs, milk, rice flour, baking powder, sugar, salt, and melted butter.
  3. Heat a buttered griddle over medium heat.
  4. Drop 1/4 cup mounds and flatten them slightly. They will not look like smooth wheat batter.
  5. Cook until the underside is golden and the cake holds together, then flip and finish.
  6. Serve warm with butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory version leaves out the sugar and adds a sliced scallion.
  • Wheat flour in place of rice flour means the cakes are no longer gluten-free.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Cane syrup
  • Shrimp gravy, for a savory plate, if you eat shellfish
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Maine blueberry pancakes

Tart wild blueberries bursting into a buttered, lightly sweet wheat cake.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Maine blueberry pancakes fold lowbush wild blueberries into a wheat batter. Those berries are native to the region, and Wabanaki communities have long gathered them. The pancake is a breakfast use of the berry. Machias sits in Washington County, where the wild blueberry harvest is part of the local economy.

Cultural background

Lowbush berries are smaller and more tart than many cultivated highbush berries. Frozen wild berries are the honest off-season choice. Do not thaw them before they go into the batter.

Why it tastes this way

Wild berries have more acid and deeper color than large cultivated ones, so they stain the crumb purple and taste sharper. A thicker batter keeps the juice from spreading into one gray cake. Butter and a light syrup keep the fruit in front.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter with whole wild blueberries folded in.

Authentic touch. Use wild blueberries, fresh or frozen. Fold them in at the end and do not mash them.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and maple syrup.

Field notes

  • Lowbush wild blueberries are native to Maine and the Maritimes.
  • Wabanaki people gathered them long before this wheat pancake existed.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle
  • 1 cup wild blueberries, fresh or frozen

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  2. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter until just combined. The batter should be a little thick.
  3. Fold in the blueberries. If they are frozen, do not thaw them first.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Cook until you see berries at the surface and the underside is brown, then flip. Turn the heat down if the berries scorch.
  6. Serve when the center is set, with butter and maple syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Frozen berries go in still frozen so they do not dye the whole batter.
  • Cultivated blueberries work, with a milder taste.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Maple syrup
  • Butter
  • A spoon of extra berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Israel · No chili heat · 4 skillet flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Israel · Middle East

Malawach

Flaky laminated flatbread, salty and toasted, with a pull-apart layer at the edge.

35 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 skillet flatbreads

History

Malawach is a Yemeni Jewish laminated flatbread. Families brought it from Yemen, and many arrived in Israel in the 1949 to 1950 airlift often called Operation Magic Carpet. In Israeli home kitchens it became a breakfast bread: the dough is layered with fat, chilled, and cooked in a skillet. This recipe uses oil, so the written dough has no dairy. Grated tomato, a hard-boiled egg, and zhug are the plate around it. The origin is Yemeni. The weekday pan on this page is an Israeli one.

Cultural background

The dough is often mixed at night and cooked in the morning, which suits a home kitchen more than a stall. It is richer than a plain pita and flakier than a single-layer chapati. Restaurants later put it on menus. The method here stays with the skillet.

Why it tastes this way

Lamination is the flavor: thin sheets of dough fried lightly in their own oil, so the bread tastes toasted and almost pastry-like without sugar. Salt is the only seasoning in the dough. Grated tomato adds acid, and zhug adds chile and herbs at the table. Those condiments are why one bread can taste different from one house to the next.

How it is made

Mix a soft dough, rest it, then stretch it thin, brush it with oil, and fold it into a packet. Chill the packet. Cook it in a dry skillet from cold-ish dough until both sides are browned and the layers pull apart. Serve with grated tomato.

Authentic touch. Layer the dough and chill it before you cook it, so the flakes separate. Remember both kitchens: the bread's Yemeni origin, and the Israeli home breakfast it became. Oil in this recipe keeps the dough free of dairy.

How it is used

Malawach is breakfast. A typical plate is the bread with grated tomato, zhug, and a hard-boiled egg. Honey is the sweet path. Egg and zhug are accompaniments. Zhug is hot even though the bread is not.

Field notes

  • Malawach is a laminated flatbread of Yemeni Jewish origin, cooked in Israeli home kitchens.
  • This dough uses oil, so the bread itself is dairy-free.
  • Grated tomato and zhug are breakfast partners, not fillings baked inside.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 4 tablespoons neutral oil, divided

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, salt, water, and one tablespoon of oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth and rest it for 15 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough into 4. Stretch one piece into a thin sheet, brush it with oil, and fold it into a small layered packet.
  3. Repeat with the rest. Cover and chill the packets for at least 20 minutes so the layers firm up.
  4. Cook each packet in a dry skillet over medium heat, turning, until both sides are browned and the edge flakes. This is laminated dough, not a batter.
  5. Serve in an Israeli home-breakfast way: with grated tomato, and with zhug or egg if you want them.

The dough contains wheat. Zhug served alongside is a chile sauce. A hard-boiled egg adds egg only on plates that include it.

Refrigerate leftover malawach and reheat it in a dry skillet so the layers crisp. Raw packets can be kept refrigerated for a day before cooking. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Butter or margarine instead of oil changes the fat and may add dairy.
  • Honey instead of tomato makes a sweet breakfast.
  • A thinner packet cooks faster and flakes more. A thick one stays bready.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Grated fresh tomato
  • Zhug
  • A hard-boiled egg
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 6 stuffed dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Masala dosa

A fermented rice-and-urad sheet wrapped around a turmeric potato filling, with curry leaf and green chile.

45 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 stuffed dosas

History

Masala dosa uses the same fermented rice and urad batter as a plain dosa. The potato masala is a filling, not a second grain and not a different ferment. Restaurant kitchens in Karnataka, including Bengaluru's hotel dosas, made the potato-stuffed fold a city breakfast. The filling itself is a home potato palya that already belonged on the plate beside a plain dosa.

Cultural background

The potato should be dry enough to sit inside the roll without soaking the crisp side. Onion cooked in oil with mustard seed, curry leaf, and turmeric is the usual base. A wet curry would belong in a bowl, not in the fold.

Why it tastes this way

The batter still tastes of fermented rice and urad. The masala adds an earthy, lightly bitter turmeric note, the sweetness of onion, and the pop of mustard seed. Because the potato is spiced and then wrapped, each bite mixes a sour crisp sheet with a soft, salted center. The green chile is there for aroma more than burn.

How it is made

Ferment a rice and urad batter, cook a dry spiced potato, spread a thin dosa, lay the potato in a line, and roll.

Authentic touch. Spread the potato in a strip on the set dosa, not in a tall mound. Too much filling cracks the sheet before it can be rolled.

How it is used

Cut the roll only if you must. Most tables eat it with coconut chutney and sambar, starting at the crisp tail.

Field notes

  • The batter is still rice and urad. Potato never replaces either grain.
  • A masala dosa that sits too long steams the crust from the inside out.

Ingredients

  • 1 batch fermented rice and urad dosa batter, about 3 cups
  • 3 medium potatoes, boiled, peeled, and crumbled
  • 1 small onion, sliced
  • 1 green chile, slit
  • 1/2 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 8 curry leaves
  • 1/4 teaspoon turmeric
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for the griddle
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt, or to taste

Steps

  1. Use a bubbly fermented rice and urad batter, ground and rested as for a plain dosa. Active prep here is the filling and the cooking, not a second grind.
  2. Heat 2 tablespoons of oil. Add mustard seeds. When they pop, add curry leaves, onion, and green chile. Cook until the onion softens.
  3. Add turmeric and the crumbled potato. Season with salt. Mash lightly and cook until the mixture looks dry. Cool a little.
  4. Heat a griddle, grease it, and spread a ladle of batter in a thin round. Oil the edge and cook until the underside is golden.
  5. Lay a strip of potato across the center. Fold the dosa over the filling. Serve at once with chutney.

Use batter that smells pleasantly sour. Discard it if it is moldy or putrid.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of ghee on the potato, which adds dairy
  • A little cooked green pea in the masala

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Fresh coconut and cilantro
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 2 skilletfuls

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Matzo brei

Soft scrambled egg bound around soaked matzo, salty and toasted in butter.

10 min prep · 8 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 skilletfuls

History

Matzo brei is matzo soaked, mixed with egg, and fried, a Passover breakfast in Ashkenazi homes and a year-round habit in many Jewish American kitchens. Matzo is unleavened wheat, so the dish is not gluten-free. Both a salted version and a cinnamon-sugar version are established. This written recipe is the salted one, pinned in Brooklyn.

Cultural background

Plain wheat matzo, broken and briefly soaked, is the base. Egg matzo is a different cracker. The dish is an egg skillet cake more than a poured pancake.

Why it tastes this way

A short soak softens the cracker without dissolving it, so you still taste shards of toasted matzo. Egg makes a soft scramble around those shards. Butter browns at the edge of the pan. Too much water turns it into mush, which is why the soak is brief.

How it is made

Soaked matzo scrambled with egg in butter.

Authentic touch. Soak the sheets just until they bend, then squeeze them. The sweet table version gets cinnamon sugar at the end. This batch is salted.

How it is used

Hot from the skillet, salted. A sweet serving gets sugar and cinnamon instead.

Field notes

  • Matzo brei is a Passover breakfast in many Ashkenazi homes and is also eaten the rest of the year.
  • Matzo is wheat, so the dish is not gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 4 sheets plain wheat matzo
  • 4 large eggs
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter
  • 1/4 teaspoon black pepper

Steps

  1. Break the matzo into small pieces and cover them with cold water for about 1 minute, until they bend. Drain and squeeze gently.
  2. Beat the eggs with the salt and black pepper. Stir in the matzo.
  3. Melt the butter in a skillet over medium heat.
  4. Add the egg mixture and cook, turning with a spatula, until the egg is just set and some edges are brown.
  5. Serve at once, salty, or follow the sweet variation if that is your table.
  6. Eat it hot. It toughens as it sits.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • For the sweet version, reduce the salt and dust the finished brei with sugar and cinnamon.
  • Some kitchens fry it as a single pancake instead of a scramble.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Feast or fast day
  • Salt and black pepper
  • Applesauce
  • Cinnamon sugar, for the sweet version
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · No chili heat · 8 hotcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Mexican hotcakes

Soft wheat cakes with browned butter, finished with cajeta, honey, or condensed milk.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 hotcakes

History

Wheat hotcakes spread through Mexican breakfasts in the twentieth century, when baking powder, packaged flour, and diner griddles showed up on fonda menus. A morning cook still turns them out beside coffee and eggs. Corn tortillas and other masa cakes are much older in Mexico. These hotcakes are a later wheat batter. They are not an ancient recipe, and they are not a corn cake under a new name.

Cultural background

In a fonda, hotcakes are ordinary city breakfast, not a festival dish. The plate is familiar in Mexico City and in towns across the country, usually sweetened at the table rather than in the bowl.

Why it tastes this way

The crumb tastes of wheat and browned butter, and baking powder keeps it soft if you stop stirring once the flour disappears. Cajeta, goat-milk caramel, is tangy and dark. Sweetened condensed milk is plain and very sweet. Honey stays floral. The topping does as much work as the batter, which is why a stack without one of them tastes unfinished.

How it is made

Whisk milk, egg, and melted butter. Stir them into the dry ingredients only until the flour is wet. Cook small rounds in a buttered skillet until the bubbles stay open, then flip once.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter for five minutes, then leave the lumps alone. A smooth, overworked batter makes a tough cake. The griddle is ready when a drop of water skitters.

How it is used

Serve straight from the pan at breakfast, with cajeta, honey, or condensed milk, and coffee. This is a fonda plate, not a street antojito.

Field notes

  • Baking powder is a nineteenth-century tool, and this Mexican breakfast cake follows that later wheat style.
  • Cajeta is goat milk cooked with sugar. It is not the same jar as cow-milk dulce de leche.
  • Corn cakes were already the daily griddle food of Mexico long before these hotcakes arrived.

Ingredients

  • 150 g wheat flour
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 240 ml milk
  • 1 egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter, plus more for the skillet
  • Cajeta, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, sugar, and salt in a bowl.
  2. Whisk the milk, egg, and melted butter in a second bowl, then pour them into the flour and stir only until the dry streaks disappear.
  3. Rest the batter for 5 minutes. Heat a skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Pour about 60 ml of batter for each cake. Cook until the bubbles stay open and the underside is browned, then flip and cook the second side.
  5. Serve at once with cajeta. Honey or sweetened condensed milk can go on at the table instead.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Honey or sweetened condensed milk can replace the cajeta.
  • A spoon of orange zest in the batter is a home variation.
  • Whole wheat flour makes a heartier cake and still contains wheat.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Café de olla or black coffee
  • Eggs on the same breakfast plate
  • Sliced fruit beside the stack
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Michigan cherry pancakes

Tart cherry against a lightly sweet buttermilk cake.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Tart cherries grown on the Grand Traverse peninsula, especially around Traverse City, are the fruit in these pancakes. The orchards became a major local crop, and cafes serve cherry pancakes in season. The cake is a seasonal wheat breakfast with fruit in the batter. Dried tart cherries are the pantry version.

Cultural background

Montmorency is the tart cherry most associated with Michigan pies and breakfasts. Sweet eating cherries are a milder substitute. Pit the fruit. The red juice will stain the batter a little, which is expected.

Why it tastes this way

Tart cherries keep their acid when they are heated, so the cake does not taste only sweet. A small amount of sugar in the batter meets that acid. Butter and maple syrup at the table are the richness. Too many cherries and the cakes steam instead of brown.

How it is made

Griddled buttermilk batter with pitted tart cherries.

Authentic touch. Use tart cherries, not a sweet cherry pie filling from a can. Halve them and fold them in.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and maple syrup.

Field notes

  • Traverse City and the Grand Traverse peninsula are the center of Michigan tart-cherry growing.
  • Tart cherries, often Montmorency, are the fruit these pancakes are built on, not sweet dessert cherries.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup pitted tart cherries, halved
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 3 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Halve the cherries and pat them dry.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  3. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter. Fold in the cherries.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the underside is brown. Lower the heat if the juice starts to scorch.
  6. Serve when the center is set, with butter and maple syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Dried tart cherries, chopped, work in winter. Soak them 10 minutes first.
  • A spoon of the cherry juice in the batter makes the color deeper and the cakes looser.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup
  • A spoon of warm cherries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Minnesota wild rice pancakes

A wheat pancake with chewy, toasted grains of wild rice scattered through it.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Minnesota wild rice pancakes stir cooked wild rice into a wheat batter and cook it like any other breakfast cake. Manoomin is central to Ojibwe food and history in this region, and this pancake is a Minnesota cafe and home recipe, not an Ojibwe bread. Cooked lake rice and cooked cultivated wild rice both work in the batter, and they are not the same harvest. Bemidji is the map pin.

Cultural background

Wild rice is a grass seed, not a leftover of the Lowcountry rice pot. It stays chewy when it is folded into batter. The wheat, buttermilk, and egg make this a standard risen pancake with a grain added.

Why it tastes this way

Wild rice tastes toasted and slightly tea-like, and the grains pop between the teeth because they do not dissolve. Buttermilk is the soft background. If the rice is blended smooth, that texture disappears and the cake tastes muddy.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter with cooked wild rice folded in.

Authentic touch. Fold cooked grains in at the end. Do not call this an Ojibwe recipe. If you use lake rice, know that it is a different harvest from paddy-grown wild rice.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and maple syrup.

Field notes

  • Manoomin is central to Ojibwe life, and this wheat pancake is a separate Minnesota breakfast.
  • Lake-harvested wild rice and cultivated paddy rice are not the same crop.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup cooked wild rice, cooled
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Fluff the cooked wild rice so the grains are separate.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  3. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter until just combined. Fold in the rice.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat and pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Cook until the underside is brown and the grains on top look hot, then flip and finish.
  6. Serve with butter and maple syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leftover cooked wild rice from a supper is the practical start.
  • A savory cake leaves out the sugar and is eaten with eggs.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup
  • A few extra warm grains of wild rice on top
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free
