Madagascar · No chili heat · About 12 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Madagascar · East Africa

Mofo gasy

Small, sweet rice cakes with a yeasted crumb and a browned top.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · About 12 small cakes

History

Mofo gasy means Malagasy bread, and on a morning street in Antananarivo it is a small sweet cake of rice flour, sold by the piece with coffee. The batter is yeasted, sweetened, and cooked in small rounds. Rice is the grain on this page. A wheat version exists in some pans and is not the one written here. Madagascar sits in East Africa on this map.

Cultural background

Vendors cook mofo gasy early, because it is breakfast. The cakes are thick for their width, not crepes. People eat them walking to work. The sweetness is in the batter. There is no savory filling.

Why it tastes this way

Rice flour tastes mild and slightly grassy, and sugar turns that mildness into a breakfast cake. Yeast gives a soft dome and a faint fermented smell, closer to a little brioche than to a sour injera. The pan browns the bottom, which is the bitter-sweet edge against the sweet crumb.

How it is made

Mix rice flour, sugar, yeast, salt, and water into a thick batter. Rest it until it doubles and looks bubbly. Cook small rounds on a lightly oiled griddle, covered, until the bottoms brown and the centers set. Serve them warm with coffee.

Authentic touch. Keep the cakes small and sweet, and cook them in batches the way a street pan does. A large unsweetened rice pancake is a different food. This version stays with rice flour.

How it is used

Mofo gasy is street breakfast and a sweet snack. Coffee is the usual partner. It is not a stew bread and not a dessert with sauce, though it is sweet.

Field notes

  • The name mofo gasy means Malagasy bread.
  • Street vendors sell the sweet cakes in the morning.
  • This recipe uses rice flour and is gluten-free if the flour is pure.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups rice flour (about 260 g)
  • 1/3 cup sugar
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 1/2 cups warm water
  • Oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir the rice flour, sugar, yeast, salt, and warm water into a thick batter. Cover the bowl.
  2. Leave it until it is bubbly and visibly risen, about 45 to 75 minutes depending on the room.
  3. Heat a griddle over medium heat and wipe it with oil. Drop small rounds, about a spoon each, leaving space between them.
  4. Cover and cook until the bottoms are brown and the tops are set. Flip once if the top is still wet, and cook briefly.
  5. Serve warm. These are the small sweet street cakes, not a savory rice crepe.

Gluten-free only if the rice flour has no wheat in it. The sugar level makes this a sweet breakfast, which matters if you are limiting sugar.

Eat mofo gasy the morning they are cooked. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat them covered. They dry out quickly. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A wheat-flour mofo gasy is softer and is not gluten-free.
  • More sugar makes a cake closer to a sweet bun. Less sugar tastes grainier.
  • A spoon of mashed banana moistens the batter and changes the flavor.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Coffee
  • A second cake, because they are small
  • Fresh pineapple
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Molasses pancakes

Strong, dark molasses in a tender wheat crumb, bittersweet rather than floral.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Molasses pancakes are a wheat breakfast sweetened with molasses, the sugarcane syrup bought from the store, rather than the sorghum pressed at a nearby mill. In Appalachia both sweeteners were used, and they are not the same product. This cake keeps a strong molasses note in the crumb. Clay, West Virginia, is the map pin in the upland South.

Cultural background

Molasses is a byproduct of sugar making. It was a trade sweetener in mountain stores. Sorghum has its own page because the flavor and the agriculture are different.

Why it tastes this way

Molasses tastes darker and more bitter than sorghum, with a note some people compare to licorice. That bitterness is the flavor, so do not drown it in extra sugar. Soda and buttermilk lift the batter and soften the edge. The cakes brown fast.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter sweetened with molasses.

Authentic touch. Use unsulfured molasses in the batter and serve more at the table if you want it. Sorghum or maple makes a different pancake.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and, if you want it, a thin ribbon of molasses.

Field notes

  • Molasses comes from sugarcane refining. Sorghum syrup comes from sorghum cane pressed nearby.
  • Appalachian cooks used both, and this page is the molasses cake.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups buttermilk
  • 1/4 cup unsulfured molasses, plus more to serve
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  2. Whisk the buttermilk, molasses, egg, and melted butter until the molasses dissolves.
  3. Stir the wet mixture into the flour until just combined.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium-low heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the underside is deep brown and the top is set. Do not wait for a pale cake to darken further or it will scorch.
  6. Serve with butter and more molasses if you want it.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A milder cake uses two tablespoons of molasses instead of a quarter cup.
  • Sorghum in the same amount is the other Appalachian sweetener and belongs to the sorghum page.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Molasses
  • Black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 8 small cheelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Moong dal cheela

A soft yellow-lentil pancake, bright with ginger and green chile, less nutty than a besan cheela.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 small cheelas

History

Moong dal cheela is a North Indian pancake made from soaked split yellow moong, ground and cooked the same day. It is not a besan cheela. The dal is the whole batter, not chickpea flour stirred with water. Rajasthani and other northern kitchens use it as a light breakfast. Andhra pesarattu uses whole green moong and has its own page. This one is the split yellow dal of the north.

Cultural background

Soaking is the only wait. There is no ferment to plan the night before. Ginger in the grind is what makes a plain dal batter taste finished. Jaipur homes serve it with a sharp chutney or a spoon of pickle.

Why it tastes this way

Split yellow moong grinds sweet and mild, with a legume aroma that stays fresher than toasted besan. Ginger and green chile cut that sweetness. Cumin adds a warm note without the thyme-like ajwain of a Delhi gram cheela. The pan dries the surface so the cake tastes toasted at the rim and custardy in the center.

How it is made

Soak yellow moong dal, grind it with ginger and chile, and cook small pancakes at once.

Authentic touch. Drain the dal well before grinding. Extra soak water makes a watery crepe that never browns. The batter should drop from a spoon in a ribbon.

How it is used

Serve warm with cilantro chutney. It is breakfast food, eaten with the hands or a fork the same morning it is ground.

Field notes

  • This cheela is ground moong dal, not chickpea flour.
  • Split yellow moong is the usual dal. Whole green moong is the Andhra pesarattu.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup split yellow moong dal
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 green chile
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and a splash for grinding
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse the dal and soak it for 2 to 3 hours. Drain thoroughly.
  2. Grind with ginger, chile, cumin, salt, and as little water as you need for a thick, smooth batter.
  3. Cook without an overnight ferment. If the batter looks stiff, add a teaspoon of water.
  4. Oil a medium-hot pan. Spread a small ladle into a neat round and cook until the underside is golden.
  5. Flip and cook until the center is set. Repeat with the remaining batter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped spinach folded through the batter
  • A pinch of turmeric for color

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Cilantro chutney
  • A lime wedge
  • Sliced tomato
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Morocco · No chili heat · About 12 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Morocco · North Africa

Moroccan baghrir

Spongy semolina cakes, yeasted and sour-sweet, soaked with butter and honey.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · About 12 small pancakes

History

Baghrir are the thousand-hole semolina pancakes of Morocco, raised with yeast rather than with a long teff ferment. The batter is blended smooth and thin, cooked on one side, and covered in holes that drink a sauce of melted butter and honey. The same pancake is also cooked in Algeria and Tunisia. Semolina is wheat, so this is not a gluten-free bread.

Cultural background

Baghrir belong to breakfast and to the hour when guests drink tea. They are sold at stalls and cooked at home in a small pan. The first cake of the batch is the test: if the holes do not open, the pan is too cool or the batter is too thick.

Why it tastes this way

Fine semolina gives a faint grain sweetness that white flour alone does not. Yeast opens the crumb and leaves a mild ferment flavor. Butter and honey run into the holes, so each bite is rich and sweet on a bread that was not sugary in the batter. Cooking only one side keeps the holes intact.

How it is made

Bloom yeast in warm water, then blend it with fine semolina, a little wheat flour, sugar, and salt until the batter is thin and silky. Rest it until foamy. Cook small rounds in a nonstick pan on one side until the surface is all holes and no wet shine remains. Pour warm butter and honey over the stack.

Authentic touch. Blend the batter until it is perfectly smooth and cook it on one side only. The thousand holes are the dish. A flipped baghrir is just a heavy semolina pancake.

How it is used

Baghrir are breakfast or a sweet snack, often with mint tea. They are eaten with a spoon once the butter and honey have soaked in. Savory toppings are a separate habit and not this recipe.

Field notes

  • English descriptions call them thousand-hole pancakes because the surface should be covered in open holes.
  • Semolina is wheat. This recipe contains wheat, dairy, and honey.
  • The batter is yeasted and cooked on one side. It is not a buckwheat crepe.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups fine semolina (about 240 g)
  • 1/2 cup wheat flour
  • 2 teaspoons instant yeast
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 2 1/2 cups warm water
  • 3 tablespoons unsalted butter
  • 3 tablespoons honey

Steps

  1. Blend the semolina, wheat flour, yeast, sugar, salt, and warm water until the batter is completely smooth and about as thin as cream.
  2. Cover and rest for 30 to 45 minutes, until the surface is foamy. Stir once.
  3. Heat a nonstick pan over medium heat. Do not add a pool of oil. Pour a small ladle of batter and let it spread.
  4. Cook on one side until the top is covered with holes and looks dry. Do not flip. Slide the pancake onto a towel.
  5. Melt the butter with the honey and spoon that sauce over the warm pancakes so it runs into the holes.

Semolina and wheat flour both contain gluten. The finish contains dairy and honey.

Eat baghrir soon after the honey goes on. Plain cooked cakes can be refrigerated and reheated, then sauced. Do not leave buttered cakes out. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Orange-blossom water in the honey is a common home perfume.
  • A longer rest makes a tangier cake. A short rest tastes more of plain yeast.
  • Algerian and Tunisian batches follow the same hole-and-honey idea with local pans.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Mint tea
  • More warm honey, if the first spoon was shy
  • A handful of fresh fruit
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Morocco · No chili heat · 6 thick rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Morocco · North Africa

Moroccan harcha

Crumbly semolina pan bread, buttery and lightly salty, with a toasted crust.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 thick rounds

History

Harcha is a Moroccan semolina pan bread, pressed into rounds and cooked on a griddle. It is not a pourable pancake and not a laminated msemen. Coarse or fine semolina is bound with fat and a little milk, then flattened and toasted in a pan until the crumb is tender at the center and crusty at the rim. It belongs to breakfast and to the tea tray.

Cultural background

Home cooks and café counters both keep harcha for the morning. It is cut into wedges and served warm. The texture is closer to a crumbly cake of semolina than to a folded flatbread. Semolina is the point. A wheat-flour pancake will not do this job.

Why it tastes this way

Semolina tastes sweeter and grittier than white flour, and butter carries that grain flavor. A small amount of sugar, if you use it, stays in the background. The griddle browns the surface so the outside is toasted while the inside stays moist and sandy. Milk softens the crumb.

How it is made

Rub melted butter into semolina with salt, a little sugar, and baking powder. Add warm milk until the dough holds together. Shape thick rounds, cook them on a moderate griddle under a loose cover, and turn them once. Slice into wedges.

Authentic touch. Pat the dough into thick disks. Do not try to pour it. Harcha is a pan bread. Cook it slowly enough that the center sets before the crust burns.

How it is used

Harcha is breakfast or an afternoon snack with mint tea, butter, honey, or cheese. It is not a street crepe and not a stew bread. Honey and extra butter at the table add more dairy and honey on top of a bread that already contains dairy.

Field notes

  • Harcha is semolina pan bread, cooked on a griddle in thick rounds.
  • It is not msemen. There are no thin folded layers.
  • Semolina is a form of wheat, so the bread contains gluten.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fine semolina (about 320 g)
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 cup melted unsalted butter
  • 1/2 cup warm milk, plus a spoon more if needed

Steps

  1. Stir the semolina, salt, sugar, and baking powder in a bowl. Pour in the melted butter and rub it through until the grain looks like damp sand.
  2. Add the warm milk and mix to a soft dough that holds a shape. It should not pour.
  3. Divide the dough into 6 pieces and pat each into a round about a finger thick. This is pan bread, not a batter.
  4. Cook the rounds on a moderate griddle, covered loosely, for several minutes a side. Turn once. The crust should spot and the center should set.
  5. Rest them for a minute, then cut into wedges. Serve warm.

Semolina is wheat. The dough contains butter and milk.

Hold leftovers in a covered tin and reheat on a dry pan. Refrigerate if the kitchen is hot. The crumb dries faster than a loaf of bread. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Oil instead of butter makes a dairy-free pan bread. That is not the version written here.
  • A coarser semolina gives a more granular crumb.
  • A pinch of anise is a regional habit. This recipe leaves it out.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Mint tea
  • Honey
  • Soft cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Morocco · No chili heat · 8 square flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Morocco · North Africa

Msemen

Flaky, lightly salty layered flatbread with toasted semolina between the folds.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 square flatbreads

History

Msemen is a Moroccan griddled flatbread made by oiling and folding a soft semolina dough into a square. It is a laminated dough, not a pourable batter. Street griddles in Marrakesh turn out stacks for breakfast, plain or filled. The plain bread is the one written here. You will also see the name written m'smen.

Cultural background

Msemen is street food and home food at once. A cook stretches the dough thin, brushes it with oil, folds it, and cooks it on a hot plate until the layers blister. Honey comes at the table for a sweet breakfast. A savory filling is a different batch.

Why it tastes this way

The flavor is toasted wheat and semolina, plus the oil that fries a little between the folds. Salt in the dough keeps it from tasting like pastry. Because the dough is laminated rather than poured, the bite is flaky and chewy at the same time. Honey on top is optional sweetness, not a flavor baked into the square.

How it is made

Mix flour, fine semolina, salt, and water into a soft dough and rest it. Divide it, oil each piece, stretch it paper thin, fold it into a square, and griddle it in a lightly oiled pan until both sides are spotted and the layers separate at the edge.

Authentic touch. Stretch the dough thin enough to see the board through the center before you fold it. Thick folds stay bready. This is laminated dough, not a batter you pour into a pan.

How it is used

Plain msemen is breakfast with honey or with cheese and olives. It is also a street snack eaten from a paper square. Filled versions, including those with preserved meat, are variations and change the diet tags.

Field notes

  • Msemen is a laminated dough. It is not a pourable batter.
  • The usual shape is a square, folded so the layers stay inside.
  • The name is also written m'smen.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 1/2 cup fine semolina
  • 1 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water, plus more if the dough is stiff
  • 1/4 cup neutral oil, plus more for folding and the pan

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, semolina, salt, water, and a spoon of oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth. Rest it, covered, for 15 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough into 8 pieces and roll each into a ball. Coat the balls with oil so they do not dry.
  3. Oil the board. Stretch one ball into a very thin rectangle. This is laminated dough, not a batter. Brush it with oil and sprinkle on a pinch of semolina.
  4. Fold the sides inward to make a square, then flatten the square gently. Repeat with the rest.
  5. Cook the squares on a hot griddle filmed with oil, turning once, until both sides are blistered and golden and the edge shows layers.

The dough contains wheat. A meat filling is a different recipe and is not vegetarian.

Eat msemen warm. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat them on a dry skillet so the layers crisp again. Do not store them soaked in honey. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Butter or smen, aged butter, can replace the oil. That version contains dairy.
  • A filling of khlii, preserved meat, makes the bread not vegetarian.
  • An onion and spice filling is a savory street version and is still a folded dough.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Honey
  • Olives and fresh cheese
  • Mint tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Medium · 6 chutney dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Mysore masala dosa

A crisp fermented dosa spread inside with red chile-garlic chutney and a line of potato masala.

45 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 chutney dosas

History

Mysore masala dosa is a restaurant style associated with Mysuru and the wider Karnataka hotel kitchen. The batter is still fermented rice and urad. What marks the plate is a red chutney, usually dried red chile and garlic, spread on the dosa before the potato goes in. This page does not name an inventor or a founding year. The red smear is the distinction from a plain potato masala dosa.

Cultural background

The chutney is cooked or ground smooth enough to paint with the back of a spoon. It should season the sheet, not drown it. Kitchens in Mysuru vary the chile and the amount of garlic. The potato remains the familiar dry masala.

Why it tastes this way

Dried red chile brings heat and a brick-red color that fresh green chile does not. Garlic, raw or lightly cooked depending on the cook, stays sharp against the sour rice batter. Potato and turmeric soften those edges. Because the chutney is spread on the cooked surface, it fries for a few seconds and tastes toasted rather than like a cold sauce.

How it is made

Cook a thin fermented dosa, paint it with red chile chutney, add a strip of potato masala, and fold.

Authentic touch. Spread a thin film of red chutney on the dosa as soon as the top sets, then add potato. A thick layer of chutney burns before the potato warms.

How it is used

Serve immediately, chutney side in, with coconut chutney if you want a cool contrast. The interior is medium-hot.

Field notes

  • The red chutney spread is what makes a masala dosa a Mysore masala dosa.
  • The grain is still fermented rice and urad, the same family as a plain dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 batch fermented rice and urad dosa batter, about 3 cups
  • 4 dried red chiles, stemmed
  • 3 garlic cloves
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil, plus more for cooking
  • Salt
  • 3 boiled potatoes, crumbled
  • 1 small onion, sliced
  • 1/2 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 1/4 teaspoon turmeric
  • 8 curry leaves

Steps

  1. Have bubbly fermented rice and urad batter ready, made as for a plain dosa.
  2. Soak the dried chiles in hot water for 10 minutes. Grind them with garlic, a pinch of salt, and a teaspoon of water to a thick paste.
  3. For the potato, heat a tablespoon of oil, pop the mustard seeds, add curry leaves and onion, then turmeric and potato. Salt it and cook until dry.
  4. Spread a thin dosa on a hot oiled griddle. When the top sets, smear a thin layer of red chutney over it.
  5. Add a line of potato, fold, and cook another half minute so the chutney heats through. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of jaggery on the side for a sweet-hot plate
  • Less dried chile for a milder chutney

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • A ripe banana, a common South Indian breakfast companion
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · 8 pieces

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Navajo frybread

Fried wheat dough, blistered and salty, crisp outside and soft inside.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · 8 pieces

History

Navajo frybread is a food of survival under confinement and commodity rations, not a pre-contact tradition. Wheat flour and fat were issued foods, including during the Long Walk and the 1860s imprisonment at Bosque Redondo, and later commodity programs kept those ingredients in Navajo kitchens. Families still fry the bread at home and at public events. This page gives the plain flour version and does not treat that history as an ancient custom.

Cultural background

Window Rock is the capital of the Navajo Nation and the map pin. The bread is fried, which is why the journal files it as a fritter. Baking powder is part of the later household dough. The written recipe uses oil and water.

Why it tastes this way

Hot oil puffs the dough and makes a blistered crust while the inside stays bready. Salt is the main seasoning. There is no fermented sourness and no corn flavor. Any sweetness comes from a topping, not from the dough in this recipe.

How it is made

Leavened wheat dough fried in hot oil.

Authentic touch. Fry it. A baked disk of the same dough is a different bread. A small slit in the center helps the dough cook evenly. Remember the ration history when you tell it.

How it is used

Hot, as a bread. Beans are a common savory topping. Powdered sugar is a sweet one. Neither topping is required for the dough.

Field notes

  • Frybread came from rationed flour and fat under confinement, including Bosque Redondo, and it is not a pre-contact food.
  • A slit in the center of each disk is a common way to help it fry through.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 4 cups neutral oil, for frying

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, baking powder, and salt. Add the warm water and mix to a soft dough. If it is dry and cracking, add another tablespoon of water.
  2. Cover and rest 10 minutes.
  3. Divide into 8 pieces. Pat each into a disk about 1/2 inch thick and cut a small slit through the center.
  4. Heat 1 inch of oil in a deep skillet to 350°F.
  5. Fry one or two pieces at a time until blistered and golden, about 1 to 2 minutes per side. Do not crowd the pan.
  6. Drain on a rack and serve hot.

Use a deep pan, keep the oil below smoking, and do not leave it. Drain the bread on a rack, not on a pile of paper that can slide into the fat.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some cooks use a spoon of powdered milk in the dough, which adds dairy.
  • Lard was a ration fat. This written recipe uses neutral oil.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Breakfast
  • Cooked beans
  • Powdered sugar
  • Stew, using the bread as a scoop
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 10 soft dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Neer dosa

A soft, pale, almost translucent rice roll with a clean cereal taste and no sour ferment.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 soft dosas

History

Neer dosa belongs to Mangaluru and the Tulu coast of Karnataka. Neer means water, which matches the batter: soaked rice ground with enough water to look like thin rice milk. No fermentation is required. The dosa is cooked quickly, stays flexible, and is rolled rather than crisped into a wafer. It is a coastal rice bread, separate from the fermented rice-and-urad dosa of the inland hotel menu.

Cultural background

Tulu and other coastal kitchens serve it with coconut chutney, a chicken or fish curry, or a simple scrape of jaggery and grated coconut. This recipe keeps the dosa itself free of fish and meat so the tags match the written batter.

Why it tastes this way

Without urad and without a ferment, there is no tang and no lacy wheat crust. Freshly ground rice tastes sweet and mild. The extra water lets the sheet set thin and pliable instead of brittle. Coconut, if you add a little to the grind, rounds the flavor the way coastal cooking often does, with the fat of the nut rather than with ghee.

How it is made

Soak rice, grind it with plenty of water to a watery batter, and cook soft rounds that can be rolled at once.

Authentic touch. Use a warm, not screaming-hot, pan and spread with the back of the ladle in one or two strokes. The dosa is done when the top turns from shiny to matte. Browning it hard dries it out.

How it is used

Stack them under a clean cloth so they stay flexible. Serve with coconut chutney. A fish curry is a common Mangaluru partner and would add a fish allergen.

Field notes

  • Neer dosa does not need an overnight ferment. The batter is cooked the day it is ground.
  • The sheet is supposed to bend. A cracker-crisp result is a different dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 1 1/4 cups water, plus more for grinding
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon grated coconut, optional
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse the rice and soak it in water for 3 to 4 hours. Soaking is waiting, not active prep.
  2. Drain and grind the rice with the coconut, if using, and fresh water until the batter is completely smooth and as thin as light cream.
  3. Stir in the salt. The batter should pour in a steady stream. Add water if the grind is thick.
  4. Heat a nonstick or well-seasoned pan over medium heat. Grease it lightly. Pour a ladle of batter and tilt the pan, or spread once, to make a thin round.
  5. Cover for a few seconds if the top looks wet. When the surface is matte and the edge lifts, roll the dosa off. Keep the rolls covered while you finish the batch.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A small piece of coconut ground with the rice
  • A pinch of salt only, which is the plainest home version

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Jaggery and fresh coconut
  • A vegetable coconut curry
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

New England cornmeal cakes

Coarse corn and molasses against a tangy buttermilk crumb.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 cakes

History

New England cornmeal cakes mix cornmeal with wheat flour and molasses, a Yankee breakfast that uses both the region's cornmeal habit and molasses from Atlantic trade. They are thicker and sweeter than a Rhode Island johnnycake. Portsmouth, New Hampshire, is the map pin for a coastal New England kitchen. The wheat flour means these cakes are not gluten-free.

Cultural background

Molasses was a common sweetener in New England because it moved through northern ports. The cornmeal gives the crumb a sandy texture that all-wheat pancakes do not have.

Why it tastes this way

Cornmeal toasts faster than wheat and tastes fuller. Molasses adds bitterness and iron-dark sweetness. Buttermilk and soda keep the cake from tasting heavy even with the coarser meal.

How it is made

Griddled cornmeal and wheat batter sweetened with molasses.

Authentic touch. Let the cornmeal sit in the buttermilk for 10 minutes before you add the egg and flour, so the meal swells. Skip that soak and the cakes taste gritty.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and maple syrup or more molasses.

Field notes

  • Molasses in New England cooking follows Atlantic trade into northern ports.
  • Soaking the cornmeal briefly softens the grit before the cakes hit the griddle.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup yellow cornmeal
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 2 tablespoons molasses
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal, buttermilk, and molasses. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Whisk the flour, baking powder, baking soda, and salt in another bowl.
  3. Stir the egg and melted butter into the cornmeal, then add the flour mixture and stir until just combined.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the tops are set and the undersides are brown. Cook until the center is done.
  6. Serve with butter and maple syrup or molasses.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • All-cornmeal johnnycakes are the Rhode Island page, and they are a different texture.
  • A spoon less molasses makes them milder.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup
  • Molasses
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 hotcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

New Mexico blue corn hotcakes

Toasted blue corn in a tender buttermilk cake, nutty and a little sweet.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 hotcakes

History

New Mexico blue corn hotcakes are a cafe and home breakfast that mixes blue cornmeal into a wheat batter. Blue corn is a Southwestern crop with a long history in Pueblo farming, while a thick hotcake with wheat flour, buttermilk, and egg is a later plate. It is not Hopi piki, which is a paper-thin sheet on a stone. Santa Fe is the map pin.

Cultural background

Blue cornmeal tastes toastier than yellow meal and colors the batter gray-blue or lavender. The wheat flour and buttermilk make a risen cake, which is why this page is not gluten-free and not a piki recipe.

Why it tastes this way

Blue corn has a deeper, toastier flavor than a standard yellow meal. Buttermilk softens that flavor and lifts the cake. Butter browns the surface. Syrup will cover the corn if you pour it on heavily, so the corn shows up best with a light hand.

How it is made

Griddled blue-cornmeal and wheat batter.

Authentic touch. Use blue cornmeal, not food coloring in a yellow-meal batter. Keep this cake distinct from piki: it is thick, wheat-based, and cooked like a pancake.

How it is used

Stacked, with butter and a light pour of syrup.

Field notes

  • Blue corn in New Mexico is an old Pueblo crop. The thick wheat hotcake is a later cafe breakfast.
  • Hopi piki is a different food: a paper-thin sheet, not this stack.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup blue cornmeal
  • 3/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the blue cornmeal, flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  2. Stir in the buttermilk, eggs, and melted butter until just combined. Rest 5 minutes.
  3. Butter a griddle over medium heat.
  4. Pour 1/4 cup cakes. They brown a little faster than all-wheat pancakes.
  5. Flip when the top is set and cook until the center springs back.
  6. Serve with butter and a light pour of syrup so the corn still shows.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A higher share of blue cornmeal makes a more fragile, stronger-tasting cake.
  • This batter cannot be made gluten-free by swapping in more cornmeal unless the wheat flour is removed and the recipe is rebuilt.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup or honey only if honey fits the table
  • Piñon only as an optional tree-nut topping
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · About 18 fritters

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

New Orleans calas

Fried rice fritters, lightly sweet, with nutmeg and a powdered-sugar coat.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · About 18 fritters

History

New Orleans calas are fried rice fritters sold for generations by Black vendors, many of them women, who carried the cakes through the streets. Rice growing in Louisiana depended on the skill of enslaved West Africans and on the plantation economy built on their labor. This page does not claim that one West African village recipe became calas. The fritter that can be documented is the Creole rice cake sold in the city.

Cultural background

Older calas were yeasted. This recipe uses a short rise and then fries the batter. Cooked rice is the body. Wheat flour and egg bind it. The written recipe has no milk.

Why it tastes this way

Rice stays in soft grains inside a crisp fried shell. Nutmeg is the spice most associated with the fritter, used lightly. Sugar in the batter and powdered sugar afterward make them a sweet street breakfast. Yeast gives a mild ferment flavor if the batter actually rises. If it does not rest, the fritters taste heavy.

How it is made

Yeasted cooked-rice batter fried in oil and dusted with powdered sugar.

Authentic touch. Fry them. A baked rice muffin is not a calas. Dust them with powdered sugar while they are hot, which is how street calas were eaten.

How it is used

Hot, as a breakfast or a snack, with powdered sugar.

Field notes

  • Calas were sold on New Orleans streets by Black vendors, often women.
  • The rice in Louisiana cooking is tied to enslaved West African skill, and this page does not rename one village recipe as calas.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups cooked medium-grain white rice, cooled
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/4 teaspoon grated nutmeg
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 6 cups neutral oil, for frying
  • 1/2 cup powdered sugar, for serving

Steps

  1. Mash the rice with a fork or your hand so some grains break and some remain.
  2. Stir in the yeast, sugar, salt, nutmeg, eggs, and flour. The batter should be thick and spoonable.
  3. Cover and let it rise in a warm place until puffy, about 1 hour. That rise is not added to the active prep time.
  4. Heat 1 1/2 inches of oil in a deep pan to 350°F.
  5. Slip heaping tablespoons of batter into the oil. Fry until deep golden, turning once, about 2 to 3 minutes.
  6. Drain on a rack, dust with powdered sugar, and serve hot.

Fry in enough oil to cover the fritters halfway or more, and do not leave the pot. Keep the heat at a steady 350°F.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Baking powder in place of yeast is a quicker home batter and will taste less fermented.
  • A splash of milk is a later enrichment and would add dairy.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Powdered sugar
  • Coffee
  • A little more nutmeg
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Canada · No chili heat · 12 toutons

Flying Pancakes of the World

Canada · North America

Newfoundland toutons

Fried bread dough, crisp at the rim, with salt pork and a side of molasses.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 toutons

History

Toutons are pieces of bread dough fried for breakfast in Newfoundland, often in salt pork fat and almost always with molasses at the table. Scrunchions, the crisp bits of salt pork, are part of that plate. Saint John's is the map pin for an island-wide habit. The dough here is a simple yeast dough, and the rise happens in the steps rather than in a multi-day prep time.

Cultural background

Toutons use the same kind of dough as a plain white loaf. Frying is what makes them toutons. Molasses on the hot bread is the usual sweetness. This written recipe fries them in rendered salt pork, so it contains meat.

Why it tastes this way

Pork fat browns the dough and adds salt. The inside stays soft because the pieces are fried only long enough to color and heat through. Molasses is bitter-sweet and runs into the fried face. A dough fried in a dry pan without fat will toughen instead of crisp.

How it is made

Yeast dough pinched into pieces and fried in rendered salt pork fat.

Authentic touch. Fry the pieces in rendered salt pork fat and serve molasses on the side. Butter is a home substitute and would make a different allergen list.

How it is used

Hot, with dark molasses.

Field notes

  • Toutons are fried bread dough, a Newfoundland breakfast, commonly served with molasses.
  • Salt pork fat is the usual frying medium, and the crisp bits are called scrunchions.

Ingredients

  • 2 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup warm water
  • 4 ounces salt pork, diced
  • 1/2 cup molasses, to serve

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, yeast, sugar, and salt. Add the warm water and mix to a soft dough. Knead a few times in the bowl.
  2. Cover and let the dough rise until puffy, about 1 hour. That rise is unattended time, not extra active prep.
  3. While the dough rises, cook the diced salt pork in a skillet over medium-low heat until the fat renders and the bits are crisp. Scoop the bits out and leave about 3 tablespoons of fat in the pan.
  4. Pinch off pieces of dough and flatten them into small rounds.
  5. Fry in the pork fat over medium heat until both sides are golden and the center is cooked, about 2 to 3 minutes per side.
  6. Serve hot with molasses and the crisp pork bits.

Render the pork slowly and pour off excess fat before it smokes. Do not leave the pan.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leftover risen bread dough is the practical Newfoundland start when you already have a loaf going.
  • Frying in butter instead of salt pork removes the meat and adds dairy.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Molasses
  • A cup of tea
  • The crisp salt-pork bits from the pan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Nicaragua · No chili heat · 8 güirilas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Nicaragua · Mexico & Central America

Nicaraguan güirilas

Thick young-corn pancakes with cuajada folded in, savory and a little milky, barely sweet.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 güirilas

History

Güirilas belong to Nicaragua, especially the northern highlands, where young corn comes in and cuajada is on the table. The cake is a fresh-corn pancake with curd cheese in the batter. A chorreada across the border in Costa Rica is a different breakfast, usually sweeter and finished with sour cream. Neither cake is masa. Both sit with the older corn foods of the isthmus, which predate wheat hotcakes. This recipe keeps the cheese inside the güirila and leaves the sugar out.

Cultural background

In Matagalpa and nearby highland towns, güirilas are a seasonal breakfast and a snack when maíz tierno, young corn, is being cut. Cuajada is a fresh curd, mild and a bit crumbly, not a long-aged cheese. The cake is thicker than a crepe and smaller than a stuffed arepa.

Why it tastes this way

Young corn is juicier and less starchy than a dried kernel, so the pancake tastes green and sweet even with no sugar added. Cuajada melts only partly and leaves salty, milky curds in the crumb. Egg sets the cake. A spoon of untreated fine cornmeal, if the blend is thin, disappears into that texture. You should taste corn and cheese, not a wheat griddle and not nixtamal.

How it is made

Pulse young corn with egg, a little milk, and salt. Fold in crumbled cuajada and a spoon of fine cornmeal if needed. Cook thick rounds on a buttered skillet.

Authentic touch. Grind the corn coarse and fold the cuajada in at the end so it stays in pieces. Cook the pancakes thicker than a chorreada and more slowly, until the center loses its raw shine. Do not sweeten the bowl.

How it is used

Serve them warm, with more cuajada on the side if you have it, and coffee. They are breakfast, not a dessert crepe.

Field notes

  • Güirilas are Nicaraguan. Chorreadas are Costa Rican. Both use fresh corn, and they are not the same cake.
  • Cuajada is a fresh curd cheese. It stays in soft pieces inside the pancake.
  • This batter is not nixtamalized masa and not a wheat hotcake.

Ingredients

  • 4 cups young corn kernels
  • 100 g cuajada or other fresh unsalted curd cheese, crumbled
  • 1 egg
  • 2 tablespoons milk
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon fine cornmeal, not masa harina
  • Butter for the skillet

Steps

  1. Pulse the corn, egg, milk, and salt until coarse and thick, not smooth.
  2. Stir in the crumbled cuajada and the cornmeal. The batter should hold a spoon.
  3. Heat a skillet over medium-low heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon thick rounds. Cook slowly until the underside browns and the top loses its wet shine, then flip and cook through.
  5. Serve warm, with more cuajada if you want it.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of sugar is a home choice. The highland version here stays unsweetened.
  • Costa Rican chorreadas are the fresh-corn pancake without cuajada in the batter, usually served with natilla.
  • Masa harina is the wrong sack. The corn has to be fresh and young.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Extra cuajada
  • Black coffee
  • A spoon of sour cream, which is closer to the Costa Rican plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Nigeria · No chili heat · About 16 molded cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Nigeria · West Africa

Nigerian masa

Mildly sour rice cakes with crisp rims and a soft, slightly sweet center.

30 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · About 16 molded cakes

History

Masa is the Hausa fermented rice cake cooked in a dimpled pan. Waina is another name for it. Rice is soaked, ground, and left to sour, then spooned into oiled cups so each cake takes a round, puffed shape. In Kano and across Hausa kitchens it is breakfast and street food, eaten with sugar, honey, or a soup. This recipe is rice only, so it can be gluten-free.

Cultural background

The pan matters as much as the ferment. Traditional cups are set into a wide clay or metal pan. A home cook can use a heavy dimpled pan if the cups are well oiled. The cake is a snack you buy by the piece as well as a dish made for the house.

Why it tastes this way

Fermentation takes the raw starch edge off the rice and leaves a light sourness. A little sugar in the batter keeps it in breakfast territory. The oiled mold fries the rim while the covered center stays tender. It does not taste like a wheat pancake, because there is no wheat.

How it is made

Soak rice, grind it with water into a thick batter, and ferment it until slightly sour. Stir in salt, sugar, and a pinch of yeast if the kitchen is too cool for a wild ferment. Oil the dimples of a masa pan and cook spoonfuls, turning once, until both sides are golden.

Authentic touch. Use a dimpled pan and oil the cups. Waina and masa are the same family of cakes. Do not add wheat flour if you are keeping the gluten-free tag.

How it is used

Masa is breakfast and a street snack. Sugar or honey goes on top, or the cakes are eaten with miyan taushe, a pumpkin soup, or another Hausa soup. Honey and soup are not in the batter.

Field notes

  • Waina is another name for these Hausa rice cakes.
  • They are cooked in a dimpled pan, not poured as a wide flatbread.
  • A rice-only batter can be gluten-free if the yeast and the pan are not carrying wheat.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups raw rice (about 400 g), soaked 4 hours and drained
  • 1 1/2 cups water, for grinding
  • 1/2 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • Oil for the dimples

Steps

  1. Grind the drained rice with the water to a thick, slightly grainy batter. Stir in the yeast and sugar. Leave the salt out.
  2. Cover and rest the batter for 4 to 8 hours, until it looks bubbly and smells faintly sour. This is a fermented rice batter.
  3. Stir in the salt. The batter should drop from a spoon in a heavy ribbon. Thin it with a spoon of water if it stands up in peaks.
  4. Oil the cups of a dimpled masa pan and heat them until the oil shimmers. Fill each cup about two-thirds full.
  5. Cover and cook until the bottoms are golden, then turn each cake and cook the second side. Serve warm. Waina is the same cake under another name.

Gluten-free only if the rice, yeast, and pan have no wheat on them. Hot oil in the dimples can spit.

Refrigerate leftover masa and reheat in a lightly oiled pan. The rims soften in a covered box. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Waina is another name, not a different grain.
  • A wild ferment without added yeast takes longer and tastes a little sharper.
  • Wheat flour stirred in for softness removes the gluten-free claim.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Street food
  • A sprinkle of sugar
  • Honey
  • Miyan taushe, pumpkin soup
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · Mild · 8 memelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Oaxacan memelas

An elongated masa cake spread with oil, black beans, and a mild salsa, toasted at the edges.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 memelas

History

Memelas are an Oaxacan comal cake: masa patted into a long oval, cooked, then brushed with fat and salsa. In many Oaxacan kitchens the fat is asiento, the soft drippings left after pork is rendered, or lard. This recipe uses neutral vegetable oil instead, so the plate stays vegetarian and vegan and the allergen list has no meat. The dough is still nixtamalized masa, a corn food older in Mexico than wheat hotcakes. A memela is longer than a sope and is not split like a gordita.

Cultural background

On a Oaxacan breakfast table, memelas sit beside eggs, beans, and salsa, or they are eaten from a market comal. The shape is elongated on purpose. A round sope from another state is a different antojito.

Why it tastes this way

The masa toasts along the thin edges and stays soft in the thicker middle. Oil carries the aroma of the salsa into the surface. Black beans add an earthy layer. The salsa, tomato and a small chile, is acidic and only mildly hot. Without asiento, you lose the porky depth those drippings give, and you taste the corn and the salsa more directly.

How it is made

Griddle oval masa cakes, brush with vegetable oil, spread a thin layer of black beans and salsa, and toast them again until the edges crisp.

Authentic touch. Pat the dough into a long oval, not a circle. Brush the oil on after the first cook, then return the memela to the comal so the salsa dries a little and sticks.

How it is used

Eat them for breakfast or as a market snack, with extra salsa if you want it. If you switch the oil for asiento, say so, because the dish is then made with pork.

Field notes

  • Asiento is pork fat, not a spice. This page leaves it out and uses vegetable oil.
  • Memelas are elongated. Sopes are smaller rounds with a pinched rim.
  • The masa is nixtamalized. Plain cornmeal will not stretch into the same oval.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 320 ml warm water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons neutral vegetable oil
  • 200 g cooked black beans, mashed smooth
  • 2 tomatoes, 1 jalapeño, and a pinch of salt, chopped into a mild salsa

Steps

  1. Mix the masa harina, salt, and warm water. Knead, cover, and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Divide into 8 pieces and pat each into an elongated oval, thicker in the center.
  3. Cook on a dry comal over medium heat until both sides are set and spotted, about 3 minutes a side.
  4. Brush each memela with vegetable oil. Spread a thin layer of black beans and a spoon of salsa.
  5. Return them to the comal for a minute so the toppings heat and the edges crisp. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Asiento or lard in place of vegetable oil is the older Oaxacan spread. It adds meat and is not vegetarian or vegan.
  • Crumbled fresh cheese is a common finish. It adds dairy.
  • A red salsa de chile pasilla oaxaqueño is a local sauce. Use a mild amount if you want to keep the heat down.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Black coffee or hot chocolate
  • Eggs cooked in salsa, on the side
  • Sliced avocado
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Russia · No chili heat · 12 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Oladyi

Small thick Russian pancakes on kefir, quicker than yeasted blini.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 small pancakes

History

Oladyi are small thick Russian pancakes, usually raised with kefir or sour milk and a little soda rather than a long yeast sponge. They are breakfast and snack food, not the ceremonial yeast blini of the Maslenitsa page, though both are pancakes. Apple oladyi are a common sweet variation. This recipe is the plain kefir cake.

Cultural background

They are home cooking, dropped from a spoon into a buttered pan. Sour cream is the topping. They do not need a butter-week setting to make sense.

Why it tastes this way

Kefir makes them tangy and tender. The crumb is thicker than a blin, so the inside stays soft while the outside browns. Sugar is light. Sour cream echoes the fermented milk in the batter. They taste fresher and less yeasty than a risen blin.

How it is made

Flour, egg, kefir, soda, sugar, and salt are mixed and cooked at once as small thick cakes in butter.

Authentic touch. Use kefir or a real sour milk, not sweet drinking yogurt if you can help it. Cook them small. A yeast blin is the other page.

How it is used

Warm, with sour cream, at breakfast or as a snack. Jam makes them a dessert.

Field notes

  • Oladyi are the small kefir pancakes, not the yeasted Maslenitsa blini.
  • They are cooked as soon as the batter is mixed.
  • Sour cream is the usual topping.

Ingredients

  • 200 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1/2 tsp baking soda
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 300 ml kefir
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking soda, and salt. Beat in the egg and kefir until just smooth.
  2. Cook spoonfuls in butter over medium heat until the tops set, then turn.
  3. Cook until the centers spring back.
  4. Serve with sour cream.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Diced apple folded into the batter
  • Yeasted blini, which are the butter-week page

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Dessert
  • Sour cream
  • Jam
  • A spoon of honey, which adds a honey allergen
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 8 onion dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Onion rava dosa

Crisp semolina lace crowded with sweet onion, green chile, and curry leaf.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 onion dosas

History

Onion rava dosa keeps the unfermented semolina batter and puts onion in the sheet itself. Udupi hotel kitchens, which sent many South Indian breakfasts to other cities, treat it as its own dosa on the board, next to a plain rava dosa. The onion is mixed through or scattered on the pour so it fries into the lace. It is still wheat semolina, not a fermented rice and urad dosa with onions on the side.

Cultural background

Cut the onion small enough to cook in the time the lace crisps. Large raw chunks steam and leave pale pockets. A pinch of ginger is common in Udupi batters and keeps the onion from tasting flat.

Why it tastes this way

Onion fries in the oil at the edge of the batter and turns sweet. Semolina still supplies the wheat toast and the brittle holes. Chile and curry leaf cut that sweetness. Because there is no overnight souring, the onion stays bright instead of fading into a fermented tang.

How it is made

Mix a thin semolina and rice-flour batter, fold in plenty of onion, and pour it loosely on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Scatter the onion as you pour, then leave the pan alone until the rim lifts. Stirring after that pulls the lace apart.

How it is used

Serve open-faced so the onion stays visible, with coconut chutney. Folding is fine once the sheet is crisp.

Field notes

  • Onion rava dosa is still a semolina batter. The onion does not change the grain.
  • Udupi restaurants often list it apart from both plain dosa and plain rava dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup fine wheat semolina
  • 1/3 cup rice flour
  • 1 1/2 cups cold water
  • 1 medium onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1 teaspoon grated ginger
  • 10 curry leaves, chopped
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir semolina, rice flour, salt, and water until smooth and thin. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Fold in onion, chile, ginger, curry leaves, and cumin. The batter should look busy with onion and still pour from a ladle.
  3. Heat a griddle well. Oil it. Pour the batter in ribbons so holes remain, carrying onion along with each pour.
  4. Add extra onion to any bare spot. Drizzle oil and cook until the onion browns and the sheet snaps at the edge.
  5. Remove it whole. Stir the bowl before the next ladle so the onion does not settle at the bottom.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A teaspoon of grated ginger in the batter
  • A few cashews on one dosa, which adds tree nut

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • A small bowl of sugar and ghee for people who want a sweet bite
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 6 green cheelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Palak cheela

A green spinach and gram-flour pancake, savory with ginger and cumin, soft rather than crisp.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 green cheelas

History

Palak cheela is a North Indian gram-flour pancake with spinach ground or finely chopped into the batter. Besan is still the structure. The spinach is the vegetable, not a dye from somewhere else. Kitchens from Punjab through Delhi make it as a breakfast that uses a bunch of greens. It is unfermented. It is not the plain onion besan cheela, and it is not a moong dal cake.

Cultural background

Chandigarh markets sell spinach through the cool months, which is when this cheela tastes sweetest. The leaf should be washed well. Grit in the batter is the usual failure, not the spice. A mild green chile is enough.

Why it tastes this way

Spinach sweetens and loosens besan, and it turns the crumb green and moist. Ginger keeps the green from tasting grassy in a raw way. Cumin is the savory line. Because the cake is thicker and wetter than a plain cheela, the chickpea flavor is softer and the spinach reads in every bite.

How it is made

Blend spinach with besan, ginger, and spices, then cook small green pancakes on a moderate pan.

Authentic touch. Squeeze the cooked or very finely chopped spinach so the batter does not become a soup. Extra leaf water steams the cake and stops it browning.

How it is used

Serve hot with a spoon of pickle or a dairy-free chutney. It is a savory breakfast.

Field notes

  • Palak is spinach. The flour is still besan.
  • The cake is mixed and cooked at once, with no ferment.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups spinach leaves, washed
  • 1 cup besan (chickpea flour), with no wheat added
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 green chile
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water only if the batter is stiff
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Blend spinach, ginger, and green chile to a coarse puree.
  2. Stir in besan, cumin, and salt. Add a spoon of water only if you cannot spread the batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Oil a pan over medium heat. Spread a small round.
  4. Cook until the underside browns and the top loses its raw shine. Flip and cook through so the besan is done.
  5. Repeat. The green darkens as it cooks, which is expected.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A handful of fenugreek leaves with the spinach
  • Grated carrot for sweetness

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Mint chutney
  • Lime
  • A side of yogurt, which is dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 4 very large dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Paper dosa

A very large, very thin fermented rice sheet that shatters at the edge and tastes toasted.

40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 very large dosas

History

Paper dosa is a size and a cook, not a different grain. The batter is the fermented rice and urad batter of a plain dosa. The cook spreads it across a wide griddle until the sheet is almost translucent, then keeps it on the heat until the rim turns brittle. Restaurant menus sometimes call that spread a paper roast. The spectacle is the diameter. The cereal is unchanged.

Cultural background

Homes with a smaller pan still make a paper-style dosa by using less batter and a hotter surface. The restaurant version is larger because the griddle is larger. Either way, the test is thinness and a dry crackle, not a new recipe of flours.

Why it tastes this way

More of the batter touches iron, so toast and a light ferment sourness dominate. There is almost no soft center to cushion the taste. Oil at the edge fries the lace. Because the grain is still rice and urad, the aroma matches a plain dosa that has been cooked further and thinner.

How it is made

Spread fermented rice and urad batter very thin on a broad hot griddle and cook it until the sheet is crisp enough to crack.

Authentic touch. Use less batter than pride suggests, and spread in one confident spiral. A second pass piles batter back onto the center and ruins the paper thickness.

How it is used

Bring it to the table whole, standing or folded once, with chutney and sambar. It is a shared breakfast more than a stuffed one.

Field notes

  • Paper dosa does not introduce wheat, rava, or millet. It is a thin cook of rice and urad batter.
  • The wide restaurant griddle is why the famous ones look longer than a home dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup idli rice
  • 1/3 cup skinned urad dal
  • 1/4 teaspoon fenugreek seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for a wide griddle

Steps

  1. Soak rice, and soak urad with fenugreek, in separate bowls for 4 to 6 hours. Grind the urad fluffy and the rice fine. Mix with salt.
  2. Ferment until bubbly and mildly sour, commonly 8 to 14 hours. Active prep does not include that rest. Discard spoiled batter.
  3. Loosen the batter with water until it spreads easily in a thin film. Heat a wide griddle until a water drop dances.
  4. Grease lightly. Pour a small ladle and spread it at once to the full width of the pan, as thin as the batter will allow.
  5. Oil the edge and cook without flipping until the sheet is pale gold, dry, and brittle at the rim. Fold once and serve.

Discard batter that is moldy or smells putrid.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A brush of oil only, no filling
  • Ghee on the last ten seconds of cooking, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Molaga podi with sesame oil
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Paraguay · No chili heat · 1 large cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Paraguay · South America

Paraguayan mbeju

A crisp griddled cake of manioc starch, cornmeal, and cheese, salty at the edge and tender in the middle.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 1 large cake

History

Mbeju is a Paraguayan breakfast cake made from manioc starch, corn flour, and cheese, cooked on a griddle until the edge crisps. Manioc, cassava, is an old staple along the Paraguay River. The starch in a modern kitchen is commercial, already processed. This recipe uses that starch and does not teach you to grind raw cassava. Cheese and butter make it a dairy dish. It is often eaten with cocido, a hot yerba mate infusion, which is a drink beside the plate and not an ingredient in the dough.

Cultural background

In Asunción and in the countryside, mbeju is breakfast and an afternoon bite, sometimes cooked in a broad pan so several people can break pieces off. The dough is crumbly when it goes into the pan and sets as the starch heats. Corn flour keeps it from being only elastic tapioca.

Why it tastes this way

Manioc starch goes chewy and then crisp as it dries on the griddle. Cornmeal adds a toasted grain note. Fresh cheese salts the cake and browns in spots. Butter helps the edge fry. There is no sugar and no chile, so the flavor stays savory and mild. Cocido beside it is smoky and bitter, which is why the two share a table.

How it is made

Rub commercial manioc starch with cornmeal, grated fresh cheese, butter, salt, and a splash of milk. Press the crumb into a skillet and cook slowly until the bottom crisps and the center sets. Flip once if it will hold.

Authentic touch. Mix the starch, cornmeal, cheese, and butter to a damp crumb, not a pourable batter. Press it into a hot pan and let it set before you touch it. Turning too soon breaks the cake. The cheese should be fresh and salty, not a hard aged cheese that stays in pebbles.

How it is used

Break it into wedges at breakfast, with cocido or coffee. It is a bread and a cheese dish at the same time.

Field notes

  • Use commercial manioc starch. Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous, and this recipe does not teach home processing.
  • Mbeju is starch, corn, and cheese on a griddle, a Paraguayan breakfast.
  • Cocido is the drink beside it, not a liquid in the dough.

Ingredients

  • 150 g commercial manioc starch (cassava starch)
  • 80 g fine cornmeal
  • 100 g grated fresh cheese
  • 30 g butter
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons milk

Steps

  1. Rub the manioc starch, cornmeal, cheese, butter, and salt together until the mixture looks like damp crumbs. Sprinkle on the milk and mix. It should clump, not pour.
  2. Heat a skillet over medium-low heat and press the crumb in an even layer.
  3. Cook without stirring until the bottom is crisp and the center has set, about 10 minutes.
  4. Flip in one piece if you can, or cut it in the pan and turn the pieces. Cook until the second side spots.
  5. Serve warm, in wedges.

Use commercial manioc (cassava) starch. Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This recipe does not teach home processing of raw cassava.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pork fat instead of butter is used in some kitchens. It adds meat and is not vegetarian.
  • More cornmeal makes a mealier cake. More starch makes it chewier.
  • An egg in the dough adds the egg allergen and is not required here.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Cocido, hot yerba mate
  • Coffee with milk
  • A slice of tomato
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 potato cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Pennsylvania Dutch potato pancakes

Shredded potato, onion, and pepper, crisp at the edge and soft in the middle.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 potato cakes

History

Pennsylvania Dutch potato pancakes are shredded-potato cakes griddled by German-speaking communities in Pennsylvania. Pennsylvania Dutch means Deutsch, not a recipe from the Netherlands. The cakes are cooked on a griddle, with crisp edges and a soft center. Lancaster County is the map pin.

Cultural background

Potato cakes traveled with German-speaking cooks and became everyday food, not only a holiday plate. They are related to other shredded-potato cakes and are not the same page as a Jewish latke.

Why it tastes this way

Potato starch that stays in the bowl is part of what holds the cake together. Rinsing the shreds until the water runs clear can make them fall apart. Onion browns at the edge and keeps the potato from tasting flat. The griddle, with only a thin film of oil, fries the surface without deep-frying the center.

How it is made

Shredded potato and onion griddled in a thin film of oil.

Authentic touch. Griddle them, do not deep-fry them. Squeeze excess water from the potatoes and leave the white starch in the bowl.

How it is used

Hot, with applesauce or sour cream if you want dairy at the table. The cakes themselves have no milk or butter.

Field notes

  • Pennsylvania Dutch comes from Deutsch, meaning German, not from the Netherlands.
  • These cakes are griddled. The funnel cake from the same communities is the fried one.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 pounds russet potatoes
  • 1 small yellow onion
  • 2 large eggs
  • 3 tablespoons all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1/4 teaspoon black pepper
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil

Steps

  1. Peel the potatoes and grate them on the large holes of a box grater. Grate the onion the same way.
  2. Squeeze the mixture in a towel over a bowl. Pour off the water that collects and keep the white starch at the bottom of the bowl.
  3. Stir the potato, onion, starch, eggs, flour, salt, and pepper together.
  4. Heat a thin film of oil in a skillet over medium heat.
  5. Drop 1/4 cup mounds, flatten them, and cook until the underside is deep brown, about 3 to 4 minutes. Flip and cook until the center is tender.
  6. Add oil as needed. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Add a spoon of minced parsley for a greener cake.
  • Flour can be reduced if the potatoes are very starchy, but do not rinse the starch away.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Applesauce
  • Sour cream, if you eat dairy
  • Chives
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Peru · Mild · 2 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Peru · South America

Peruvian tacu-tacu

A pan-fried cake of leftover rice and beans, garlicky, with a crisp face and mild ají amarillo.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 cakes

History

Tacu-tacu is a cake of leftover rice and beans, fried in a skillet until the outside is crisp. It is Lima criollo cooking. Afro-Peruvian cooks helped build that coastal cuisine in the colonial city and afterward, working with Indigenous ingredients and Spanish techniques in kitchens shaped by slavery and, later, by free Black communities. The dish itself is practical: the previous day's rice and beans, garlic, onion, and ají amarillo, pressed into a hot pan. A fried egg or a breaded steak is a common addition. Those are not in this recipe.

Cultural background

Criollo cooking is the coastal home cooking of Lima and other Peruvian port cities. Tacu-tacu is what a cook does with rice and beans that are already seasoned and a little dry. It is lunch or a heavy breakfast. This version stays with the rice and beans so the plate can be vegan.

Why it tastes this way

Day-old rice fries harder and crisper than fresh rice, which is why leftovers are the right start. Beans break into the grains and salt the cake. Garlic and onion, cooked first, perfume the oil. Ají amarillo is fruity and mildly hot, enough to set the heat at mild without burning. There is no cheese and no egg in the cake, so the flavor stays with the beans, the rice, and the chile.

How it is made

Fry onion and garlic, add ají amarillo, cooked beans, and cooked rice. Mash part of the mixture, press it into a cake in the skillet, and fry until a crust forms on both sides.

Authentic touch. Use rice and beans that are dry enough to mash and hold a shape. Wet rice steams and never crusts. Press the cake into the pan and leave it alone until the bottom is brown. Then flip once. Stirring makes fried rice, not tacu-tacu.

How it is used

Slide the cake onto a plate for lunch or breakfast. A fried egg on top is a different plate and adds egg. Breaded steak adds meat. The cake here is the rice and beans alone.

Field notes

  • Tacu-tacu is leftover rice and beans fried into a cake, a Lima criollo dish.
  • Afro-Peruvian cooks were part of building that coastal kitchen. The page stays with the dish and that history.
  • Let the cake sit in the pan long enough to crust. Stirring it turns it into fried rice.

Ingredients

  • 3 cups cooked white rice, cold
  • 2 cups cooked canary or black beans, drained
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 2 garlic cloves, minced
  • 1 tablespoon ají amarillo paste
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil

Steps

  1. Heat 1 tablespoon oil and cook the onion and garlic until soft. Stir in the ají amarillo paste.
  2. Add the beans and mash about half of them into the onion. Fold in the rice and salt. The mixture should hold together.
  3. Heat the remaining oil in a skillet over medium heat. Add half the mixture and press it into a thick cake.
  4. Cook without stirring until the bottom is brown, then flip and brown the other side. Repeat with the rest.
  5. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Canary beans, black beans, or lentils all appear in Lima kitchens. Keep them cooked and dry.
  • A breaded steak beside the cake is a restaurant plate and adds meat.
  • Leave out the ají and the heat drops to none. The written recipe includes a tablespoon.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • A fried egg, which adds egg and means the plate is no longer vegan
  • Salsa criolla of onion and lime
  • Avocado
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
