India · Mild · 8 pesarattu

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Pesarattu

A green-gold moong dal pancake, herbal from ginger and green chile, with a soft crisp edge.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pesarattu

History

Pesarattu is the moong-dal pancake of Andhra cooking. Whole green gram is soaked and ground. Many kitchens cook it after that soak, with no long ferment, or after only a short rest. It is often eaten with ginger chutney, and a famous plate pairs it with upma. The batter is lentil, not the rice-and-urad ferment of a classic dosa, even though both are griddled breakfasts in South India.

Cultural background

Andhra tables treat pesarattu as its own order, especially when upma is tucked inside or served beside it. This recipe is the plain moong pancake. Upma is a pairing, and it is usually wheat semolina, so adding it would change the gluten-free tag.

Why it tastes this way

Green moong tastes grassy and slightly sweet, different from the sour rice dosa beside it on many menus. Ginger and green chile are ground in, so the heat is aromatic and mild. Because the batter is often unfermented, there is little tang. The griddle dries the surface and leaves a legume toast.

How it is made

Soak whole green moong, grind it with ginger and chile, and cook small pancakes on an oiled griddle without waiting for a long ferment.

Authentic touch. Grind the soaked moong until it is smooth but not watery. A batter that is too thin tears. A batter that is pasty never crisps at the rim.

How it is used

Serve with ginger chutney. If you add semolina upma, say so at the table, because that side contains wheat.

Field notes

  • Pesarattu is a moong pancake. It is not a rice-and-urad dosa renamed.
  • Many Andhra cooks grind it and cook it the same morning.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup whole green moong dal
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 or 2 green chiles
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Rinse the moong and soak it in water for 3 to 4 hours. Drain.
  2. Grind with ginger, chile, cumin, salt, and just enough fresh water to make a thick, pourable batter. Cook it the same day, or rest it up to an hour. No overnight ferment is required.
  3. Heat a griddle and grease it. Pour a ladle and spread it into a small round, thicker than a paper dosa.
  4. Oil the edges. Cook until the underside is spotted brown and the top looks set. Flip if you want both sides dry.
  5. Repeat with the rest of the batter, stirring between pancakes so the cumin does not sink.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A handful of chopped onion pressed on top
  • A short one-hour rest if you like a little tang

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Ginger chutney
  • Chopped onion
  • A rice upma made without wheat, if you want the stuffed plate and still need it gluten-free
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 8 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Plain dosa

Crisp at the edge, faintly sour, with a toasted rice smell and a soft underside.

40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 dosas

History

Dosa is old in South India. The exact origin century is not settled, so this page does not name one. What the kitchens agree on is the batter: raw rice and skinned black gram, soaked, ground, salted, and fermented, then spread on a hot griddle. Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, and Kerala all keep that ferment and change the sides.

Cultural background

A home batter is judged by smell and bubbles. A warm night may finish it by morning. A cool kitchen needs more time near the stove. The sourness should stay gentle. Harsh smell or visible mold means the bowl is discarded.

Why it tastes this way

Rice supplies the starch that dries against iron and turns crisp. Urad dal brings protein and a soft stickiness so the sheet can spread without tearing. Fermentation adds a mild tang and the gas that opens lacy holes at the rim. A few fenugreek seeds, traditional in many batters, leave a faint bitter aroma under the toast.

How it is made

Soak rice and urad separately, grind them to different textures, ferment until the batter is lively, and cook thin rounds on a lightly oiled griddle.

Authentic touch. Spread from the center while the batter still flows. A pan that is too hot tears the sheet. A pan that is too cool glues it down and never crisps.

How it is used

Eat it at once with coconut chutney and sambar. A covered dosa steams itself and loses the crisp edge.

Field notes

  • Skinned black gram is the urad dal in this batter, sometimes sold as white urad.
  • The same fermented batter can be steamed as idli. The griddle is what makes the dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup idli rice or other short-grain raw rice
  • 1/3 cup skinned urad dal (black gram)
  • 1/4 teaspoon fenugreek seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Rinse the rice. Rinse the urad with the fenugreek. Soak them in separate bowls for 4 to 6 hours.
  2. Drain and grind the urad with cold water to a light, fluffy paste. Grind the rice with water to a slightly grainy batter. Stir them together with the salt. The mix should thickly coat a spoon.
  3. Cover the bowl and ferment until it is bubbly and mildly sour, often 8 to 14 hours. In a cool room give it longer. This wait is not part of the active prep time. Discard the batter if it smells putrid or grows mold.
  4. Stir the batter down and loosen it with a spoon or two of water if it is too stiff to spread. Heat a griddle until a drop of water skitters. Grease it lightly.
  5. Pour a ladle in the center and spiral outward. Drizzle a little oil around the edge. Cook until the underside is spotted gold and the top looks dry. Fold and serve immediately.

Ferment in a clean bowl. Discard batter that smells rotten, turns an odd color, or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ghee on the rim instead of oil
  • A spoon of cooked rice in the rice grind for a softer crumb

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Tomato chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sri Lanka · Mild · 6 rotis

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sri Lanka · South Asia

Pol roti

A griddled coconut flatbread, lightly sweet from the coconut, with onion and green chile in the dough.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 rotis

History

Pol roti is a Sri Lankan coconut roti, griddled rather than baked in a tandoor. Pol means coconut. Scraped coconut is mixed into wheat flour with salt, onion, and often green chile, then patted and cooked on a flat pan. It is everyday breakfast and accompaniment food, especially along the south coast and in home kitchens. It is a flatbread, not a hopper and not a sweet tea-time pancake.

Cultural background

Galle kitchens serve it with lunumiris, with seeni sambol, or beside a curry. The bread itself can be mild. This recipe puts a little green chile in the dough so the heat matches the tag. Coconut here is fresh and unsweetened.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh coconut melts into the dough and tastes sweet and rich against wheat. Onion softens on the griddle. Green chile flickers rather than burns if it is chopped fine. Because the bread is cooked dry with only a film of oil, you taste toasted coconut and flour instead of a fried crust.

How it is made

Mix wheat flour with grated coconut, onion, and green chile, pat thick rounds, and griddle both sides.

Authentic touch. Pat the roti thicker than a chapati. A thin one dries out before the coconut toasts. Medium heat and a patient few minutes are the method.

How it is used

Serve warm, broken by hand, with an onion sambol. It also sits beside a vegetable curry as an accompaniment.

Field notes

  • Pol means coconut in Sinhala. The roti is built around fresh coconut.
  • It is cooked on a griddle, in rounds you pat by hand.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups whole wheat flour or all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 cup freshly grated coconut
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • About 1/2 cup water
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix flour, coconut, onion, chile, and salt. Add water gradually until a soft dough forms. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Divide into six balls. Pat each into a thick round on a floured sheet.
  3. Heat a griddle and film it with oil. Cook a roti on medium until the underside is spotted brown.
  4. Flip and cook until the center is no longer doughy and the coconut smells toasted.
  5. Repeat. Keep the stack covered with a cloth.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few curry leaves chopped into the dough
  • Shredded carrot for a lunchbox roti

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Onion sambol
  • Seeni sambol
  • A mild dhal curry
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Poland · No chili heat · 8 filled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Poland · Eastern Europe

Polish naleśniki

Thin Polish crepes with a sweet cheese filling, rolled and warmed.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 filled crepes

History

Naleśniki are Poland's thin wheat crepes, eaten sweet or savory. This page writes the sweet farmer-cheese filling, a home dessert and a weekend breakfast. They are not placki ziemniaczane, the potato pancakes, and they are not the Ashkenazi latke. The batter is the usual egg, milk, and flour crepe. Jam alone is an honest simpler serving and is listed as a variation.

Cultural background

They are made in ordinary kitchens, rolled, and sometimes given a minute in butter so the outside browns. Sweet cheese, jam, and fruit are the fillings people expect. There is no single feast day that owns them.

Why it tastes this way

The crepe is tender and a little sweet. Farmer cheese with sugar and vanilla tastes clean and slightly tart. A light fry browns the roll without drying the cheese. They should not be thick. A heavy pancake will not roll around this filling.

How it is made

Thin crepes are filled with sweetened farmer cheese, rolled, and warmed in butter.

Authentic touch. Cook the crepes thin and fill them while they are still flexible. Vanilla and lemon zest in the cheese are enough. Do not turn this into a potato placki.

How it is used

As dessert or a sweet breakfast, with a spoon of sour cream if you want it.

Field notes

  • Naleśniki are thin crepes. Placki are the potato pancakes.
  • Sweet cheese is a standard home filling.
  • They are everyday food, not a single feast-day cake.

Ingredients

  • 130 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 300 ml whole milk
  • 1 tbsp sugar, plus 2 tbsp for the filling
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • 300 g farmer cheese, drained
  • 1 tsp vanilla sugar or 1/2 tsp vanilla extract
  • 1 tsp lemon zest

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, eggs, milk, 1 tbsp sugar, and salt. Rest 10 minutes and cook thin crepes in butter.
  2. Mix the farmer cheese with 2 tbsp sugar, the vanilla, and the lemon zest.
  3. Fill and roll the crepes.
  4. Warm the rolls in a little butter until the outsides color. Serve with sour cream or extra sugar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Jam instead of cheese
  • A savory mushroom or spinach filling

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Sour cream
  • A dusting of sugar
  • Jam, if you skip the cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Portugal · No chili heat · 8 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Portugal · Southern Europe

Portuguese panquecas

Thin Portuguese crepes with orange zest, rolled with jam.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 crepes

History

Panquecas are Portugal's poured pancakes, thin crepes served with sugar or jam. They are not rabanadas, the fried Christmas bread on the previous page. Orange zest in the batter is the version written here, a nod to Portugal's oranges without turning the cake into a Suzette sauce. They are a home dessert and a breakfast. There is no feast day that this page claims for them.

Cultural background

A Portuguese home crepe is closer to a French crepe than to a thick American stack. Jam, sugar, or a chocolate filling are all served. This recipe uses jam so it stays free of trademark spreads.

Why it tastes this way

Orange zest oils the batter lightly. The crepe browns in butter and tastes of milk and egg. Jam is the fruit. They are mild and flexible. The Christmas rabanada is heavier, spicier, and made of bread. If these taste like fried bread, the batter was too thick.

How it is made

A thin batter with orange zest is cooked as crepes and rolled with jam.

Authentic touch. Keep the distinction: panquecas are poured. Rabanadas are soaked bread. Orange zest goes into the batter, not a flaming sauce.

How it is used

As dessert or breakfast, rolled, with jam or sugar.

Field notes

  • Panquecas are poured crepes. Rabanadas are fried bread.
  • This batter includes orange zest.
  • Jam is the filling, not a branded spread.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • 350 ml whole milk
  • 1 tsp orange zest
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, salt, eggs, milk, and orange zest. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook thin crepes in a buttered pan.
  3. Spread with jam.
  4. Roll and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lemon zest instead of orange
  • Rabanadas, the Christmas fried bread

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Jam
  • Sugar
  • A spoon of orange marmalade
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 6 pudlas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Pudla

A turmeric-yellow Gujarati besan pancake, savory and a little sweet, eaten with chutney.

15 min prep · 18 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 pudlas

History

Pudla is the Gujarati besan pancake that opens a breakfast, often with chutney and a cup of tea. Turmeric is mixed into the gram flour, so the cake arrives yellow. Green chile, cilantro, and sometimes sesame sit in the batter. It is usually unfermented and cooked as soon as the bowl is smooth. A Delhi cheela can share the flour and still be a different plate: this one belongs to a Gujarati morning, not to a tiffin counter seasoned with ajwain.

Cultural background

Ahmedabad homes treat pudla as ordinary breakfast cooking, the way other regions treat a toast. The chutney beside it is part of the habit, not a restaurant garnish. Sesame, when it is used, is toasted by the pan rather than ground into a paste.

Why it tastes this way

Turmeric colors the crumb and adds a light bitter note that Gujarati food often balances with a pinch of sugar. Besan still tastes of chickpea. Cilantro and green chile keep it fresh. A little sugar, common on Gujarati savory tables, rounds the spices without turning the pudla into a dessert.

How it is made

Whisk besan with turmeric, chile, cilantro, and a pinch of sugar, then griddle thin yellow pancakes.

Authentic touch. Cook it fully. Besan that is still raw in the center tastes chalky and bitter in a way turmeric did not intend. Medium heat and an extra minute are the fix.

How it is used

Serve straight from the pan with green chutney. Breakfast is the right hour. A sweeter hando version exists and is a different bowl.

Field notes

  • Pudla is a Gujarati breakfast cake. Turmeric in the batter is part of that habit.
  • It is besan, mixed and cooked the same morning, without a ferment.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup besan (chickpea flour), with no wheat added
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1/4 teaspoon turmeric
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • 1 teaspoon sesame seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk besan, turmeric, sugar, salt, and water into a smooth batter. Rest it 10 minutes.
  2. Stir in chile, cilantro, and sesame seeds.
  3. Oil a pan over medium heat. Pour a ladle and spread a thin round.
  4. Cook until the surface looks dry and the underside is speckled deep gold. Flip and cook through so no raw besan remains.
  5. Keep finished pudlas warm under a cloth and send them out with chutney.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Shredded cabbage in the batter
  • Methi leaves when they are in season

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Cilantro-mint chutney
  • Sweet mango pickle
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Puerto Rico · No chili heat · 12 fritters

Flying Pancakes of the World

Puerto Rico · Caribbean

Puerto Rican almojábanas

Fried rice-flour and cheese fritters, crisp at the edge, salty and chewy inside.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 fritters

History

Almojábanas in Puerto Rico are fritters of rice flour and cheese, fried and eaten at breakfast or as a snack. The name is older than the island recipe and is also used in Colombia, where the dough is often cheese and a starch such as corn starch, and the cakes may be baked rather than fried. This page is the Puerto Rican fritter: rice flour, cheese, egg, and milk, with no wheat. Rice flour is a later pantry staple here. It is not masa and not cassava.

Cultural background

Bakeries and home kitchens in San Juan fry them in small batches. The cheese is often a firm cow-milk cheese that can be grated, including the Edam-style cheeses long sold on the island. Any firm, mildly salty cheese that grates will work. The Colombian cake with the same name is a cousin, not a copy.

Why it tastes this way

Rice flour fries to a thin crust and a chewy, almost stretchy center, different from a wheat doughnut. Cheese melts into that center and salts it. Egg and milk bind the batter and brown at the edge. There is little sugar, so the fritter eats like a bread, not like a dessert. A gluten-free baking powder lifts it just enough that the middle is not a solid lump.

How it is made

Stir rice flour, gluten-free baking powder, salt, grated cheese, egg, and milk into a thick batter. Fry spoons of it in oil until golden and cooked through.

Authentic touch. Grate the cheese fine so it disperses. A chunk of cheese in a loose batter leaks into the oil. Fry until the center is set, not only until the outside colors. Use a gluten-free baking powder if you need the fritters to stay free of wheat.

How it is used

Eat them hot, at breakfast with coffee, or as a street snack. They dry out as they cool, so the pan and the plate should be close.

Field notes

  • Puerto Rican almojábanas are fried rice-flour fritters. The Colombian cake of the same name is often a different starch.
  • There is no wheat in this recipe if the baking powder is gluten-free.
  • Cheese is the main flavor. The fritter is savory-salty, not a sugar doughnut.

Ingredients

  • 150 g rice flour
  • 1 teaspoon gluten-free baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 120 g grated firm white cheese
  • 1 egg
  • 80 ml milk
  • Neutral oil for frying

Steps

  1. Stir the rice flour, baking powder, and salt. Toss in the grated cheese.
  2. Beat the egg with the milk and stir it in until the batter is thick enough to hold a spoon.
  3. Heat 2 centimeters of oil over medium heat until a drop of batter sizzles at once.
  4. Fry spoons of batter, turning once, until golden and cooked through, about 3 minutes.
  5. Drain and serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Colombian almojábanas often use corn starch and cheese and may be baked. That is a related cake, not this batter.
  • A little sugar in the batter makes them closer to a sweet fritter.
  • Wheat flour in place of rice flour means the fritters contain gluten.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Street food
  • Café con leche
  • Guava paste on the side
  • A cup of hot chocolate
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Pumpkin pancakes

Soft pumpkin, cinnamon, and ginger in a lightly sweet wheat cake.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Pumpkin pancakes are a seasonal American breakfast made with pumpkin puree and warm spices in a wheat batter. Canned puree made the cake an easy autumn diner special, and roasted pumpkin, mashed and drained, works the same way. The cake does not have a single town of origin. Circleville, Ohio, is the map pin because the town grows pumpkins, not because the recipe was invented there.

Cultural background

Pumpkin pie spice blends vary. This recipe uses cinnamon, ginger, and nutmeg so the amounts are clear. The puree is wet, so the batter needs less milk than a plain pancake.

Why it tastes this way

Pumpkin tastes sweet and vegetal, and the spices make it read as pie rather than as squash soup. Browned butter at the edge is the contrast. Too much puree and too little flour makes a gummy cake that never browns.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter mixed with pumpkin puree and spices.

Authentic touch. Use plain pumpkin puree, not a can of sweetened pie filling. The sugar and spices are already in the recipe.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and maple syrup.

Field notes

  • Canned pumpkin puree turned this into a standard autumn diner special.
  • Circleville is a pumpkin-growing town, and the pin is there for the crop, not for an invention claim.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup plain pumpkin puree
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground ginger
  • 1/4 teaspoon ground nutmeg
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, cinnamon, ginger, nutmeg, and salt.
  2. Whisk the pumpkin, buttermilk, egg, and melted butter until smooth. Stir into the flour until just combined.
  3. Butter a griddle over medium-low heat. Pumpkin browns quickly.
  4. Pour 1/4 cup cakes. Flip when the underside is brown and the top looks set.
  5. Cook the second side until the center springs back.
  6. Serve with butter and maple syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Roast a sugar pumpkin, mash it, and drain it in a sieve if you are not using canned puree.
  • A darker molasses in place of the sugar makes a heavier cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup
  • Toasted pecans only if a tree-nut topping is wanted
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 8 ragi dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Ragi dosa

An earthy, malt-dark finger-millet dosa with a mild chile edge and a crisp skin.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 ragi dosas

History

Ragi dosa is a finger-millet griddle cake of Karnataka and Tamil kitchens, where ragi is an everyday grain. This version mixes ragi flour with a little rice flour and ferments the batter so it belongs with the dosa family. It is gluten-free only when neither flour has wheat added, which is how the ingredients here are written. Instant ragi dosas mixed with yogurt are another home habit. They are a different bowl from this ferment.

Cultural background

Ragi porridge and ragi mudde are the better-known millet dishes. The dosa is the griddle form, useful when cooks want the same grain in a crisp sheet. The color is naturally dark. That darkness is the millet, not burnt batter.

Why it tastes this way

Finger millet tastes earthy and slightly malty, with a bitterness that toasting on the pan turns pleasant. A little rice flour keeps the sheet from crumbling. Fermentation softens the bitterness and adds a small tang. Onion and green chile, if you scatter them on top, brighten a grain that can otherwise taste flat.

How it is made

Stir ragi flour and rice flour into a batter, ferment it until mildly sour, and cook thin seasoned dosas.

Authentic touch. Oil the pan well. Ragi batter sticks more than a rice-and-urad batter. Cook it a shade longer than a pale dosa so the millet flavor rounds out.

How it is used

Serve with coconut chutney. The dark color looks fully cooked even early, so trust the edge: it should lift cleanly.

Field notes

  • Ragi is finger millet, a different grain from rice, wheat, and sorghum.
  • The dosa is gluten-free in this recipe because the flours are ragi and rice, with no wheat.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup ragi flour (finger millet), with no wheat added
  • 1/4 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours with water and salt into a smooth batter about as thick as crepe batter.
  2. Cover and ferment 8 to 12 hours until mildly sour and a few bubbles show. Active prep is only the mixing. Discard the batter if it spoils.
  3. Stir in chile and cilantro. Add a splash of water if the ferment made it stiff.
  4. Heat a griddle, oil it generously, and spread a thin round.
  5. Cook until the edge lifts and the underside smells toasted. Flip briefly if the center is still wet. Oil the pan again before the next dosa.

Discard the ferment if it smells rotten or grows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped onion and cilantro pressed on top
  • A pinch of cumin in the batter

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • A dry potato palya
  • Plain yogurt on the side, which is dairy and separate from this batter
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 8 crisp dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Rava dosa

A lacy, cracker-crisp semolina sheet scented with cumin, black pepper, and curry leaf.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 crisp dosas

History

Rava dosa is a semolina batter, usually stirred and cooked without the long rice-and-urad ferment. Wheat semolina, often called rava or sooji, is the grain that builds the crisp web. Rice flour is there to help the sheet set. It became a restaurant standard because a cook can mix it to order when a fermented batter has run out. It is a different dosa from the fermented rice and urad kind.

Cultural background

The batter is meant to be thin and a little lumpy. Those lumps and the water around them are what leave the holes. A thick batter makes a pancake, which is not the point of this page.

Why it tastes this way

Semolina browns faster than a wet fermented rice batter and tastes toasted and wheat-y. Cumin and crushed black pepper sit on the surface instead of disappearing into a ferment. Curry leaf, dropped into hot oil or onto the batter, adds a sharp green note. The lack of a sour ferment is the flavor clue that this is rava dosa.

How it is made

Whisk semolina, rice flour, and water into a thin batter, season it, and pour it loosely onto a hot griddle so it laces.

Authentic touch. Pour the thin batter from the ladle in separate streams and let the holes stay open. Do not spiral it into a solid disk.

How it is used

Lift it off while it is still brittle. Coconut chutney is the usual partner. It softens if it waits in a stack.

Field notes

  • Rava here is wheat semolina, so this dosa is not gluten-free.
  • The lacy holes are a pouring method. They are not a sign of fermentation.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup fine wheat semolina (rava or sooji)
  • 1/3 cup rice flour
  • 1 1/2 cups cold water, plus more if needed
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/4 teaspoon crushed black pepper
  • 1 green chile, finely chopped
  • 8 curry leaves, torn
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk semolina, rice flour, salt, and cold water into a thin batter. Rest it 15 minutes so the semolina hydrates. It should stay pourable.
  2. Stir in cumin, pepper, green chile, and curry leaves. Thin with a splash of water if it has thickened.
  3. Heat a griddle until very hot. Grease it lightly and pour the batter in separate ribbons rather than one tight spiral.
  4. Fill obvious gaps with a little more batter, then drizzle oil around the lace. Cook until the sheet is golden and rigid.
  5. Loosen the edge and lift the dosa off. Cook the rest, stirring the batter each time so the spices do not sink.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few cumin seeds extra on the surface
  • Buttermilk in place of part of the water, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Coconut chutney
  • Allam-style ginger chutney
  • Sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · 10 thin cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Rhode Island johnnycakes

Toasted corn, salt, and a crisp edge, with almost no sweetness in the cake itself.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 thin cakes

History

Rhode Island johnnycakes are thin cornmeal cakes, traditionally made with white flint cornmeal scalded with boiling water. The name is disputed: journey cake and Shawnee cake are both proposed sources, and neither explanation is settled. South County cooks spread the batter thin so the cakes crisp, while other Rhode Island versions are thicker and use milk. This recipe is the thin water-scalded kind.

Cultural background

White flint corn was the local grain that millers stone-ground for these cakes. Spellings vary in the state: johnnycake and jonnycake both appear on menus and in cookbooks.

Why it tastes this way

Boiling water swells the cornmeal and gives the cake a soft center under a brittle crust. There is no wheat gluten to make it bready. The flavor is the grain itself, plus whatever fat the griddle contributes.

How it is made

Scalded cornmeal spread thin on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Use stone-ground white cornmeal if you can find it. Spread the batter thin. A thick, cakey round is the other Rhode Island style.

How it is used

Hot from the griddle. Butter and maple syrup are common at the table and are not part of this batter.

Field notes

  • Journey cake and Shawnee cake are both disputed explanations of the name.
  • Both the spellings johnnycake and jonnycake are used in Rhode Island.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup stone-ground white cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup boiling water, plus 1 to 2 tablespoons cold water if needed
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil, for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal and salt in a heatproof bowl.
  2. Pour the boiling water over the meal and stir until a thick batter forms. Rest 5 minutes.
  3. If the batter is too stiff to spread, stir in cold water a spoon at a time. It should move slowly off the spoon.
  4. Film a griddle with oil and heat it over medium.
  5. Spread about 2 tablespoons of batter into a thin round. Cook until the edge is crisp and the underside is browned, then flip and finish.
  6. Serve at once. The cakes harden as they cool.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A milk batter, cooked thicker, is the other well-known Rhode Island style.
  • Some cooks grease the griddle with bacon fat instead of oil.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Maple syrup
  • Butter, if you eat dairy
  • Salt fish cakes, in an old Rhode Island breakfast
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Malaysia · No chili heat · 6 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Malaysia · Southeast Asia

Roti canai

A flaky, laminated wheat flatbread, griddled until the outside shatters and the inside stays stretchy.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 flatbreads

History

Roti canai is a Malaysian griddled flatbread made from laminated dough. Oil or ghee is spread through a wheat dough that is stretched, coiled, and cooked on a griddle until it flakes. The bread has Indian-Muslim heritage in Malaysia, carried by cooks and shops rather than by a single named inventor on this page. It is breakfast and late-night food, usually with dal or a curry. Singapore's roti prata is the same family and has its own page about coffee-shop life there. This page is the Malaysian bread.

Cultural background

Mamak stalls clap the dough on the board until it is wide and thin, then crumple it onto the griddle. The clap is skill. At home, rolling and coiling still makes layers if the dough is well oiled and rested. The bread itself is not spicy. The curry beside it may be.

Why it tastes this way

Layers of dough fried in ghee taste rich and wheat-y, with a crisp leaf and a chewy center. Salt is the only seasoning in the bread, so the flavor is fat and toast. A lentil curry or a spicy dal adds the salt and chile the roti does not carry. The lamination is why it tastes different from a single chapati: you get many surfaces of fried dough in one piece.

How it is made

Laminate a wheat dough with ghee, stretch and coil it, and griddle it until flaky.

Authentic touch. Oil the dough generously and rest it. A dry dough will not stretch into layers. Cook it on a moderately hot griddle and press it so the coil makes contact.

How it is used

Serve hot with dal or a chicken curry. The chicken curry would add meat. A lentil dal keeps the meal vegetarian. Tear the bread and dip it.

Field notes

  • Roti canai is laminated dough, stretched and griddled, not a poured batter.
  • Its heritage in Malaysia is Indian-Muslim cooking, and this page does not name one inventor.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose wheat flour
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2/3 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons ghee or neutral oil, plus more for cooking
  • 1 teaspoon sugar

Steps

  1. Mix flour, salt, sugar, water, and a tablespoon of ghee into a soft dough. Knead until smooth.
  2. Divide into six balls. Coat each with ghee. Rest at least 1 hour. That rest is not active prep.
  3. Stretch or roll one ball thin, spread a little ghee, and fold or coil it into a layered round.
  4. Griddle on medium heat with a film of ghee, pressing, until both sides are flaky and spotted brown.
  5. Repeat. Serve at once with dal.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Roti telur, with an egg cracked on the griddle, which adds egg
  • A banana and sugar version

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Lentil dal
  • Sugar on a piece, for a sweet bite
  • A fish curry, which would add fish
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Russia · No chili heat · 12 blini

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Russian blini

Yeasted wheat blini for Maslenitsa, butter week, with sour cream and butter.

1 hr 40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 blini

History

These are yeasted wheat blini from Russia. Maslenitsa is butter week, the week before Lent, not a single Tuesday. Blini are cooked through that week and also outside it. Buckwheat blini are traditional too and have their own page. This page is the wheat batter, the one most tables mean when they pile on butter and sour cream. A popular image treats the round blin as a sun at the end of winter. That is a popular reading. The kitchen facts are yeast, butter, and a week of cooking.

Cultural background

Maslenitsa food is rich in butter and dairy before the fast. Blini are breakfast, snack, and feast food in that week. Caviar and smoked fish are festive toppings. Sour cream and butter are the everyday ones, and they are what this recipe requires.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast gives a mild sourness and a softer crumb than an unleavened crepe. Butter in the pan and on the cake is the point of butter week. Sour cream adds tang and fat. The cakes are a little thicker than a French crepe and full of small holes. Sugar in the batter is only a pinch, so the topping does the sweetening if you want it.

How it is made

A yeast batter of wheat flour, milk, egg, and butter rises until bubbly, then is cooked in a buttered pan and stacked with more butter.

Authentic touch. Cook them through butter week if you are keeping Maslenitsa, and do not call that week a Tuesday. Brush each blin with butter as it comes off the pan. Keep them covered so they steam and stay flexible.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and sour cream. Jam, herring, or smoked fish are toppings. Fish would add a fish allergen and is not in this batter.

Field notes

  • Maslenitsa is butter week before Lent, not one Tuesday.
  • Wheat blini and buckwheat blini both exist. This page is the wheat yeast batter.
  • The sun story is a popular reading. The recipe is yeast and butter.

Ingredients

  • 250 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 300 ml warm milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 40 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the pan and for brushing
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, yeast, sugar, warm milk, egg, salt, and melted butter into a batter like cream. Cover until bubbly and risen, about 1 hour.
  2. Stir it down. Add a splash of milk if it is too thick to spread.
  3. Cook small rounds in a buttered pan until the top is set and full of holes, then turn and brown the other side.
  4. Brush each blin with butter and stack them. Serve with sour cream.

The batter should smell yeasty. Discard it if it smells spoiled.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of buckwheat in the batter, still not the buckwheat page
  • A thin unleavened blinchik, which is the filled crepe page

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Melted butter
  • Sour cream
  • Jam, or herring for a fish plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Samoa · No chili heat · About 12 fried rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Samoa · Oceania

Samoan panikeke

Round fried cakes, lightly sweet, with mashed banana in a soft wheat crumb.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · About 12 fried rounds

History

Panikeke are Samoan round fried cakes, and the name comes from the English word pancake. They are often made with mashed banana, baking powder, and flour, then fried into rounds for breakfast, for church gatherings, and for the shop counter. The borrowed name already places them in the period when English words and wheat flour were part of island cooking. They are not a pre-contact dish. This recipe fries them, which is how they are cooked, and keeps the banana in the dough.

Cultural background

In Apia and in villages, panikeke are a batch food: one bowl, one pan of oil, a plate that empties fast. Ripe banana is the fruit most often mashed in. They are a snack more than a ceremony. The shape is a round fritter, not a thin crepe.

Why it tastes this way

Banana sweetens the dough and keeps it moist in the middle while the oil browns the outside. Wheat and baking powder make a soft, bready crumb rather than a fruit leather. Sugar is light, so the banana remains the sweetener you taste. Salt at the edge of the dough keeps it from eating flat.

How it is made

Mash a ripe banana and stir it with flour, baking powder, sugar, salt, and a little water into a soft dough. Shape rounds and fry them in oil until golden and cooked through. Drain and eat warm.

Authentic touch. Fry rounds, and keep the banana in the dough if you are cooking this version. The name comes from pancake, but the method is a fritter. A thin griddled crepe is not panikeke.

How it is used

Panikeke are a snack and a breakfast cake, at home and at gatherings. They are eaten plain or with a little butter. Butter adds dairy and is not in this dough. They are not a savory stew bread.

Field notes

  • The name panikeke comes from the English word pancake.
  • The cakes are round and fried, often with banana.
  • This recipe is a fritter, not a thin batter cooked on a dry griddle.

Ingredients

  • 1 ripe banana, mashed
  • 1 cup wheat flour (about 125 g)
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1/4 cup water
  • Oil for frying

Steps

  1. Mash the banana until smooth. Stir in the flour, baking powder, sugar, salt, and water to make a soft dough you can shape.
  2. Rest the dough for 10 minutes.
  3. Shape small rounds with floured hands.
  4. Fry the rounds in hot oil, turning once, until they are golden and the center is cooked. Panikeke are fried cakes, and the name comes from pancake.
  5. Drain and serve warm.

The dough contains wheat. Frying oil is hot. There is no egg and no dairy in the dough. Butter on the side would add dairy.

Eat panikeke the day they are fried. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat them in a hot oven. The banana crumb does not stay crisp. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Without banana, the dough is a plainer fried round and less like the usual panikeke.
  • A spoon of mashed pumpkin is another home mash and a different flavor.
  • Egg or milk in the dough changes the allergens. This batch has neither.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • Tea or cocoa
  • A ripe banana on the side
  • Butter, which adds dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

San Francisco sourdough pancakes

Tangy sourdough in a thin, buttered wheat pancake.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

San Francisco's sourdough reputation belongs first to its bread bakeries, which were part of the city's nineteenth-century food supply. Pancakes made from a bubbly starter are a home and diner use of that same ferment. This page does not name a bakery brand or a single inventor of the pancake. The map pin is the city itself.

Cultural background

A San Francisco starter is a wheat starter kept in a cool coastal kitchen. Any active wheat starter will make the cakes. The long life of the starter happens before this recipe. Active prep is mixing and cooking.

Why it tastes this way

The starter's acid is the sour taste, and it also reacts with baking soda for lift. Milk and egg soften a flavor that would be sharper in lean bread. Butter browns the surface. A starter that has not been fed tastes sluggish and the cakes spread without rising.

How it is made

Thinner sourdough batter cooked like pancakes on a buttered griddle.

Authentic touch. Start with a starter that is already bubbly and has been fed ahead of time. Stir in the soda at the last minute.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and jam or syrup.

Field notes

  • The city's sourdough fame is a bread story. These pancakes are a breakfast use of that starter.
  • The starter has to be fed before you begin. That feeding is not the active prep time.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup active sourdough starter, fed ahead of time
  • 3/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups whole milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda

Steps

  1. Whisk the starter, flour, sugar, salt, milk, egg, and melted butter until smooth.
  2. Rest 15 minutes. The starter should already have been bubbly before you mixed it.
  3. Stir in the baking soda.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes.
  5. Flip when the tops are full of small holes and the undersides are brown.
  6. Cook until the center is set. Serve with butter and jam or syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A thicker batter, with less milk, eats more like the Alaska flapjack.
  • Leave out the soda for a flatter, more purely sour cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Berry jam
  • Maple syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 16 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Scotch pancakes

Small thick drop scones from a Scottish girdle, butter and jam.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 16 small pancakes

History

Scotch pancakes, also called drop scones, are small thick cakes cooked on a girdle. They are not the thin English Shrove pancake and they are not a crepe. A modern batter uses baking powder or buttermilk and soda. Earlier dropped cakes relied more on eggs. This recipe uses baking powder and buttermilk because that is the home cake people mean by the name now. The country pin is the United Kingdom, and the culture is Scottish.

Cultural background

They are a tea-table and breakfast cake across Scotland. A heavy girdle, or a thick frying pan, is the tool. The cakes are eaten the day they are cooked.

Why it tastes this way

Buttermilk and a quick leavener make a soft, slightly tangy crumb. The girdle browns the outside before the inside dries. Sugar in the batter is light. Jam does the rest. Overmixing makes them tough, because the flour is wheat.

How it is made

Flour, egg, buttermilk, and baking powder are mixed briefly and dropped onto a lightly greased girdle.

Authentic touch. Drop the batter from a spoon. Do not spread it into a crepe. Turn when bubbles sit open on the top, and only once.

How it is used

Warm, with butter and jam, at breakfast or tea. They are not rolled.

Field notes

  • Drop scone is the other name. The cake is thick and small.
  • They are a different batter from English Shrove pancakes.
  • A girdle is a griddle. The spelling is the Scottish one.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 180 ml buttermilk
  • 20 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the girdle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, sugar, and salt. Beat the egg with the buttermilk and melted butter.
  2. Stir the wet ingredients into the dry until just combined. A few lumps are fine.
  3. Grease a girdle or heavy pan over medium heat. Drop spoonfuls and cook until bubbles open on top.
  4. Turn once and cook until the centers spring back. Butter them and serve with jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of raisins in the batter
  • Thick cream instead of butter

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Butter
  • Raspberry jam or another jam
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 12 oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Scottish oatcakes

Thin, dry, unleavened oatcakes from a Scottish girdle.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 12 oatcakes

History

Scottish oatcakes are thin unleavened oat breads cooked on a girdle. They are a staple accompaniment, not a dessert pancake, and they are not the yeasted oatcakes of Staffordshire or Derbyshire. Oats were the grain that reliably grew in much of Scotland. This recipe uses oatmeal, salt, a little fat, and hot water. There is no wheat flour in it.

Cultural background

An oatcake is travel food and table food. It is eaten with cheese, soup, or fish, and it keeps better than a soft pancake. The texture should be crisp after cooling, not floppy.

Why it tastes this way

Toasted oatmeal is nutty even though the recipe has no nuts. Salt and a little fat keep it from tasting raw. The girdle dries the cake. Hot water softens the oats just enough to roll. They taste plain on purpose, because cheese or a salty fish is supposed to do the seasoning.

How it is made

Medium oatmeal is mixed with salt, a pinch of baking soda, oil, and hot water, rolled thin, cut, and baked on a dry or lightly oiled girdle until firm.

Authentic touch. Roll them thin and cook them slowly. Use oats milled apart from wheat if you need them gluten-free. Ordinary oatmeal is often handled near wheat, so the gluten-free label depends on the oats you buy.

How it is used

With soup, cheese, or smoked fish. The cakes themselves are vegan. A herring or a buttered slice changes that plate.

Field notes

  • These are unleavened and crisp. Staffordshire oatcakes are yeasted and soft.
  • They are an accompaniment as often as they are a snack.
  • The recipe has no wheat unless you choose the variation that adds it.

Ingredients

  • 200 g medium oatmeal, plus more for rolling
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp baking soda
  • 20 ml neutral oil
  • 120 ml hot water

Steps

  1. Stir the oatmeal, salt, and baking soda. Add the oil and hot water and mix to a stiff dough. Rest 5 minutes.
  2. Roll or press the dough thin on an oatmeal-dusted board. Cut rounds or triangles.
  3. Cook on a medium-low girdle until the edges lift and the cake is dry and pale gold, then turn and finish the other side.
  4. Cool on a rack until crisp. Eat with cheese, soup, or fish.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of wheat flour, which is common and removes the gluten-free diet
  • Fine oatmeal for a smoother cake, or pinhead oats kept coarse

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Snack
  • Cheddar or a hard cheese
  • Smoked fish
  • A bowl of soup
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 8 thick dosas, about 3 sets

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Set dosa

Thick, soft, and mildly sour, with a spongy face and a pale golden underside.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 3 · 8 thick dosas, about 3 sets

History

Set dosa is a Karnataka breakfast dosa made from fermented rice and urad, cooked thick and served in a set, usually two or three cakes on one plate. The name comes from that serving, not from a new grain. A little flattened rice in the grind is a common way to keep the crumb soft. Hotel kitchens in Bengaluru and Mysuru plate it with a potato palya and chutney, and homes do the same with a smaller pan.

Cultural background

The batter is close to an idli batter, sometimes a touch looser. Cooks cover the pan so steam finishes the top. Leaving it uncovered and thin would turn it into an ordinary crisp dosa, which misses the set.

Why it tastes this way

A thicker pool of fermented batter keeps moisture in the center, so the sourness reads as soft and bready instead of as a toasted edge. Flattened rice lightens the crumb. Oil on the underside adds a little browning, but the cake is not chased to a cracker. The mild ferment is the main flavor.

How it is made

Ferment a soft rice and urad batter, pour small thick rounds, cover them, and serve two or three together.

Authentic touch. Cook on medium-low and cover the pan. If you spread the batter thin, you have left set dosa behind.

How it is used

Bring the set to the table stacked, with potato palya and coconut chutney. They are eaten with the hands, torn, not cut like a cake.

Field notes

  • A set is the portion: two or three thick dosas, not a different cereal.
  • Covering the pan is what keeps the center spongy.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup idli rice
  • 1/4 cup skinned urad dal
  • 2 tablespoons flattened rice (poha)
  • 1/8 teaspoon fenugreek seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse and soak the rice in one bowl. Soak the urad, fenugreek, and flattened rice in another for 4 to 5 hours.
  2. Grind the urad mixture to a fluffy paste and the rice to a fine batter. Combine with salt. The batter should be thick, like a soft idli batter.
  3. Ferment covered until risen and mildly sour, often 8 to 12 hours. Do not count the ferment as active prep. Discard it if it spoils.
  4. Stir gently. Heat a pan on medium-low, grease it, and pour a small ladle without spreading it to the rim. The round should stay about a finger thick.
  5. Cover and cook until the top is set and the underside is pale gold. Repeat. Serve three cakes as one set.

Discard fermented batter that smells rotten or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of sugar in the batter, common in some Karnataka homes
  • Ghee on the griddle, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Potato palya
  • Coconut chutney
  • A thin vegetable sagu
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

China · No chili heat · 4 crisp crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Shandong jianbing

A very thin, cracker-crisp crepe, torn into pieces and eaten with scallion and a savory paste.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 crisp crepes

History

Shandong jianbing is a crisp street crepe from Shandong, and it is a different food from the Tianjin jianbing that wraps a youtiao. This one is often cracker-crisp. Cooks tear it into pieces. It is not always wrapped around a fried dough stick. The batter is thin, sometimes with millet or other coarse grain beside wheat, and it is cooked until it dries and blisters. Jinan is a fair place to meet it. The two jianbing share a family name and not a single assembly.

Cultural background

A brittle sheet can be broken over a plate of condiments or folded loosely. Treating it like the soft Tianjin wrap makes it crack in the wrong way and misses the texture Shandong stalls chase. Egg is common. A youtiao is not the defining center of this page.

Why it tastes this way

Cooking the batter dry concentrates a toasted wheat flavor and leaves almost no soft crumb. Millet, if you include it, adds a faint bitterness and a coarser bite. Scallion and fermented bean paste supply the salt and the green note the crisp sheet lacks. Because there is no juicy fritter inside, the taste is cleaner and more cracker-like than a Tianjin breakfast.

How it is made

Cook an extra-thin wheat and millet batter until it is brittle, then tear it and eat it with scallion and bean paste.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter thinner than a crepe you mean to fold, and leave it on the heat until it snaps. If it bends like cloth, it needs another minute.

How it is used

Break the sheet at the griddle and eat the pieces with the condiments. It is a street breakfast, not a wrapped sandwich.

Field notes

  • Shandong jianbing is often cracker-crisp and torn, not wrapped around a youtiao.
  • Tianjin's egg-and-fritter jianbing is the other famous street version.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons millet flour
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 egg
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 2 tablespoons fermented bean sauce
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours, salt, and water into a very thin batter.
  2. Oil a griddle well and heat it until hot. Pour a small ladle and spread it as thin as you can.
  3. Break the egg onto the sheet if you want it richer, and spread it into a film.
  4. Cook without folding until the crepe is dry, blistered, and brittle enough to tear.
  5. Tear it into pieces. Eat them with scallion and fermented bean sauce.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • All wheat flour if you have no millet
  • An egg spread on the sheet before it dries

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Fermented bean paste
  • Chopped scallion
  • A spoon of chile oil if you want heat
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Singapore · No chili heat · 6 pratas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Singapore · Southeast Asia

Singaporean roti prata

A flaky laminated flatbread from a Singapore coffee shop, crisp at the edge and chewy inside, served with curry.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 pratas

History

Roti prata is Singapore's coffee-shop flatbread, cooked to order on a griddle and flipped until the layers separate. The dough is laminated with ghee or oil, the same family as Malaysian roti canai, and this page keeps the Singapore plate: a morning or late supper in a coffee shop, torn into curry gravy. Indian-Muslim cooks built that trade in Singapore as well as across the causeway. The pages stay separate so the coffee-shop habit is not collapsed into the Malaysian one. Tissue prata, stretched paper-thin and dusted with sugar, is a sweet variation.

Cultural background

In a Singapore coffee shop the prata arrives with a small bowl of curry and, often, a drink. The savory bread is the breakfast. A tissue prata is ordered when someone wants sugar instead of gravy. Home cooks can coil an oiled dough and griddle it. The flake still depends on fat between the layers.

Why it tastes this way

Ghee and wheat, fried in thin layers, taste toasted and rich. The interior stretches. Salt in the dough is plain, so the curry provides spice, coconut, and salt. A tissue prata tastes of sugar and crisp dough instead. The coffee-shop setting does not change the chemistry. It changes the meal around the bread: curry at breakfast, and the option of a sweet paper-thin cousin from the same ball of dough.

How it is made

Laminate a wheat dough with ghee, griddle it until flaky, and serve it with a curry gravy in the coffee-shop way.

Authentic touch. Stretch the dough thin and do not be afraid of an uneven edge. Those thin flaps are what crisp. Rest the oiled dough long enough that it relaxes, or it will snap back into a bun.

How it is used

Tear it and dip it in curry for a savory breakfast. For a sweet order, skip the curry and dust a thinner bread with sugar.

Field notes

  • Singapore roti prata is the same laminated-dough family as Malaysian roti canai, eaten here as coffee-shop food.
  • Tissue prata is the sweet, very thin version from the same dough.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose wheat flour
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 2/3 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons ghee, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix flour, salt, sugar, water, and a spoon of ghee into a soft dough. Knead until smooth.
  2. Divide into six pieces, coat them in ghee, and rest at least 1 hour. The rest is not active prep.
  3. Stretch one piece thin, spread a little ghee, and fold or coil it into layers.
  4. Griddle with ghee over medium heat, pressing and flipping, until both sides are spotted and flaky.
  5. Repeat and serve with curry, or stretch one piece thinner, cook it crisp, and dust it with sugar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Tissue prata dusted with sugar
  • An egg fried onto the prata, which adds egg

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • A mild potato and lentil curry
  • Sugar for a tissue-style piece
  • Coffee or tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Singing hinnies

Currant griddle cakes from Northumberland. The fat sizzles, which is the singing.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Singing hinnies are currant griddle cakes from Northumberland and the north-east of England. The usual kitchen explanation of the name is that the fat sings on a hot griddle, and that hinny is a local word for someone dear. That is an explanation of a name, not a dated record, and this page treats it as that. The cake is a fat-rubbed dough cooked on a girdle, split, and buttered.

Cultural background

They belong to a tea table in the north-east. Currants are the fruit. There is no yeast. Chemical leavener in this recipe is a home convenience so the cake is not leaden.

Why it tastes this way

Butter in the dough and butter on the griddle brown at the edge. Currants sweeten single bites rather than the whole crumb. The inside stays short, closer to a scone than to a crepe. A hot griddle is what makes the fat sing and the base crisp.

How it is made

Fat is rubbed into flour with currants and a little leavener, mixed to a soft dough with milk, rolled into rounds, and cooked on a greased griddle.

Authentic touch. Use enough fat that you can hear it. A dry pan will not sing, and the cake will toughen. Split the hinny while warm and butter the cut face.

How it is used

Split and buttered, as a snack or a substantial tea. They are not a poured pancake.

Field notes

  • The singing is the sound of fat on a hot griddle.
  • They are a Northumberland cake, cut and buttered like a griddle scone.
  • Currants, not chocolate, are the fruit in the dough.

Ingredients

  • 250 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 60 g cold unsalted butter, plus more for the griddle
  • 40 g currants
  • 120 ml whole milk

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, baking powder, and salt. Rub in the cold butter until the bowl looks like crumbs. Stir in the currants.
  2. Add the milk and bring the dough together. It should be soft, not wet. Pat into rounds about 1 cm thick.
  3. Cook on a buttered griddle over medium heat until the underside is brown, then turn. You should hear the fat.
  4. Split each cake and butter it. Eat while warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few strips of candied peel with the currants
  • Lard in place of some of the butter, which adds a meat allergen and removes the vegetarian diet

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • Extra butter
  • A slice of cheese
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Canada · No chili heat · 8 skillet cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Canada · North America

Skillet bannock

Plain wheat bread, slightly salty, with a fried face and a soft center.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · 8 skillet cakes

History

Bannock is a Scottish word for a flat griddle bread. Many First Nations and Métis communities in Canada made skillet bannock after wheat flour became a trade good and a ration staple. This recipe is that simple flour version: it is not a claim that one nation owns the bread, and it is not a claim that this flour bread predates flour. Winnipeg is the map pin because it is a Métis center, not because the bread belongs to one city.

Cultural background

The written dough is flour, baking powder, salt, oil, and water, cooked in a skillet. Lard was a common fat after it was available through trade and rations. This batch uses oil, so the written recipe stays free of meat and dairy.

Why it tastes this way

The flavor is wheat and salt, with browning from the skillet. Baking powder lifts it enough to keep the center from turning to hardtack. Oil in the dough makes the crumb shorter. There is no sour ferment and no corn taste.

How it is made

Soft baking-powder dough cooked in a greased skillet and flipped once.

Authentic touch. Cook it in a skillet and turn it once. This is the simple flour bread. Do not add ingredients to make it seem older than flour.

How it is used

Hot, broken into pieces, with jam, stew, or nothing.

Field notes

  • Bannock is the Scottish name for a griddle bread, later made across Canada after flour was a trade and ration staple.
  • This page is the simple flour version and does not assign it to one nation.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for the skillet
  • 3/4 cup cold water

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, baking powder, and salt. Add the oil and water and mix to a soft dough. If it is crumbly, add a spoon of water.
  2. Divide the dough into 8 pieces and pat each into a round about 1/2 inch thick.
  3. Film a skillet with oil over medium heat.
  4. Cook the rounds, a few at a time, until the underside is brown and the side looks set, about 4 minutes.
  5. Flip and cook until the center is no longer doughy.
  6. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Raisins in the dough are a common sweet version and still fit this flour bread.
  • Lard in place of oil is closer to many camp kitchens and makes the recipe no longer vegan or meat-free.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Snack
  • Jam
  • Stew
  • A drizzle of oil or butter if you eat dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Somalia · No chili heat · About 10 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Somalia · East Africa

Somali canjeero

Soft, lightly sour wheat cakes full of small holes, mildly sweet at the edge.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · About 10 small cakes

History

Canjeero is the spongy fermented pancake of a Somali breakfast. The same bread is also called laxoox and anjero. Those are names for this cake, not three different recipes on this page. It is cooked in a small pan until the top is pitted with holes, then eaten with ghee, honey, or a light soup. Yemeni lahoh is a related spongy bread with its own page and its own table. This one stays in Mogadishu.

Cultural background

Breakfast can be a stack of canjeero, a spoon of subag, which is ghee, and tea. Some houses prefer honey. Others set the bread next to a thin soup or a bit of stewed meat. The batter is a home ferment, often helped with a pinch of yeast when the kitchen is cool.

Why it tastes this way

An overnight ferment gives a gentle sourness, and a little sugar keeps it from tasting like injera. Wheat makes the crumb softer and less earthy than teff. The holes hold ghee or honey. A soup-soaked piece tastes savory because of the broth, not because the batter was seasoned like a stew.

How it is made

Whisk wheat flour with warm water, a little sugar, and a pinch of yeast. Rest it overnight until bubbly and slightly sour, then salt it. Cook small rounds in a lightly oiled pan on one side until the top is spongy and set. Serve warm. The ghee, honey, and soup stay at the table, not in the batter.

Authentic touch. Cook one side and wait until the holes cover the surface before you lift the cake. A flip makes it bready. Keep the Somali breakfast toppings, ghee, honey, or soup, and do not dress this page as Yemeni lahoh.

How it is used

Canjeero is breakfast. A common plate is the bread with ghee and honey, or the bread torn into a light soup. It is not the liner under a large platter of stews. Sesame oil is another home finish.

Field notes

  • Laxoox and anjero are other names for this Somali breakfast pancake.
  • The cake is fermented and spongy, and it is usually eaten in the morning.
  • Yemeni lahoh is related and has its own page. It is often served with foul or eggs.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 2 1/2 cups warm water
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, warm water, sugar, and yeast into a batter like thin cream. Leave the salt out for now.
  2. Cover the bowl and leave it at room temperature overnight, until it smells faintly sour and the surface is bubbly.
  3. Stir in the salt. The batter should pour. Add a splash of water if it thickened overnight.
  4. Heat a small nonstick pan over medium heat and wipe it with a drop of oil. Pour a thin layer of batter.
  5. Cover and cook on one side until the holes set and the top is no longer wet. Do not flip. Repeat with the rest of the batter and serve warm.

This recipe contains wheat. Ghee and honey at the table add dairy and honey for anyone who uses them.

Refrigerate leftover cakes and reheat them covered so they do not dry. Do not leave cooked cakes in a warm kitchen overnight. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of yesterday's batter can replace the yeast.
  • Some cooks replace part of the wheat with sorghum. Any wheat left in the bowl means the bread is not gluten-free. This written recipe stays with wheat.
  • A little cardamom in the batter is a family choice, not a requirement.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Ghee, called subag
  • Honey
  • A light soup or broth
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
