Japan · No chili heat · 3 very thick cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Souffle hotcakes

Extremely thick, jiggly pancakes with a browned cap and a soft, egg-white interior.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 3 very thick cakes

History

Souffle hotcakes are a contemporary Japanese cafe trend of the 2010s. Cooks whip egg whites, fold them into a small amount of flour and milk, and bake the batter slowly in rings so the cakes rise very tall. The style moved through city cafes and then into home kitchens. It is a recent fashion in the hotcake story, separate from the kissaten hotcake and from a traditional confection like dorayaki. It is contemporary.

Cultural background

The cakes need time and low heat. A ring keeps the side from flowing away. Powdered sugar and butter are the usual finish, sometimes with fruit. They collapse as they cool, which is expected. The texture is the reason people order them.

Why it tastes this way

Whipped egg white makes the crumb light and a little eggy, closer to a souffle than to a diner pancake. A small measure of flour and milk keeps it from being only meringue. Slow browning adds a toasted cap while the inside stays moist and sweet. The height is steam and foam, so the flavor is delicate. Syrup or cream makes it a dessert even at breakfast.

How it is made

Whip egg whites, fold them into a yolk and flour batter, and cook tall rounds in rings on very low heat under a lid.

Authentic touch. Fold the whites in gently and do not open the lid for the first several minutes. A slam of the pan knocks the rise out. Low heat is the whole trick.

How it is used

Serve immediately. They are best while still tall. Dust with powdered sugar and add butter or cream.

Field notes

  • Souffle hotcakes are a 2010s cafe trend. They are contemporary.
  • The height comes from whipped egg whites cooked slowly in rings.

Ingredients

  • 2 eggs, separated
  • 2 tablespoons all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons milk
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking powder
  • A pinch of salt
  • Butter for the pan and rings
  • Powdered sugar to serve

Steps

  1. Stir the yolks with flour, milk, baking powder, and salt into a thick paste.
  2. Whip the whites to soft peaks, adding the sugar gradually. Fold the whites into the yolk paste in two additions.
  3. Butter two or three rings and set them in a buttered pan on the lowest heat. Fill the rings nearly to the top.
  4. Cover and cook about 8 minutes without lifting the lid. Flip the rings carefully, cover again, and cook until the centers are just set.
  5. Remove the rings, dust with powdered sugar, and serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few drops of vanilla
  • Matcha in the batter, which turns them green and slightly bitter

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Powdered sugar
  • Butter
  • Whipped cream and berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Southeastern corn griddle cakes

Plain corn, salt, and a toasted surface, with no sugar and no wheat.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Southeastern corn griddle cakes sit in the family of corn breads cooked by Native nations of the Southeast, where corn was the staple grain long before wheat arrived. Those breads are not all the same. This plain cornmeal cake does not belong to one nation, and it is not a ceremonial bread rewritten as a diner recipe. The batter is meal, water, and salt, cooked on a griddle.

Cultural background

The Tennessee River valley is a geographic pin for the wider Southeast, not a claim that one town or one nation owns the cake. Hopi piki is a different food and has its own page. Wheat is left out on purpose.

Why it tastes this way

The flavor is toasted cornmeal and salt. Water instead of milk keeps it plain. A moderate thickness gives a soft center and a firm crust. Sugar, egg, and wheat would each pull it toward a later American pancake.

How it is made

Plain cornmeal batter cooked as medium rounds on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook meal, water, and salt. Do not present the result as the ceremonial bread of a nation, and do not say that every Southeastern nation made this exact cake.

How it is used

Hot, as a bread. It needs no syrup.

Field notes

  • Corn was the staple of Southeastern Native nations long before wheat flour arrived.
  • This recipe is a plain griddle cake in that family, and it is not owned by one nation.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup warm water
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal, salt, water, and oil into a batter that holds a shape and still spreads a little.
  2. Rest 5 minutes. If it stiffened, stir in a spoon of water.
  3. Film a griddle with oil over medium heat.
  4. Drop about 1/4 cup of batter for each cake and nudge it into a round about 1/2 inch thick.
  5. Cook until the underside is brown and the edge looks dry, then flip and cook until the center is set.
  6. Serve hot as a bread.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A thinner batter, cooked crisp, moves toward lacy cornbread.
  • Adding wheat flour, buttermilk, and egg makes a New England-style corn cake and adds those allergens.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Salt
  • Cooked greens or beans
  • A drizzle of oil
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Southern banana pancakes

Ripe banana mashed into a tangy buttermilk pancake, soft and sweet.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Southern banana pancakes are buttermilk cakes with ripe banana in the batter. Bananas arrived in Gulf ports as an imported fruit and became an ordinary grocery item, and the cake is a home breakfast built on that fruit. It is separate from the New Orleans banana-sauce dessert and from Hawaiian diner hotcakes. Mobile is the map pin because it is a Gulf port.

Cultural background

A very ripe banana, with a spotted peel, mashes smooth and tastes sweet enough that the batter needs little sugar. Green bananas stay starchy and dull.

Why it tastes this way

Mashed banana adds sugar and a floral flavor and makes the crumb moist. Buttermilk keeps it from cloying. Slices on top brown in the butter. If the banana is underripe, the cakes taste flat and dry.

How it is made

Griddled buttermilk batter with mashed banana and banana slices.

Authentic touch. Mash a ripe banana into the batter and put two or three slices on each cake as it cooks. This is not the rum sauce of Bananas Foster.

How it is used

Hot, with butter. Syrup is optional.

Field notes

  • Bananas became a Southern grocery item through the fruit trade at Gulf ports.
  • This cake is separate from Bananas Foster and from Hawaiian macadamia hotcakes.

Ingredients

  • 1 very ripe banana, mashed, plus 1 ripe banana, sliced
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mash one banana until mostly smooth.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  3. Stir in the mashed banana, buttermilk, egg, and melted butter until just combined.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium-low heat. Pour 1/4 cup cakes and press two or three banana slices onto each.
  5. Flip when the underside is golden. The slices brown quickly, so watch the heat.
  6. Serve when the center is set.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave the slices off and keep the banana only in the batter.
  • A pinch of cinnamon is optional and not required.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • A few extra banana slices
  • Coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Southern buttermilk pancakes

Tangy buttermilk, a little sugar, and a soft crumb with a browned rim.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Southern buttermilk pancakes are thick wheat cakes that became a regional breakfast after chemical leaveners and readily available dairy were both in the kitchen. The acid in buttermilk reacts with baking soda and keeps the crumb tender. Atlanta is only the map pin: the same stack is a home and diner breakfast across the South. Butter and a pour of cane syrup or sorghum usually finish the plate.

Cultural background

This is the later wheat stack, separate from cornmeal hoecakes and from rice cakes. Baking powder and soda are nineteenth-century tools, and they are what make this cake rise as high as it does.

Why it tastes this way

Buttermilk tastes slightly sour, and that sourness is the point, not a flaw. Melted butter in the batter and on the griddle browns at the edge. Stop stirring once the flour disappears: extra stirring tightens gluten and turns the crumb tough.

How it is made

Griddled in butter over medium heat until the center is set.

Authentic touch. Use real buttermilk, not regular milk with a splash of lemon. The griddle is ready when a drop of water skitters.

How it is used

Stacked hot, with butter and cane syrup or sorghum.

Field notes

  • Chemical leaveners, not yeast, are what lift this particular Southern stack.
  • A watery batter makes them spread thin, and a stiff batter makes them heavy.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 cups buttermilk
  • 2 large eggs
  • 3 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt in a bowl.
  2. In a second bowl, whisk the buttermilk, eggs, and melted butter.
  3. Pour the wet ingredients into the dry and stir just until no dry flour remains. A few lumps are fine.
  4. Heat a griddle over medium heat and film it with butter. A drop of water should skitter.
  5. Pour about 1/4 cup batter for each cake. Cook until the bubbles on top pop and stay open, then flip and cook until the center is set.
  6. Serve at once with butter and cane syrup or sorghum.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave the sugar out when the plate is savory.
  • Replace two tablespoons of the flour with cornmeal for a coarser crumb.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Cane syrup
  • Sorghum syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 8 hoecakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Southern hoecakes

Plain toasted corn, salt, and a fried edge, denser than a wheat pancake.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 hoecakes

History

Southern hoecakes are round cornmeal cakes cooked on a griddle. The story that they were baked on the blade of a hoe is widely repeated and disputed, and this page does not treat that story as fact. Hoecakes were ordinary food in the South, including at eighteenth-century Virginia tables such as Mount Vernon. This version is meal, salt, and boiling water, with no wheat.

Cultural background

Corn was the everyday grain. A hoecake was a way to cook a quick bread on a flat iron when there was no oven loaf. The name survives even though the hoe-blade explanation is not settled.

Why it tastes this way

Scalding the meal softens the coarse parts and lets the cake hold together without egg or flour. The surface against the fat tastes toasted. The inside stays moist and distinctly of corn.

How it is made

Shaped cornmeal rounds griddled in a thin film of oil.

Authentic touch. Make them thicker than a Rhode Island johnnycake, about a finger thick, and cook them through. Ignore the hoe story and use a griddle.

How it is used

Hot, as a bread beside beans or with syrup if you want them sweet.

Field notes

  • The hoe-blade baking story is famous and disputed.
  • George Washington's household at Mount Vernon ate hoecakes, which is well documented.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups cornmeal
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups boiling water
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal, salt, and oil in a bowl.
  2. Pour in the boiling water and stir to a dough stiff enough to hold a round. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. With wet hands, shape 8 rounds about 1/2 inch thick.
  4. Heat a film of oil on a griddle over medium heat.
  5. Cook the cakes until the underside is browned and the side looks set, about 4 minutes, then flip and cook until the center is no longer wet.
  6. Serve hot. They are bread, not a tall diner stack.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Grease the griddle with bacon drippings if you eat pork.
  • A spoon of wheat flour in the batter is a later, softer version and is no longer gluten-free.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Potlikker or beans
  • Sorghum syrup
  • Butter, if you eat dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Spain · No chili heat · 12 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Spain · Southern Europe

Spanish tortitas

Small thick Spanish pancakes with lemon, a home breakfast, not a tortilla.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 small pancakes

History

Tortitas in this sense are small thick pancakes cooked in homes across Spain, served with sugar, jam, or chocolate. They are not tortilla de patatas, which is an omelet and has its own page that says so. They are not Galician Carnival filloas and not the shrimp fritters of Cádiz. This is the everyday wheat pancake, raised with baking powder, scented with lemon.

Cultural background

They are a breakfast and a children's snack. The name tortita is also used for other small round sweets. On this page it means the poured pancake. A heavy pan and a spoon are the tools.

Why it tastes this way

Baking powder makes them softer and thicker than a filloa. Lemon zest keeps the sweetness bright. Butter browns the outside. Sugar on top melts into the crust. They taste like a small breakfast cake, not like olive oil and potato.

How it is made

A thick batter of flour, egg, milk, baking powder, and lemon is cooked as small rounds and sugared.

Authentic touch. Do not confuse the name with a Spanish tortilla. If someone asks for tortilla, they may want the omelet. These are tortitas, plural of the small pancake, served with sugar.

How it is used

At breakfast or as a snack, with sugar or jam.

Field notes

  • Tortitas here means small pancakes, not a potato omelet.
  • The omelet is the Spanish tortilla page.
  • Filloas and frixuelos are the thin Carnival crepes.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • 1 tbsp sugar, plus more for serving
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 200 ml whole milk
  • 1 tsp lemon zest
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, sugar, and salt. Beat in the egg, milk, and lemon zest until just smooth.
  2. Cook spoonfuls in butter over medium heat until bubbles set, then turn.
  3. Cook until the centers spring back.
  4. Sprinkle with sugar and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A banana slice on the batter
  • Filloas, the thin Carnival crepe, which are a different page

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Snack
  • Sugar
  • Jam
  • A square of chocolate, melted on the hot cake if you want it
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sri Lanka · No chili heat · 8 hoppers

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sri Lanka · South Asia

Sri Lankan hoppers

A crisp-edged rice bowl with a soft coconut center and a light ferment tang.

35 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 hoppers

History

Hoppers are Sri Lanka's bowl-shaped rice cakes, kin to Kerala appam and still their own daily bread. Fermented rice and coconut milk are poured into a small curved pan, swirled, and covered. Coconut toddy is a traditional ferment. Yeast is the home substitute in this recipe. A hopper plate in Colombo might come with lunumiris or seeni sambol. Egg hoppers, with an egg broken into the center, are a separate page.

Cultural background

Hopper shops pour from a bowl of batter that has worked overnight. The edge laces where it is thin, and the middle stays pale and spongy. String hoppers, which are pressed rice noodles, are a different food and not this pan.

Why it tastes this way

Coconut milk sweetens the rice and keeps the center custardy. Fermentation adds a clean sour note that toddy makes fruitier and yeast makes milder. The hot curved wall fries the thin film into a cracker while the covered center steams. Salt is spare. The sambol beside the hopper carries chile and, often, dried fish.

How it is made

Ferment rice ground with coconut milk, swirl it in a hot hopper pan, and cover until the bowl sets.

Authentic touch. Swirl once and cover. If you leave the batter pooled without climbing the sides, you have a thick pancake, not a hopper.

How it is used

Serve immediately with onion sambol. Some sambols include dried Maldive fish, which would add a fish allergen. The hopper batter here does not.

Field notes

  • Sri Lankan hoppers and Kerala appam share a pan shape and keep different names and tables.
  • An egg cracked into the center is an egg hopper, written on its own page.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 1/4 cup cooked rice
  • 3/4 cup coconut milk
  • 1/4 teaspoon dry yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water as needed
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Soak the raw rice for 3 hours, drain, and grind it with the cooked rice and a little water until smooth.
  2. Stir in coconut milk, yeast, sugar, and salt. Aim for a batter that pours in a ribbon.
  3. Ferment covered until bubbly, often 6 to 12 hours. Exclude that rest from active prep.
  4. Heat a hopper pan, grease it, pour a ladle, and swirl so the sides film and the center stays thick.
  5. Cover and cook on medium-low until the rim is crisp and the center is spongy and set. Repeat.

Discard batter that smells rotten or grows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Toddy in place of yeast where it is sold fresh and safe
  • A sweeter center with an extra spoon of coconut milk

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Onion sambol without dried fish
  • Seeni sambol
  • A mild coconut gravy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 large oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Staffordshire oatcakes

Large, soft, yeasted oatmeal pancakes from the Potteries, rolled around a savory filling.

1 hr 20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 large oatcakes

History

Staffordshire oatcakes are yeasted oatmeal pancakes from the Potteries, the towns around Stoke-on-Trent. They are large, thin, and flexible, sold in oatcake shops and rolled around bacon, cheese, or sausage. They are not the dry Scottish oatcake, and they are not the Derbyshire batter on the next page. Both counties use yeast and oats. The Staffordshire cake is oat-heavy and meant to fold.

Cultural background

An oatcake shop batter is a local product. Home cooks keep a jug of it warm so the yeast stays alive. The cake is breakfast and lunch food, eaten hot.

Why it tastes this way

Oatmeal makes the crumb soft and a little sweet, and the yeast adds a mild sour note. The griddle dries the surface without turning it into a biscuit. A salty filling, cheese or bacon, is the usual contrast. This recipe leaves the filling for the plate so the batter stays meat-free.

How it is made

Fine oatmeal and a smaller share of wheat flour are mixed with yeast and warm milk, left until bubbly, then poured as large thin rounds on a greased bakestone or frying pan.

Authentic touch. Cook them large and thin enough to roll. A cake that cracks when folded needs a looser batter or a slightly hotter pan and a shorter cook.

How it is used

Hot, rolled or folded with cheese. Bacon or sausage is the shop-counter filling and adds meat. The batter itself is vegetarian.

Field notes

  • Potteries oatcakes are soft and rolled. Scottish oatcakes are crisp and dry.
  • Oatcake shops in Stoke-on-Trent still sell them as everyday food.
  • The batter is yeasted. It is not a baking-powder pancake.

Ingredients

  • 200 g fine oatmeal
  • 80 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 450 ml warm milk and water, mixed half and half
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • Oil or butter, for the pan
  • 100 g cheddar, for serving

Steps

  1. Stir the oatmeal, flour, yeast, sugar, salt, and warm liquid into a batter like thin cream. Cover and leave until foamy, about 1 hour.
  2. Heat a wide pan or griddle and grease it lightly.
  3. Pour a thin round and cook until the top sets and the underside is pale gold. Turn once and cook through.
  4. Stack the cakes in a cloth so they stay flexible. Fill with cheese, roll, and eat hot.

Keep the rising batter covered. Discard it if it smells rotten.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Derbyshire oatcakes, a related but thicker local batter
  • A sweet roll with butter and honey, less typical than the savory shop cake

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Melted cheddar
  • Bacon or a pork sausage, if you eat meat
  • A grilled tomato
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sweden · No chili heat · about 35 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sweden · Northern Europe

Swedish plättar

Tiny Swedish pancakes from a plätt pan, with jam and cream.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 35 small pancakes

History

Plättar are small Swedish pancakes cooked in a plättjärn, a pan with several round dips, often seven. The batter is the thin pannkaka batter, and the pan is what changes the cake. They are a home breakfast and a dessert, served with jam and sometimes cream. They are not poffertjes, which are yeasted and Dutch, and they are not the large pannkakor on the previous page.

Cultural background

A plätt pan is a normal tool in a Swedish kitchen. Without one, you can cook tiny pancakes in an ordinary pan, and you should say that is a substitute. The shape and the browned rim come from the mold.

Why it tastes this way

The small round browns at the edge and stays tender in the middle. Butter in the cups adds flavor fast because the cakes are thin. Jam is tart against a lightly sweet batter. They taste the same family as pannkakor, with more crust per bite.

How it is made

A thin egg batter is spooned into a buttered plätt pan, turned once, and served with jam.

Authentic touch. Grease each cup, not only the first one. Fill the dips shallowly. Turn the plättar with a thin skewer when the top has set.

How it is used

In a pile, with jam, at breakfast or as dessert. Cream is optional.

Field notes

  • A plätt pan often has seven dips.
  • The batter is close to pannkakor. The mold is the difference.
  • They are not Dutch poffertjes, which use yeast and buckwheat.

Ingredients

  • 120 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • 350 ml whole milk
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, salt, eggs, and milk into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Heat a plätt pan and butter each cup.
  3. Spoon in a shallow layer. Cook until the surface sets, turn, and brown the other side.
  4. Serve in a pile with jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory plätt with cheese, less usual than jam
  • The same batter as larger pannkakor when you have no plätt pan

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Lingonberry or strawberry jam
  • Whipped cream
  • Sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Sweet potato pancakes

Orange-fleshed sweet potato, a little cinnamon, and a moist wheat crumb.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Sweet potato pancakes mash an orange-fleshed sweet potato into a wheat batter. Sweet potatoes have been a Southern field crop for a long time, in Native gardens and later in the kitchens of enslaved and free Black cooks and of white farm families. The griddle cake is a breakfast use of the mashed root. Wilson, North Carolina, is a modern market town for the crop and the map pin, not a claim that the pancake was invented there.

Cultural background

The root and the pancake are different ages. This page is the wheat breakfast cake. It does not assign an older sweet-potato bread to one community.

Why it tastes this way

Mashed sweet potato adds moisture and a flavor closer to roasted squash than to white potato. Cinnamon sits on top of that sweetness. Because the mash is wet, the flour has to be enough to keep the cakes from slumping.

How it is made

Griddled wheat batter enriched with mashed sweet potato.

Authentic touch. Use a cooked, mashed sweet potato with a drier texture, not a can of syrup-packed pieces. Orange-fleshed varieties are what most Southern cooks mean.

How it is used

Hot, with butter. They are sweet enough that syrup can be light.

Field notes

  • Wilson, North Carolina, is a long-running market center for Southern sweet potatoes.
  • The mashed root makes the batter wetter than a plain buttermilk pancake, so the flour amount matters.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup mashed cooked sweet potato, cooled
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mash the sweet potato until smooth.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, cinnamon, and salt.
  3. Whisk the sweet potato with the buttermilk, eggs, and melted butter. Stir that into the flour just until combined.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium-low heat. The sugar and sweet potato brown quickly.
  5. Pour 1/4 cup cakes. Flip when the underside is deep gold and the top has set.
  6. Cook the second side until the center is no longer wet. Serve with butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Roast the sweet potato instead of boiling it for a deeper flavor.
  • Skip the cinnamon for a plainer cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • A thin drizzle of sorghum
  • Pecans only if you want a tree-nut topping
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Russia · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Syrniki

Pan-fried Russian farmer-cheese cakes, with sour cream and jam.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Syrniki are Russian pan-fried cakes of tvorog, egg, a little flour, and sugar. They are breakfast and dessert, not yeasted blini and not potato latkes. The same curd-cheese idea appears in other countries, including the Estonian kohupiim cakes, which have their own page. This one is the Russian breakfast cake. Flour is the binder, so they are not gluten-free.

Cultural background

Tvorog is a staple cheese. Frying it into cakes is a home method, done in butter, and served as soon as the crust is brown. Sour cream and jam are the plate.

Why it tastes this way

The cheese is tangy and rich. Sugar makes them dessert-like without hiding the tang. A thin flour coating and a little flour inside create a crust. The center stays soft, almost custardy, if the cheese was not overworked. Burnt butter tastes harsh, so the pan should be medium, not smoking.

How it is made

Drained farmer cheese is mixed with egg, sugar, and flour, shaped into small cakes, floured, and fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Drain the tvorog until it is closer to a paste than a yogurt. Wet cheese will not hold a cake. Flour the outside lightly so the crust browns.

How it is used

Hot, with sour cream and jam, at breakfast or as a sweet course.

Field notes

  • Syrniki are fried cheese cakes, not blini.
  • Tvorog, a dry farmer cheese, is the main ingredient. Flour only binds them.
  • Sour cream and jam are the usual plate.

Ingredients

  • 400 g tvorog or farmer cheese, well drained
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tbsp sugar
  • 1/4 tsp fine salt
  • 40 g plain wheat flour, plus more for shaping
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for frying
  • Sour cream and jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Mash the cheese with the egg, sugar, salt, and flour until it holds together. Do not beat it into a glue.
  2. Shape eight small cakes with floured hands and dust the outsides with flour.
  3. Fry in butter over medium heat until both sides are golden and the center is hot.
  4. Serve with sour cream and jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Raisins in the cheese
  • Estonian kohupiim pancakes, a related curd cake on its own page

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Sour cream
  • Jam
  • Honey, which adds a honey allergen
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Taiwan · No chili heat · 2 rolled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Taiwan · East Asia

Taiwanese danbing

A thin breakfast crepe with egg cooked on its face, brushed with soy sauce and rolled with scallion.

10 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 rolled crepes

History

Danbing is Taiwan's egg crepe, a breakfast from street griddles and from the windows of morning shops. Dan means egg. A thin wheat batter is spread, an egg is cooked on it, soy sauce and scallion go over the top, and the sheet is rolled. Fillings such as pork floss or cheese are common additions. This recipe keeps the egg crepe itself, without meat, so the tags stay vegetarian. It is Taipei breakfast food, fast and savory.

Cultural background

The griddle is flat and lightly oiled. The crepe has to stay flexible enough to roll and strong enough to hold the egg. Breakfast stands assemble it in a minute. At home the same batter works in a nonstick pan. Pork floss, if you add it, brings a meat allergen the written recipe does not have.

Why it tastes this way

Egg against a toasted wheat sheet is the whole flavor, salted hard by soy sauce. Scallion cuts the richness. Because the egg is spread thin, it tastes fried rather than like a folded omelet. The crepe itself is almost bland, a carrier, which is why the sauce matters. It is a breakfast built for eating out of a bag.

How it is made

Cook a thin wheat crepe, spread an egg on it with soy sauce and scallion, and roll it tight.

Authentic touch. Spread the egg while the crepe is still moist so they fuse. Brush soy sauce on the egg, not only on the plate, or the roll tastes flat.

How it is used

Eat it immediately, rolled, as a street breakfast. A hot soy milk is the usual cup beside it.

Field notes

  • Danbing means egg pancake. It is a Taiwanese breakfast crepe.
  • The plain egg roll is vegetarian. Pork floss is an addition, not the base recipe.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2/3 cup water
  • A pinch of salt
  • 2 eggs
  • 1 tablespoon soy sauce
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, water, and salt into a thin batter.
  2. Oil a pan over medium heat. Pour half the batter and swirl a thin crepe.
  3. When the top is nearly set, crack an egg onto it and spread the white and yolk into a thin layer.
  4. Brush with soy sauce and scatter scallion. Cook until the egg sets.
  5. Roll the crepe tightly. Repeat with the second egg.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pork floss in the roll, which adds meat
  • A slice of processed cheese, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Sweet or salty soy milk
  • A few slices of cucumber
  • Chile crisp on the side
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 triangles

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Tattie scones

Thin potato triangles from a Scottish girdle, eaten at breakfast.

15 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 triangles

History

Tattie scones are Scottish potato scones: mashed potato, a little flour and fat, rolled thin, cut into triangles, and cooked on a girdle. Tattie means potato. They are part of a fried breakfast or a weekend plate with eggs. They are not Lancashire's thicker fried rounds, and they are not boxty. Flour in the dough means they are not gluten-free.

Cultural background

Cold mash from the night before is the usual start. The scones are cooked in a dry or lightly greased pan so the outside toasts without becoming a fritter.

Why it tastes this way

Potato makes them mild and soft. A thin roll means the heat reaches the center and the surface dries. Salt and pepper matter because there is little else. Browning at the edge is the flavor. Butter on the hot scone melts into the face that was against the girdle.

How it is made

Mashed potato is kneaded with butter and flour, rolled into a thin round, quartered, and cooked on a hot girdle until spotted.

Authentic touch. Cut the round into quarters. A farl here is a practical cut, not the Ulster soda farl. Keep them thin enough that you can see the girdle color through the dough at the edge.

How it is used

At breakfast, with eggs, bacon, or butter. They are a savory cake.

Field notes

  • Tattie is potato. The scones are potato, not a sweet oven scone.
  • They are rolled thin and cut in triangles or quarters.
  • They belong on a Scottish breakfast plate.

Ingredients

  • 400 g cold mashed potato
  • 30 g unsalted butter, plus more for serving
  • 80 g plain wheat flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp black pepper

Steps

  1. Mash the potato with the butter, salt, and pepper until smooth. Work in the flour to a soft dough.
  2. Roll into two thin rounds on a floured board and cut each into quarters.
  3. Cook on a hot dry girdle until brown spots appear, then turn and cook the other side.
  4. Serve warm with butter, eggs, or bacon.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of chopped chives
  • Lancashire potato cakes, thicker and pan-fried in more fat

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Eggs
  • Bacon or square sausage, for a meat breakfast
  • Butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Texas pecan pancakes

Buttered wheat cakes with toasted pecan, sweet and slightly bitter at the brown edge of the nut.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Texas pecan pancakes put a native nut into a wheat breakfast cake. Pecan trees grow wild and in orchards across Texas, and Indigenous peoples of the region used the nut long before a diner pancake did. The cake itself is a twentieth-century breakfast. San Saba is a pecan-growing town and the map pin.

Cultural background

Toasting the nuts first is what makes them taste like pecans rather than pale crumbs. The wheat batter is the usual buttermilk stack with nuts folded through.

Why it tastes this way

Pecans are oily. Heat releases that oil and turns the flavor round and toasted. If the nuts go in raw, the cakes taste flatter. A bitter note from the brown skin is part of a real pecan, so do not expect a bland crunch.

How it is made

Griddled buttermilk batter with toasted pecans in and on the cakes.

Authentic touch. Toast the pecans in a dry skillet until they smell nutty, then chop them. Scatter a few more on the batter after you pour it.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and syrup, and extra pecans on the plate.

Field notes

  • The pecan is native to Texas and the wider South.
  • San Saba built a town identity on pecan growing, which is why this pin sits there.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup chopped pecans
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Toast the pecans in a dry skillet over medium heat, stirring, until they smell fragrant. Cool them, then chop if they are still in large pieces.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt. Stir in half of the pecans.
  3. Add the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter. Stir until just combined.
  4. Butter a griddle over medium heat and pour 1/4 cup cakes. Scatter a few of the remaining pecans on each cake.
  5. Flip when the tops set. Cook until the nuts on the bottom are brown, not black.
  6. Serve with butter, syrup, and the rest of the pecans.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave the nuts on top only if you need to cook a nut-free cake for someone else, and use a clean pan.
  • A darker sorghum syrup stands up to the nut better than a very light syrup.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Butter
  • Maple syrup or sorghum
  • More toasted pecans
  • Vegetarian

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 6 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Thalipeeth

A hearty multigrain griddle bread, onion-flecked, with toasted millet and chickpea in the crumb.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 flatbreads

History

Thalipeeth is a Maharashtrian griddle bread made from bhajani, a mix of roasted flours. Sorghum, millet, rice, chickpea, and wheat are a typical blend, though every household flour tin varies. Onion and cilantro are patted into the dough. It is a flatbread, not a poured dosa. This recipe includes wheat, so it is not gluten-free. Pune snacks and home breakfasts both rely on it.

Cultural background

Bhajani was a way to use several local grains in one dough. Roasting the flours before they are stored gives thalipeeth its toasted smell even before the pan. A hole poked in the center is a common cook's habit so the middle griddles through.

Why it tastes this way

Roasted sorghum and millet taste nutty and slightly sweet. Besan adds chickpea. Wheat makes the dough easier to pat. Onion and green chile steam inside the bread and keep the grains from tasting dry. Oil on the griddle, rather than a yeasted crumb, is the richness. There is no ferment tang.

How it is made

Mix a multigrain dough with onion and spices, pat it into thick rounds, and griddle both sides with oil.

Authentic touch. Pat the dough on a wet leaf, plastic sheet, or damp cloth. It cracks if you treat it like a smooth wheat chapati. Oil the cracks and keep going.

How it is used

Serve warm with a dry garlic chutney or a spoon of yogurt. Yogurt is a dairy side. The bread itself is cooked here in oil.

Field notes

  • Thalipeeth is a patted flatbread, not a ladle-poured pancake.
  • This blend includes wheat flour, so the bread is not gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup sorghum flour
  • 1/4 cup rice flour
  • 1/4 cup besan
  • 1/2 cup whole wheat flour
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • Warm water and neutral oil

Steps

  1. Stir the flours, salt, and cumin together. Add onion, chile, and cilantro.
  2. Add warm water a little at a time and mix to a stiff dough that can be patted. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. With wet hands, pat a ball into a thick round on a damp sheet. Poke a small hole in the center if you like.
  4. Transfer it to a hot oiled griddle. Cook on medium, oiling the surface, until both sides are spotted brown and the center is cooked.
  5. Repeat with the remaining dough. Serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ghee in place of oil, which adds dairy
  • A little grated cucumber if the dough feels stiff

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Dry garlic chutney
  • Plain yogurt as a side
  • Sliced red onion
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

China · Mild · 2 wrapped crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Tianjin jianbing

A soft savory crepe wrapped around egg, sauce, herbs, and a shatter of crisp fried dough.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 wrapped crepes

History

Tianjin jianbing is a street breakfast crepe. The cook spreads a thin batter, cracks an egg onto it, paints a savory sauce, adds scallion, cilantro, and chile, then presses in a crisp fried dough stick, the youtiao or guozi, and folds the crepe around it. Tianjin is the city most tied to this wrapped form. A Shandong jianbing is a different street crepe, often cracker-crisp and torn, and it has its own page. This one is the egg-and-fritter package.

Cultural background

Morning stalls work fast on a round griddle. The crepe stays flexible so it can wrap. The fritter supplies the crunch the soft sheet does not have. Sauces vary. A sweet-savory bean sauce and a chile sauce are the usual pair. This recipe uses those and skips any brand name.

Why it tastes this way

Egg fried onto the crepe tastes rich against a plain grain sheet. Fermented bean sauce is salty and slightly sweet. Cilantro and scallion freshen it. Chile keeps the heat mild unless you add more. The youtiao is dry, airy, and fried, so it crackles inside a tender wrap. The combination is the Tianjin breakfast: soft, crisp, savory, and eaten on the way to work.

How it is made

Cook a thin crepe, fry an egg onto it, add sauce and herbs, press in a crisp youtiao, and fold.

Authentic touch. Crack the egg onto the crepe while the batter is still wet and break the yolk with the spreader. An egg added after the sheet dries will not bond, and the wrap falls apart.

How it is used

Eat it at once, folded, while the fritter is still loud. It is a street breakfast.

Field notes

  • Tianjin jianbing guozi is the crepe wrapped around a crisp fried dough stick.
  • The egg is cooked on the crepe, not scrambled in a separate pan.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons mung bean flour or more wheat flour
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 2 eggs
  • 2 crisp youtiao (fried dough sticks)
  • 2 tablespoons sweet bean sauce or fermented bean sauce
  • Chile sauce to taste
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • A handful of cilantro
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Whisk the flours and water into a thin crepe batter.
  2. Oil a griddle over medium heat. Pour half the batter and spread a thin round.
  3. Crack an egg on the crepe, break the yolk, and spread it. When the top sets, flip so the egg browns briefly, then flip back.
  4. Paint on bean sauce and chile sauce. Add scallion and cilantro. Press a youtiao onto the crepe so it cracks.
  5. Fold the crepe around the fritter. Repeat with the rest.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lettuce or a cracker in place of youtiao
  • More chile sauce for a hotter wrap

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Soy milk
  • A second chile spoon if you want more heat
  • Pickled mustard tuber
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Tunisia · No chili heat · 6 round flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Tunisia · North Africa

Tunisian mlawi

Soft, oiled, layered round with a flaky edge and a mild wheat taste.

35 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 round flatbreads

History

Mlawi, also written mlaoui, is the layered flatbread of a Tunisian breakfast. Oiled dough is coiled or folded into a round, flattened, and cooked on a griddle. It is a cousin of Moroccan msemen and it is not the same bread: the Tunis version is usually a round breakfast flatbread, eaten with harissa, olive oil, honey, or an egg. This page keeps that shape and that table.

Cultural background

In Tunis, mlawi is early food, sold at stalls and cooked at home before the day heats up. The bread itself is not a chile dough. Heat arrives when someone spoons harissa beside it. A tuna or egg breakfast is a stall habit and adds fish or egg.

Why it tastes this way

Oil between the layers fries them lightly, so the bread tastes toasted and rich without butter. Semolina in the dough gives a fine grit. Because it is coiled into a round and then pressed, the flakes spiral instead of sitting in a Moroccan square. Harissa on the side is sour and hot against a mild crumb.

How it is made

Make a soft dough of flour, semolina, salt, water, and oil. Rest it, stretch it into a ribbon, brush it with olive oil, and coil it into a disk. Flatten the disk and griddle it until puffed in places and golden on both sides.

Authentic touch. Coil the oiled strip into a round, then flatten it. Do not cut the recipe across into msemen squares and rename them. The bread is unspiced. Put harissa on the plate, not in the dough, if you want this version.

How it is used

Mlawi is breakfast and street food. Eat it with olive oil and harissa, with honey, or as a wrap around an egg. Tuna is a common stall addition and would add a fish allergen that this dough does not have.

Field notes

  • Mlawi is a Tunisian layered flatbread, distinct from Moroccan msemen.
  • It is also written mlaoui.
  • The dough is mild. Harissa is served beside it rather than mixed in.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 1/2 cup fine semolina
  • 1 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 3 tablespoons olive oil, plus more for brushing

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, semolina, salt, water, and a spoon of olive oil into a soft dough. Knead briefly and rest it, covered, for 15 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough into 6 pieces. Stretch one piece into a long thin ribbon and brush it with olive oil.
  3. Coil the ribbon into a round, then press or roll the round flat. This layered round is mlawi, not a square of msemen.
  4. Repeat with the remaining dough, keeping the coils covered so the oil does not dry.
  5. Cook on a hot griddle with a trace of oil, turning once, until both sides are golden and the layers at the edge flake.

The dough contains wheat. Harissa served alongside is hot even though the bread is tagged with no chile heat.

Reheat leftover mlawi on a skillet. Refrigerate it once cool. Olive oil on the surface can go stale if the bread sits out. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The spelling mlaoui points to the same bread.
  • A square fold is closer to msemen. This recipe stays round.
  • Adding tuna or egg at the stall changes the allergens and the diet tags.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Harissa and olive oil
  • Honey
  • A fried egg, which adds egg
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Turkey · No chili heat · 4 large flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Turkey · Middle East

Turkish gözleme

Thin stuffed flatbread, griddled, with spinach and salty white cheese in the fold.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 large flatbreads

History

Gözleme is a stuffed flatbread. A soft wheat dough is rolled thin, filled, sealed, and cooked on a sac, the wide convex griddle, or in a heavy pan. It is a village and street food across Turkey, not a batter you pour. Spinach and white cheese is a common filling. Potato and minced meat are others. This recipe is the spinach and cheese one, cooked on a griddle, from a Denizli-style home kitchen.

Cultural background

At markets, the cook rolls the dough in the open and fills it to order. At home it is a way to turn greens and cheese into a meal. The bread is cut into pieces as soon as it leaves the pan. It is savory breakfast, lunch, or a snack.

Why it tastes this way

The dough tastes of wheat and salt, with brown spots from the griddle. Spinach keeps a green, slightly metallic flavor if it is only wilted, not boiled to mush. Salty white cheese seasons the filling so the bread does not need a sauce. A slick of butter on the finished bread tastes milky and rich. There is no sugar.

How it is made

Mix a soft dough and rest it. Wilt spinach with onion, salt it, and mix it with crumbled white cheese. Roll each dough piece thin, spread filling on half, fold, and cook on a hot sac or skillet, turning, until blistered. Brush with a little butter.

Authentic touch. Roll the dough thin, seal the filling inside, and cook it on a griddle. Say what it is: a stuffed flatbread, not a pancake. A minced-meat filling is a different batch and is not vegetarian.

How it is used

Gözleme is a savory meal or a street snack, often breakfast. It is eaten with tea and sometimes with yogurt on the side. It is not a dessert. The meat-filled version is common and is not the diet listed here.

Field notes

  • Gözleme is a stuffed flatbread cooked on a griddle, not a poured pancake.
  • Spinach and white cheese is one of the usual fillings.
  • A sac is the wide metal griddle many cooks still use for it.

Ingredients

  • 2 1/2 cups wheat flour (about 310 g)
  • 1 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 2 tablespoons yogurt
  • 2 tablespoons olive oil
  • 1 onion, chopped
  • 300 g spinach, chopped
  • 150 g salty white cheese, crumbled
  • 1 tablespoon butter, for finishing

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, half the salt, water, yogurt, and olive oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth and rest it for 20 minutes.
  2. Cook the onion in a dry pan until soft, add the spinach and the rest of the salt, and cook until the greens collapse and the pan is almost dry. Cool, then mix in the cheese.
  3. Divide the dough into 4. Roll each piece very thin. This is a stuffed flatbread, not a batter.
  4. Spread filling on half of each round, fold the other half over, and press the edges.
  5. Cook on a hot griddle or sac, turning once, until both sides are spotted and the dough is cooked. Brush with butter and cut into pieces.

The dough contains wheat. Yogurt, cheese, and butter are dairy. A meat filling is not part of this version.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry pan. Do not leave the cheese filling at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A potato and onion filling can be made without cheese if you also change the dough fat. This written filling contains dairy.
  • Minced meat with pepper is a widespread filling and is not vegetarian.
  • Cooking on a flat pan instead of a sac still counts if the dough is thin and turned.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Breakfast
  • Black tea
  • Plain yogurt
  • A wedge of lemon
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Turkey · No chili heat · 1 skillet cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Turkey · Middle East

Turkish kaygana

A soft flour-and-egg skillet cake, herb-flecked, with a tender center and a browned rim.

10 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 2 · 1 skillet cake

History

Kaygana is a Turkish breakfast cake of flour, egg, and milk, cooked in a skillet. Herbs sometimes go in, and so do cheese or pepper in other houses. It is quicker and thinner than a baked herb frittata. This version is the herb one, the kind a Black Sea or inland home might cook on a weekday morning, written here with a Trabzon pin because that coast still keeps a strong breakfast pan. It is not gözleme and not kuku sabzi.

Cultural background

Kaygana uses pantry ingredients when there is no time to roll dough. It is cut in the pan and eaten with bread or instead of it, with tea. The herbs should be fresh. Dried mint is a different, sharper cake.

Why it tastes this way

Egg is the main flavor, with flour taking the edge off so the cake is less like an omelette and more like a tender pancake. Milk softens it. Parsley and dill taste green and slight, not like the huge mass of herbs in an Iranian kuku. The pan butter browns the rim. There is no syrup.

How it is made

Whisk eggs, milk, flour, salt, parsley, and dill into a pourable batter. Cook it in a buttered skillet over moderate heat, covered, until just set. Fold or cut it in the pan. Serve at once with tea.

Authentic touch. Keep it a flour-and-egg skillet cake for breakfast. Do not bake it into a thick frittata and do not stuff it like gözleme. Fresh herbs, if you use them, are mixed into the batter.

How it is used

Kaygana is breakfast and a savory meal. It is eaten with tea, yogurt, or a simple salad. It is not a dessert. Cheese on top is extra dairy on a dish that already has milk.

Field notes

  • Kaygana is a Turkish flour-and-egg pancake, usually cooked for breakfast.
  • Herbs are mixed into the batter in this version.
  • It is thinner and quicker than a baked herb frittata.

Ingredients

  • 3 eggs
  • 1/2 cup milk
  • 1/3 cup wheat flour
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 2 tablespoons chopped parsley
  • 2 tablespoons chopped dill
  • 1 tablespoon butter

Steps

  1. Whisk the eggs, milk, flour, and salt until smooth. Stir in the parsley and dill.
  2. Heat the butter in a skillet over medium heat until it foams.
  3. Pour in the batter and tilt the pan. This is a flour-and-egg cake, not a rolled flatbread.
  4. Cover and cook until the center is just set and the rim is browned. Do not rush it over high heat.
  5. Cut it in the pan and serve for breakfast.

The cake contains wheat, egg, and dairy. Cook it until the egg is set, not liquid in the center.

Refrigerate leftovers the same day and reheat them in a covered pan. Do not leave the egg batter waiting at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of white cheese in the batter is a saltier breakfast.
  • Leave the herbs out for a plain flour-and-egg cake.
  • A little black pepper is enough seasoning. A chile paste would change the heat tag.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Black tea
  • Plain yogurt
  • Sliced tomato
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 4 large farls

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Ulster soda farls

Griddled soda-bread farls from Ulster, cut in quarters.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 large farls

History

Ulster soda farls are soda bread cooked on a griddle and cut into four. The country pin is the United Kingdom because Northern Ireland is the place on this map. The culture is Ulster. Buttermilk and baking soda are the leaven. There is no yeast and no potato in this dough. Potato farls are the other breakfast cake, pinned to Ireland. Both can appear on one Ulster plate. They are different doughs.

Cultural background

A soda farl is breakfast bread and an accompaniment to a fry, soup, or cheese. The griddle gives it a floury crust that an oven soda loaf does not have on the cut side.

Why it tastes this way

Buttermilk and soda taste slightly tangy and mineral. The griddle toasts the bottom. The inside stays close-grained and mild. Salt is low. Butter on the hot farl is most of the flavor people remember.

How it is made

Flour, salt, and baking soda are mixed with buttermilk to a soft dough, patted into a round, cut into farls, and cooked on a dry griddle.

Authentic touch. Handle the dough lightly and cook it as a round you have already quartered. A thin knife eased under the farl keeps it from tearing when you turn it.

How it is used

With butter, at breakfast, or beside soup. They are an accompaniment as much as a cake.

Field notes

  • The pin is the United Kingdom. The culture on the page is Ulster.
  • Soda farls are wheat and buttermilk. Potato farls are mashed potato.
  • Farl means the round was cut into four.

Ingredients

  • 250 g plain wheat flour, plus more for shaping
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/2 tsp baking soda
  • 180 ml buttermilk

Steps

  1. Sift the flour, salt, and baking soda. Stir in the buttermilk until a soft dough forms. Do not knead more than a few turns.
  2. Pat into a round about 2 cm thick on a floured board and cut into four.
  3. Cook on a medium-hot dry griddle for about 8 minutes a side, until the crust is brown and the center is cooked.
  4. Cool slightly, split, and butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of porridge oats on the board, pressed into the top
  • Oven soda bread, which is the loaf form and not this page

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Butter
  • A fried breakfast
  • Cheese and an apple
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 10 thin pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Upper Midwest Swedish pancakes

Thin, eggy, and lightly sweet, rolled around butter and jam.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 thin pancakes

History

These are the thin egg pancakes of Swedish-American kitchens in Minneapolis and the Upper Midwest. The batter is close to a crepe and is usually rolled or folded with butter and jam, often lingonberry. This is the United States page, the cake at church breakfasts and home tables. It is not the page for the Swedish original.

Cultural background

Scandinavian settlement put this breakfast on Upper Midwest tables. The cakes did not replace Indigenous food in the region. They are one immigrant griddle among others. Lingonberry jam is the familiar preserve, and raspberry jam is the common stand-in.

Why it tastes this way

More egg and milk than a diner pancake makes the cake thin and custardy. A resting batter spreads evenly and tastes less floury. Butter in the pan is the browning you taste at the edge of the roll.

How it is made

Thin egg batter swirled in a buttered skillet and rolled.

Authentic touch. Cook them thin enough to roll. A thick, baking-powder pancake is the diner stack, not this page. Lingonberry jam is the preserve most tied to the Swedish-American table.

How it is used

Rolled or folded, with butter and lingonberry or raspberry jam.

Field notes

  • This is the Swedish-American pancake of Minneapolis and the Upper Midwest, not the Swedish original.
  • Church-basement breakfasts helped keep the thin pancakes on the regional table.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 3 large eggs
  • 1 1/2 cups whole milk
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, salt, eggs, milk, and melted butter into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Heat an 8-inch skillet over medium heat and coat it with butter.
  3. Pour about 1/4 cup batter and swirl the pan so the batter covers the bottom.
  4. Cook until the top looks dry and the underside is golden in spots. Flip if you can, or slide the cake out if it is already set.
  5. Roll each cake with a little butter and jam, or fold them on a plate.
  6. Keep finished cakes warm in a low oven while you cook the rest.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A squeeze of lemon and a dusting of sugar is a plainer finish.
  • Whipped cream is a festive table addition and is still dairy.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Lingonberry jam
  • Butter
  • Raspberry jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 6 uttapams

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Uttapam

A thick, soft fermented cake topped with onion, tomato, and green chile.

35 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 uttapams

History

Uttapam is the thick member of the South Indian fermented rice and urad family. Instead of a thin spread, the cook pours a deeper round and presses onion, tomato, or chile into the wet top. Tamil homes and vegetarian hotels both claim it as everyday breakfast food. It uses the dosa ferment, then chooses toppings and thickness rather than a new flour.

Cultural background

The toppings go on while the batter is still soft so they sink and hold. A crisp paper dosa cannot carry tomato pieces. Uttapam can. Green chile keeps the heat mild if you leave the seeds out.

Why it tastes this way

A deeper batter stays moist, so the ferment tastes round and a little sweet. Onion and tomato steam on the covered pan and add juice. Chile and curry leaf keep it savory. The underside still toasts against the pan, which is why uttapam is a griddle cake and not a steamed idli with vegetables.

How it is made

Pour thick rounds of fermented rice and urad batter, press onion, tomato, and chile into the top, cover, and flip once.

Authentic touch. Press the toppings in with a spatula as soon as you pour. If you wait until the top dries, they fall off when you flip.

How it is used

Serve open so the vegetables show, with coconut chutney. Two small uttapams make a breakfast.

Field notes

  • Uttapam is thick on purpose. Spreading it thin turns it back into a dosa.
  • Onion, tomato, and chile are toppings on the ferment, not a change of grain.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented rice and urad dosa batter
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 small tomato, seeded and chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Neutral oil for the pan
  • Salt if the batter needs it

Steps

  1. Start with bubbly fermented rice and urad batter. If it is stiff, stir in a spoon of water. It should hold a thick round.
  2. Mix onion, tomato, chile, and cilantro in a bowl. Salt them lightly if the batter is already salted.
  3. Oil a medium-hot pan. Pour a ladle of batter and let it spread only into a cake about a centimeter thick.
  4. Scatter a handful of the vegetable mix on top and press gently. Drizzle oil around the edge. Cover for about 2 minutes.
  5. Flip once and cook until the vegetable side takes a little color. Serve the vegetable side up.

Use fermented batter that smells clean and sour, not rotten.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Grated carrot in place of tomato
  • A few curry leaves pressed into the top

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Corriander leaves
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Venezuela · No chili heat · 4 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Venezuela · South America

Venezuelan arepas

Thick white-corn cakes, griddled until they crackle, split and filled with fresh cheese.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 arepas

History

The corn cake is older than the stuffed breakfast sandwich. People in Venezuela were cooking ground corn on a griddle long before wheat hotcakes, and long before a Caracas counter split the cake and filled it. There is no single pre-Columbian recipe that matches today's stuffed arepa. What is old is the corn cake. Splitting it and stuffing it is a later way of eating it. Precooked white cornmeal, the flour most homes use now, is a twentieth-century shortcut: the corn is cooked and dried before it is milled. It is not nixtamalized masa, and it is not fresh sweet corn.

Cultural background

An arepa in Caracas is breakfast, a packed lunch, and a night-time snack. Fillings run from cheese to black beans to shredded beef. This recipe is the corn cake plus fresh white cheese, the vegetarian counter version. The dough is only precooked cornmeal, water, and salt.

Why it tastes this way

Precooked cornmeal cooks up dense and corny, with a skin that toasts and a center that stays steamy if the dough was wet enough. Salt is the only seasoning in the cake, so the flavor is plain corn, not chile and not wheat. Fresh cheese melts at the edges and salts the interior once the cake is split. The contrast is the point of the modern sandwich: a bland, toasted shell and a rich filling.

How it is made

Stir precooked white cornmeal into warm salted water, rest it, and form thick cakes. Griddle them until a crust forms and the inside is set, then split and fill with cheese.

Authentic touch. The dough should feel like soft clay that does not stick to your hands and does not crack when you form a ball. Seal every crack before the cake hits the griddle, or it will split in the pan. Split it only after it is cooked, with a knife around the edge.

How it is used

Eat them hot for breakfast, plain or filled. A thinner Colombian arepa, a fresh-corn cachapa, and this thick stuffed cake are three different plates.

Field notes

  • The stuffed breakfast arepa is later than the corn cake itself. Do not treat them as one ancient recipe.
  • Precooked cornmeal is not nixtamalized Mexican masa and not the fresh corn in a cachapa.
  • If the dough cracks on the griddle, it needed more water or a wetter hand before cooking.

Ingredients

  • 250 g precooked white cornmeal
  • 360 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 150 g sliced fresh white cheese
  • 1 teaspoon oil for the griddle, if needed

Steps

  1. Stir the salt into the warm water. Add the precooked cornmeal and mix until a soft dough forms. Cover and rest 5 to 10 minutes.
  2. If the dough cracks, wet your hands and knead in a spoon of water. Divide into 4 pieces and form thick cakes about 2 centimeters tall. Smooth every crack.
  3. Cook on a lightly oiled or dry skillet over medium heat for about 7 minutes a side, until a crust forms and the cake sounds slightly hollow.
  4. Cool for a minute, then split each arepa around the edge with a knife, leaving a hinge if you can.
  5. Fill with the cheese and return the cake to the pan just long enough to warm the filling. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Shredded beef or chicken is a usual filling. Either one adds meat and is not vegetarian.
  • Leave the cheese out and the cake is vegan and dairy-free. It is still an arepa.
  • Yellow precooked cornmeal makes a deeper-colored cake. It is still not masa harina.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Black beans as a second filling
  • Guasacaca, a green avocado sauce
  • Black coffee or a small cup of hot chocolate
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Vermont maple pancakes

A mild wheat cake with real maple in the batter and a stronger maple pour on top.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 pancakes

History

Vermont maple pancakes are wheat cakes served with maple syrup from the state's sugaring season. Indigenous nations of the Northeast, including the Abenaki, made maple sugar long before a buttermilk pancake existed. The stack on the plate is the later New England breakfast, with maple as the sweetener. Stowe is the map pin for a sugaring landscape that covers the state.

Cultural background

Maple syrup is graded by color and flavor, and a darker syrup tastes stronger in a batter. The pancake does not replace the older practice of making maple sugar.

Why it tastes this way

A spoon of syrup in the batter perfumes the crumb, but most of the maple taste has to come from syrup on the hot cake, where it stays fluid. Butter carries that flavor across the surface. Boiling the syrup into the batter would dull it.

How it is made

Griddled wheat cakes with maple syrup in the batter and on the plate.

Authentic touch. Use maple syrup, not a flavored table syrup. Darker syrup is easier to taste in the crumb.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and a generous pour of maple syrup.

Field notes

  • Maple sugaring in this region is older than the buttermilk pancake.
  • Darker maple syrup shows up in the batter more clearly than a very light syrup.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 2 tablespoons maple syrup, plus more to serve
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  2. Whisk the buttermilk, maple syrup, egg, and melted butter. Stir into the flour until just combined.
  3. Butter a griddle over medium heat.
  4. Pour 1/4 cup cakes and cook until the bubbles set, then flip.
  5. Cook until the center springs back.
  6. Serve with butter and more maple syrup.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the syrup in the batter and use it only as a topping.
  • A side of warm apples is a common Vermont plate and is not part of the batter.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Maple syrup
  • Butter
  • Crisp bacon, if you eat pork
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 12 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Welsh crempog

Welsh buttermilk griddle cakes, stacked with butter between the layers.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 small cakes

History

Crempog, plural crempogau, is the Welsh griddle cake. Buttermilk and bicarbonate are a common home batter, and yeasted overnight batters are also traditional. This page uses buttermilk. The cakes are stacked with butter between them, which is the serving that matters. Some families make a stack for a birthday, and some make crempog when eggs and fat are used up before Lent. Neither occasion is the only one.

Cultural background

The cake is Welsh-language food as well as a recipe. The country on the map is the United Kingdom. The culture is Welsh. A heavy pan or bakestone is the tool.

Why it tastes this way

Buttermilk and soda give a light tang and a soft crumb. Butter between hot cakes melts and salts each layer. The outside is only just browned. They taste less of sugar than a dessert crepe, unless you add jam.

How it is made

Flour, egg, buttermilk, melted butter, and baking soda are mixed to a pourable batter and cooked as small rounds, then stacked with butter.

Authentic touch. Stack them with a smear of butter on each hot cake, not only on the top one. A yeasted batter, left overnight, is the other honest version.

How it is used

In a buttered stack, at breakfast, tea, a birthday, or Shrove. Jam is optional.

Field notes

  • The plural is crempogau. The stack is buttered between the cakes.
  • Buttermilk versions and yeast versions both exist.
  • Some Welsh families stack them for a birthday.

Ingredients

  • 200 g plain wheat flour
  • 1/2 tsp baking soda
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • 300 ml buttermilk
  • 25 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for stacking and the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, baking soda, and salt. Beat in the eggs, buttermilk, and melted butter until smooth.
  2. Grease a bakestone or frying pan over medium heat.
  3. Cook small ladlefuls until the top dulls, then turn and color the other side.
  4. Stack the cakes with butter between them. Serve the stack warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A yeasted crempog batter left to rise overnight
  • A little oatmeal in place of part of the wheat flour

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Feast or fast day
  • Dessert
  • Salted butter
  • Jam
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Yemen · No chili heat · About 10 spongy cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Yemen · Middle East

Yemeni lahoh

Spongy sorghum cakes, sour and earthy, with holes that hold beans or egg.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · About 10 spongy cakes

History

Yemeni lahoh is a spongy fermented pancake, often on the breakfast table beside foul, which is stewed fava beans, or a fried egg and a spoon of samn, clarified butter. Sorghum is a traditional flour for it. This page uses sorghum alone and stays in Sana'a. Djiboutian lahoh and Somali canjeero are related names with their own recipes. They are not this plate of foul and eggs.

Cultural background

Lahoh is morning food. The batter is mixed ahead, and the pan is busy while tea is poured. Foul is a separate pot. The bread can also be eaten with honey or with a slick of samn. The sour crumb is supposed to meet something rich.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum tastes earthier and drier than wheat, and the ferment adds sourness that cuts through beans and butter. The holes are small cups for foul or egg yolk. Because this batter has no wheat and no sugar, it does not eat like a sweet Djibouti breakfast cake. Samn on top tastes nutty because it is clarified butter, not because the grain is sweet.

How it is made

Mix sorghum flour and water and ferment it until sour. Salt it, then cook rounds on one side in a covered pan until the holes set. Serve with foul, fried eggs, or samn. Those accompaniments are cooked separately. The bread itself contains no wheat.

Authentic touch. Serve it as a Yemeni breakfast with foul or eggs. Cook one side until the surface is spongy. A wheat-and-sugar batter is a different lahoh. This one is sorghum.

How it is used

Yemeni lahoh is breakfast and a savory meal when it is next to foul or eggs. Honey is the sweeter path. Samn and eggs bring dairy and egg to the plate even though the batter has neither.

Field notes

  • Yemeni lahoh is often eaten with foul or eggs.
  • This recipe is sorghum only, so it can be gluten-free if the flour is pure.
  • The Djibouti and Somali pancakes with similar names are separate pages.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups sorghum flour (about 240 g)
  • 2 1/2 cups water, plus more as needed
  • 1/2 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt

Steps

  1. Whisk the sorghum flour, water, and yeast into a batter like thin cream. Leave the salt out. There is no wheat in this recipe.
  2. Cover it overnight, until it smells sour and shows bubbles. Give it longer if the room is cold.
  3. Stir in the salt. Add water if the batter thickened past a pour.
  4. Cook rounds in a covered nonstick pan on one side until the top is full of holes and no longer wet. Do not flip.
  5. Serve with foul or eggs. Those are the Yemeni breakfast partners, cooked in their own pans.

Gluten-free only if the sorghum flour is free of wheat. Foul and eggs are separate dishes with their own ingredients.

Refrigerate leftover lahoh and reheat it covered. Cooked foul should be refrigerated promptly and not left on the bread. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some cooks add wheat flour to help the batter hold. That version is not gluten-free.
  • A longer ferment tastes sharper and works well with bland beans.
  • Honey instead of foul makes the same bread a sweet breakfast.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Foul, stewed fava beans
  • Fried eggs
  • Samn, clarified butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
